Japanese Charts: Switch 2 Sales Get A Nice Bump Ahead Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

We are back with another look at the Japanese charts, courtesy of Famitsu, and it has been another big week for Nintendo.

On the software side of things, Nintendo Switch Sports Resort continues to lead the charge, holding onto its pole position from last week with another ~23,000 units to its name. The Switch 2 edition of Minecraft also makes its chart debut this time, grabbing a podium spot at third.

Elsewhere, Final Fantasy Resonance continues to perform well on both Switch 2 and PS5, and Hello Kitty Party Land has made its chart debut in seventh and eighth on Switch 2 and 1 respectively.

The Nintendo big hitter this week comes on the hardware end, where Switch 2 has seen a decent bump from the ~mid-20,000s we usually see to a meaty 68,733 sales. We can only assume that this is thanks to the swanky new Zelda Special Edition model and in anticipation of Ocarina of Time, which, when these numbers were recorded, was only a matter of days away.

The PS5 family once again wins the combined SKU race this week, coming in with a total of 16,235, while the Switch 1 followed behind with 5,853 weekly sales.

Stellar Blade's Free Bayonetta Crossover Outfits Are Available Now on PS5, PC

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Action game royalty has officially arrived in Sony and ShiftUp's fan favourite hack-and-slasher, Stellar Blade.

The sci-fi action title has been updated with a selection of new outfits for Eve, all inspired by SEGA's beloved Bayonetta series. They're freely available to all players.

You can check them all out in this new video:

This collaboration was announced back in September, and the costumes arrive alongside the Switch 2 port of the game.

To recap, the new outfits include Bayonetta, Nun, Jeanne, and Black Tresses; the latter is an original design by ShiftUp, while the others are based on looks and characters from SEGA's series.

It's a neat bonus for Stellar Blade players who, probably, are fans of Bayonetta too.

Interestingly, Stellar Blade has also collaborated with Overwatch recently, appearing in Blizzard's shooter in the form of various character skins you can grab via the in-game store.

The Food Trends We’ll See in 2027, According to the World’s 50 Best Chefs

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

On November 4, Lima, Peru, was a playground for global palates. Star chefs from across the world flocked to the culinary hotspot for the 50 Best Restaurants 2026, a prestigious awards ceremony that crowns the top restaurants around the globe based on anonymous nominations from over a thousand industry experts. Restaurant Asador Etxebarri, which is located in Spain's Basque Country, won top billing as the World’s Best Restaurant 2026 at the event held at the ornate Teatro Municipal de Lima. And before the winners took the stage, we were on the ground as Lima and its top restaurants buzzed with excitement all week.

In addition to knowing which spots came out on top, we also wanted to understand what these results signaled about dining in the year ahead—so we asked chefs for their hot takes. After the plates, tastings, and pisco sours (so many pisco sours), these the top dining trends and places to look for in 2027, according to the world's best chefs.

Read the full list of the 50 Best Restaurants in 2026 here.

Lobster from Kjolle in Lima, which was just named No.7 on the World's 50 Best Restaurants list.

Chef-owner Pía León of Kjolle in Lima—a fine-dining wonder that showcases the megadiversity of the host nation with startlingly beautiful tasting odysseys—snagged the number seven spot this year, making it the top restaurant in the host country. With the global spotlight on her, León looks to the growing importance of knowing specifically where your food comes from, beyond just a vague sense of ‘local.’ “To me, traceability means understanding where each ingredient comes from, who produces it, and the knowledge behind it,” she told Condé Nast Traveler. “Many of the products we work with come from local producers and communities who have kept their techniques alive for many years. As cooks, we have a responsibility to recognize their work and the value of what they grow.”

It’s a feeling that chef and co-owner Josh Niland of Sydney's Saint Peter (which also appeared on our 2025 Hot List of the best new restaurants in the world) emphasizes even further. The restaurant, founded by chef-owner Niland and his wife Julie Niland, focuses on whole-fish cookery and placed at number 50 this year, also earning the title of Best Restaurant in Oceania. “I think it [will be] ridiculous,” he says, if, in 10 years, restaurants are not telling us exactly on paper where things are from. “Like I think it's non-negotiable,” he said. “If you're writing a menu right now, let alone 10 years from now, I want to know why I'm paying $60, $70 for this chicken. Where is it from? That's how I feel about it. You know, we've got QR codes on the menu that you can scan and watch a fisherman pull a fish out of the water. Like, that's probably a very drastic leap, but I feel like that's where, to a degree, the premium end of dining needs to be. We need to be incredibly transparent about our labor, how we're paying people, like where things are from, all that.”

Saint Peter in Sydney came in at No. 50 on the list, and was named the Best Restaurant in Oceania.

At New Nordic gem Kadeau in Copenhagen—number eight on the list and also recently awarded three Michelin stars this past June—head chef and co-owner Nicolai Nørregaard plays with time, drawing on both seasonal, hyper-local fresh produce, and preserved beauties from seasons past. Kadeau—where the team includes chef Francisco “Pancho” Cardenas supervising the kitchen, and Kyumin Hahn serving as partner—also won the Highest Climber award for jumping from number 41 in 2025 to its placement this year.

La Colombe in Cape Town debuted a Secret Cellar in 2024, an intimate subterranean enclave where guests hand over their phones before the meal begins. While chef James Gaag understands that some people want to take photos of their food—and certainly that Instagram traffic helps restaurants—he hopes for, but isn't counting on, more phone-free restaurant experiences.

So what, exactly, does “seasonality” mean when the seasons seem so different lately? Condé Nast Traveler asked Nørregaard just how climate change is impacting the menu, and what adjustments the team is making: “Some meals are shit,” Nørregaard said, “or some ingredients that we are relying on [are], so we need to change plans. But then maybe something else is in abundance, and then we just switch to that direction.” During Kadeau’s growing season, when it relies on produce from its garden on the Danish island of Bornholm and from what can be foraged nearby, adding more nutrients and water to the soil allows them to step in and give Mother Nature a helping hand, ensuring a better product. During the preservation season, in the colder months, the team showcases products like preserved onion that have been stored since spring and summer—but even that process is changing. Take the pine cone: A dry year might produce very few of the beauties, while a wet year will see abundance. “We sometimes need to think ahead and plan two years in advance,” he continued. “So we have to preserve something for more than one season because it might not come the next season.”

Around the globe, more nimble menus—shifting ingredients, planning well ahead of time—are on the way to account for increasingly unpredictable weather. This is a further expression of adaptations already underway, including a reduced focus on meat, due to its environmental impacts, and broadened waste-reduction efforts. For diners, this means that no two tasting menus, even at the same restaurant, may be exactly the same; that talked-about dish may be swapped out for something else when the seasons or weather requires. But this commitment to the best, and the possibility for newness on menus, presents an arguably more interesting fine dining experience. Each menu, and dish, may represent a singular moment in time.

The Sardine Pie at New Nordic gem Kadeau in Copenhagen, which placed number eight on the list.

Kadeau was also recently awarded three Michelin stars this past June.

Sprawling menus as an indicator of abundance; dozens of items on a single plate—foams, sauces, sprigs of herbs placed with tweezers; narrative-driven tasting menus that span six or seven hours. These used to scream luxury. Now? Simplicity speaks louder. It’s a trend noted by executive chef James Gaag of La Colombe in the Constantia Valley near Cape Town, owned by chef-proprietor Scot Kirton. La Colombe landed at number 35 and also took home the title of Best Restaurant in Africa.

La Colombe in South Africa placed No. 35 on the list.

At La Colombe and elsewhere, Gaag is energized by “less courses, fresher courses, lighter courses, less time at the table.” His innovative menus might be inspired by both his German upbringing and world travels, but that doesn’t mean trying to fit the entire globe in one menu. “We used to put 100 ingredients on each plate, and I think the longer I run the restaurant, the more confident I am in putting less on the plate,” he continued.

Chef Jeremy Chan, who owns Michelin-starred Ikoyi in London with co-founder Iré Hassan-Odukale, continues to rise. Ikoyi landed at number 48 this year, owing to its contemporary global menu that is inspired by both British microseasonality and meticulously sourced global spices. For Chan, though, fine dining doesn’t mean frigid. “Fine dining is about creating a refined experience, rather than a sense of preciousness or formality,” he told Condé Nast Traveler. “For me, it is about the refinement and technique of the craft, alongside the responsibility and precision in what we do to make people feel special.”

Odette, the Singapore restaurant founded by chef Julien Royer, placed at number 15 this year—one of the many high-ranking spots across Asia, including Gaggan in Bangkok (number 2, and also the Best Restaurant in Asia), Wing in Hong Kong (number 5), and the Chairman (number 6), also in Hong Kong. Condé Nast Traveler asked Chef de Cuisine Sheng Xiong—who blends his Asian heritage with French training for Odette’s menus—if there’s a fine dining trend he’d be happy to see vanish. “You mean we’re going to lose our jobs?” He joked. After his laughter subsided, the chef at the Three Michelin-starred restaurant echoed Chan’s sentiments: “I think moving forward, maybe the white-glove service [will go], because in our restaurant we treat everyone coming in like [they are] coming back home as a family member. It can be a bit too casual sometimes, and at the same time, it makes them feel comfortable. Guests are happy. At the start, Singaporeans, the older generation couldn't understand what we are doing, but as time goes by, they actually prefer that to a very stiff kind of environment.”

A Bluefin Tuna Rhubarb dish at London's Ikoyi, which landed at No. 48 on the list this year.

Chef Niko Romito of the lauded, three Michelin-starred Reale—which placed at number 27 this year—is seeing a difference in the way travelers experience his restaurant. Reale, which he co-owns with his sister Cristiana Romito, sits within a 16th-century monastery nestled in a remote medieval town in Abruzzo, Italy. As Niko Romito explained to Condé Nast Traveler, because of Reale’s remote location, tourists would often get there at the tail-end of a food tour of Italy—a tough situation to be in, when perhaps a meal at the end of a trip might feel less memorable after a string of stellar meals. “Today, it’s the exact opposite,” Romito said. “The location—tucked away in the provinces—was once a major limitation. Yet, this very constraint pushed me to create a style of cooking that transcends the distance, making the journey—the hours spent in the car—fully justified. I could say that the region where I live, Abruzzo, is beginning to emerge as a top destination for tourists. A territory rich in nature, parks, and hidden gems.”

At Singapore's Odette, number 15 on the list, Kampot Pepper Crusted Pigeon is served.

Inside Odette, the Singapore restaurant founded by chef Julien Roye

Many restaurants within this year’s list support the growing trend that rurally set tasting menus are sometimes more interesting than their big-city counterparts, luring an increasing number of diners. This year’s number one restaurant and also the Best Restaurant in Europe is Asador Etxebarri, which calls Atxondo, a village of less than 1,500 inhabitants in Spain’s Basque Country, home. Vyn, the number 23 restaurant, is located within a pastoral fishing village of about 7,000 people in southern Sweden. While Naar, number 50 and also the winner for this year’s Sustainable Restaurant Award, sees diners traveling deep into the foothills of the Himalayas for its open-flame-cooked dishes. As food tourism continues to boom, expect even more meteoric restaurants to shine from beyond bustling metropolitan areas.

Read the full list of the 50 Best Restaurants 2026 here.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Nintendo Switch 2 sales dropped by 41% from last year due to console's price increase

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo is going to need more games to convince gamers to buy the Switch 2.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nintendo Switch 2 sales have dropped 41% in the second fiscal quarter compared to the same quarter last year, as reported by CNBC and as predicted by Kantan Games CEO Serkan Toto at the beginning of June, selling only 2.67 million units from July to September. Nintendo is still sticking to its forecast of 16.5 million units sold by the end of the fiscal quarter, but is under pressure from investors to release more big hits to sell more Switch 2 units to hit its goal.

In Japan’s second fiscal quarter running from July 1 to September 30, Nintendo increased the price for the Switch 2 in the US, Canada, and Japan after an initial price hike in Japan earlier this year. Serkan Toto, CEO of Kantan Games Inc., which tracks and analyzes the Japanese game industry, expected that the price increase would lead to a decrease in sales. Despite the prediction coming true, Nintendo is assuring its investors that it would hit its forecast of 16.5 million units sold by March 31, 2027, the end of Japan’s current fiscal year.

Toto also stated, “Nintendo’s only real blockbuster this fiscal year is the Ocarina of Time remake. It’s not enough to make investors happy, especially after the device is now as expensive as it is.” The remake, set to release tomorrow, disappointed many fans, with its visuals being uncanny and overly photorealistic, instead of being true to the original art style with updated audiovisuals. The game also seems to have limited offerings to expand on the original Ocarina of Time, even missing the more difficult Master Quest mode, which many veterans were looking forward to. Overall, the remake may not generate as much excitement as Nintendo expects, with fans viewing it as more of a reskin than a release worthy of the hype. This could potentially dampen the Switch 2 sales Nintendo was hoping the remake would drive, but we will have to see how well it performs after its release.

It also doesn’t help that Macronix, one of Nintendo’s ROM chip manufacturers, recently stopped ROM chip manufacturing to focus its capacity on high-demand NOR and NAND flash chips for AI data centers, even banking on the memory price hikes for short-term gain. Currently, Nintendo is pressured to release more big games to make the Switch 2 more attractive, with new Pokémon games planned for 2027 — Who knows, maybe Nintendo might cave in and finally accept Palworld onto its platform.

Just One Drink Can Make Your Next Workout Feel Harder. Experts Outline a Simple Strategy to Help You Plan Ahead.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

How to protect your sleep, hydration, and recovery before a demanding effort.

When you have a big workout or long run coming up, you probably think through your usual checklist, like your fueling and hydration plan, what you’re going to wear, and the route you’ll take. But if you have a drink in the days leading up to that effort, it’s worth considering how it might disrupt your preparation, too.

Alcohol can affect your sleep and your hydration—two things your body relies on to recover from training and prepare for the miles ahead. And while the severity of its effects varies from person to person, even one drink can negatively affect an upcoming workout.

Now, this doesn’t mean you have to give up every happy hour or celebration during a training cycle. But if you want to feel your best for every workout on your plan, it helps to think ahead.

Sports dietitian Pam Nisevich Bede, RD, CSSD, previously told Runner’s World she recommends avoiding alcohol for at least two days—ideally three—before a key workout, long run, or race. Without that buffer, you may find yourself feeling sluggish and under-recovered when it’s time to tackle your important effort, even if you prepared well in every other way.

Even if you think a drink might help you fall asleep faster, Bede explains alcohol actually disrupts your sleep quality. This is a concern for runners because sleeping well gives your body valuable time to recover from the training you’ve already done so you’re prepared for what’s next. According to research, just one drink within an hour of bedtime could reduce rapid eye movement (or REM) sleep, which supports functions including memory and focus. If your sleep is disrupted, you may wake up feeling tired and less ready to take on the workout you planned.

Alcohol will also affect your hydration status before a run as it increases urine production, explains Ron Maughan, PhD, an honorary professor at the University of St. Andrews whose research includes alcohol’s effects on athletic performance and recovery.

Going into a long run or workout dehydrated can make it harder to perform your best, and therefore lessen the effectiveness of a workout.

These effects of alcohol don’t look the same for everyone, though. Maughan previously told Runner’s World that a small amount may have little effect on one person and a much bigger effect on another, so giving yourself more of a buffer is always a safer bet.

Bede’s recommendation is a practical way to make sure you fully recover before a key effort. Not every runner will feel fully recovered after a set number of hours post-drink, but adhering to the two-day rule can help you avoid stacking a night of drinking too close to a run you want to prioritize.

If you’re training for an upcoming race, you’ve probably already got a calendar with all of your workouts planned out day by day. You can use that same calendar to make decisions about drinking.

Start by identifying the efforts that matter most in the coming week. Maybe it’s a long run, an advanced tempo workout you’ve been building toward, or the race you’ve had circled on your calendar for months. Then work backward, aiming for at least two alcohol-free days before that effort—and three if you can.

The idea is to make the choice before you’re staring at the menu and trying to calculate whether a drink will affect tomorrow or the next day’s workout. And if you’re in a particularly heavy training block and hoping to perform at your absolute peak, Bede suggests considering a longer break from alcohol altogether.

If you do choose to have an adult beverage, Bede also recommends taking in additional fluids and electrolytes alongside the alcohol. Proper hydration won’t cancel out alcohol’s effects, but it will help support your recovery. And when an important run is approaching, that two-day buffer after a drink gives you one more way to show up ready to perform your best.

Matt Rudisill is an Associate Service Editor who has been with Runner's World since 2025. A Nittany Lion through-and-through, Matt graduated from Penn State in 2022 with a degree in journalism and worked in communications for the university's athletic department for three years as the main contact and photographer for its nationally-ranked cross country and track & field teams. Matt was also heavily involved in communications efforts for Penn State football, men's basketball, and women's gymnastics. In his role with Runner's World, Matt has interviewed Olympians, world champions, and countless experts in the field to create service content that helps runners of all ages and experience levels train smarter and race faster. When he’s not out jogging, Matt can be found tweeting bad takes about the Phillies or watching movies.

Sebastian Hoeneß discusses contrasting fortunes of Niclas Füllkrug and Deniz Undav ahead of Stuttgart-Bremen clash

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

VfB Stuttgart head coach Sebastian Hoeneß has offered his assessment of the contrasting fortunes of German strikers Deniz Undav and Niclas Füllkrug ahead of Saturday’s Bundesliga encounter with Werder Bremen. While Füllkrug has celebrated a return to the national team following an extraordinary start to the season, Stuttgart captain Undav continues to await his first Bundesliga goal of the 2026/27 campaign.

The two forwards enjoyed considerably different fortunes when Jürgen Klopp announced his 24-man Germany squad for the November international window on Thursday. Füllkrug, 33, earned his first call-up since September 2025 after scoring nine Bundesliga goals in eight appearances for Bremen. Undav, meanwhile, was once again omitted despite having scored 19 league goals for Stuttgart last season.

The 30-year-old has endured a difficult opening to the current campaign, struggling to reproduce the goalscoring form that established him among Germany’s leading domestic attackers. His performances have nevertheless retained the confidence of his head coach, who believes the striker’s contributions are being overlooked amid his lack of goals.

Speaking at Thursday’s pre-match press conference, Hoeneß acknowledged that Undav’s principal problem remains his failure to find the back of the net.

“What isn’t there are the goals,” Hoeneß said at the press conference. “If you look at the match in Istanbul, he could easily have had another two or three assists. I think he’s generally getting back into his rhythm.”

The VfB coach emphasized that Undav’s difficulties were representative of the team’s wider problems in the final third. Stuttgart created several promising opportunities against Galatasaray but ultimately failed to capitalize, suffering another disappointing defeat despite periods of encouraging attacking play.

“It’s the same with him as it is with us generally,” Hoeneß explained. “That final bit is missing. In his case, it’s the chances that other players aren’t converting. That’s obviously an issue for us.”

Undav’s underlying performances have nevertheless raised questions beyond his lack of goals. According to Opta statistics, the Stuttgart striker has won just 20 percent of his Bundesliga duels this season. Hoeneß, however, remains convinced that the 30-year-old is moving in the right direction.

Füllkrug arrives in Stuttgart enjoying a markedly different period in his career. The veteran has rediscovered his goalscoring touch since returning to Werder Bremen following difficult spells with West Ham United and AC Milan. His nine Bundesliga goals have helped propel Daniel Thioune’s side into fifth place, earning the striker another opportunity to represent Germany.

“It’s a very positive occurrence for the Bundesliga,” Hoeneß said of Füllkrug’s recall at his Thursday pre-match press conference. “He’s had a rough time. We always knew that he could do it. He’s demonstrating once again that he’s strong with both feet and his head.”

The Stuttgart trainer also praised Bremen’s collective development, describing the visitors as the team of the moment.

Bremen have collected 15 points from their opening eight Bundesliga fixtures, losing just once. Their recent 2-0 victory over Hoffenheim further demonstrated the consistency that has made them one of the early surprises of the campaign.

Stuttgart, meanwhile, have collected nine points and remain some distance behind their Saturday opponents. Hoeneß knows that his side will require an improved performance to overcome a Bremen team currently enjoying far greater consistency.

The meeting also offers an intriguing personal contrast between two experienced German internationals.

NRCC Chairman Richard Hudson loses reelection in North Carolina

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

North Carolina Rep. Richard Hudson, the chairman of the National Republican Congressional Committee, has lost his race for reelection, a stinging defeat for the man charged with preserving the House majority for the GOP.

Hudson fell to Democrat Richard Ojeda, a former West Virginia state senator who was leading  51 percent to 49 percent Thursday afternoon  when The Associated Press called the race in North Carolina’s 9th District.

Hudson had already conceded the race Wednesday, when it became clear that his path to victory was closing off.

“[The] voters of North Carolina’s 9th District made their choice and I respect it,” he said in a statement. “Since 2013 I’ve worked for North Carolina families. That includes the soldiers and families at Fort Bragg, who sacrifice more for this country than most people will ever know.”

Hudson is now the second party campaign chief to lose his seat during a midterm election this decade. In 2022, New York Rep. Sean Patrick Maloney lost reelection while serving as chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee.

Republicans across North Carolina experienced devastating losses on election night. As of Thursday afternoon, Democrats had also flipped the seats of Reps. Addison McDowell, Brad Knott and Tim Moore, as well as the open seat of retiring Rep. Chuck Edwards. The races for five other GOP-held seats remain uncalled, while Democratic Rep. Don Davis was reelected despite being targeted for defeat under a new GOP-drawn congressional map. Republicans also lost control of the North Carolina House and lost seats in the state Senate.

North Carolina’s 9th District, which includes the Eastern Piedmont region and the Fayetteville area, was far from the House battlefield for much of the midterm cycle. President Donald Trump carried the seat by 16 points in 2024, according to calculations by Inside Elections with Nathan L. Gonzales, and Hudson won a seventh term the same year by nearly 19 points. Hudson also held a financial advantage over Ojeda, raising $4.2 million to Ojeda’s $2.9 million through mid-October.

But in the waning weeks of the campaign, signs of Republican concern grew and Hudson’s campaign began spending on ads.

Ojeda was first elected to the West Virginia Senate in 2016. He ran for the U.S. House two years later as a Trump-voting Democrat but lost that race to Republican Carol Miller. He then briefly ran for the 2020 Democratic presidential nomination and also challenged GOP Sen. Shelley Moore Capito later that cycle but lost in the Democratic primary.

Ojeda moved to North Carolina in 2020, he told CityView, a local magazine, and he announced his challenge to Hudson last year.

Throughout the campaign, he attacked the incumbent over his support for Trump’s agenda, particularly the war in Iran. Ojeda is a retired Army major and the district is home to Fort Bragg, the nation’s largest Army base by population, where the Democrat was previously stationed for more than a decade.

“We did the impossible,’’ Ojeda told supporters Tuesday night. “We took on the chair of the National Republican Congressional Committee, a man with political connections, the billionaire donors, and the entire Washington establishment standing beside him.”

Hudson, 55, is finishing his seventh term in the House and his second term as NRCC chair. He had no primary opponent this year and had grown used to winning reelection by comfortable margins.

As a legislator, Hudson focused much of his policy agenda on health issues such as federal pandemic preparedness programs. He serves on the House Energy and Commerce Committee and chairs the panel’s Communications and Technology Subcommittee.

Why Liquid Death’s media chief is determined to break away from LiveRamp

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Liquid Death’s chief media officer could do without another branded hoodie this holiday season. A credible alternative to LiveRamp would go down far better.

Benoit Vatere has been looking for one since early August, when the beverage brand stopped the identity graph, after about 18 months of usage. The decision came weeks after Publicis Groupe’s plan to acquire LiveRamp was made public. In an interview with Digiday, Vatere said it was a simple choice: “I don’t want to train or have them see that super valuable information [for] anybody that I’m trying to get market share from.”

That fear stems from what LiveRamp learned about Liquid Death’s customers. The company’s job was to recognize the same people as they moved between screens and sites, so someone who saw an ad on a smart TV could be found again on a phone or a retailer’s site.

Over time that showed the brand which ads got people buying. Names and email addresses that get swept up in that tracking don’t concern Vatere since Publicis won’t see personal information. He’s far more concerned about the buying patterns. Publicis, after all, also works for brands Liquid Death competes with, like Evian and PepsiCo.

Vatere worries his patterns could end up in the data the holdco uses to train its AI tools and subsequently help make strategic calls for those rivals. Granted, the deal’s terms say that can’t happen. But Vatere expects them to change — terms and conditions get rewritten all the time, after all — not to mention the FTC’s watch over the deal lasts only five years.

“Let’s be clear about why Publicis bought LiveRamp,” continued Vatere. “They did it because there is value right? And the value is not just the revenue.”

That’s an indictment of every holdco with designs on an identity graph as it is of Publicis specifically. Vatere won’t hand his data to any company that also advises his competitors, which rules out the likes of Havas (still a LiveRamp user), WPP or Omnicom (which is building its own alternative).

In fact, Omnicom’s Acxiom has already tried its luck. Vatere sent it packing on the same grounds. “I’m not mad at Publicis. I just don’t want any of the holdcos to own it.”

That leaves independent providers, and Vatere isn’t naive about them either. Most are built with an acquisition and an exit in mind, so whichever one he picks will have to sign a contract that ends the relationship and deletes Liquid Death’s data the moment control changes hands.

“There should be a clause [that] if there’s change of ownership, change of control, contract out, right? Data deleted, like the whole thing, “ added Vatere.

He has a deadline of sorts. The LiveRamp contract runs out at the end of the year, and Liquid Death isn’t big enough to build its own graph. “I cannot do without it right now,” he said.

Chances are he won’t have to. The industry has no shortage of would-be LiveRamp replacements and plenty of them have already found their way to Vatere’s DMs. Earlier this week, the marketer posted his stance on LinkedIn after seeing reports that the holdco wasn’t concerned about competitors leaving now the deal has closed.

The replies read like a who’s who of the opportunists circling the fallout. One of them was LiveRamp’s CEO, said Vatere. He sent him a connection request, which was duly accepted. Nothing has come of it so far. Not much could. The only thing that would win him back is LiveRamp pulling out of the deal — and that ship has sailed.

Vatere is far from the only industry player to have taken this stance. LiveRamp rivals have been fielding calls and pitching for business since the deal was announced in the Spring. Both WPP and Omnicom have stopped working with the solution.

Advertisers have spent years investing heavily in cloud infrastructure, customer data platforms and first-party data infrastructure with the aim of retaining ownership of customer information as they activate media strategies. CMOs worried about maintaining that control may be at the front of the queue to speak to alternative providers.

According to Forrester analyst Nikhil Lai, Vatere’s not alone in his objections.  On the whole, he said clients are less worried about privacy breaches, and more concerned with indirectly aiding competitors working with Publicis Groupe.

“I have no doubt LiveRamp is going to remain privacy safe… but privacy-safe and structurally neutral are no longer synonymous,” said Lai.

And although he noted the privacy and data neutrality guardrails the FTC placed around Publicis’ deal are “very strong,” it won’t stop marketers yearning for control considering bespoke or off-the-shelf alternatives such as Snowflake or Databricks.

“Advertising is an inherently zero-sum game. You need to beat your competitors to grow,” said Ruben Schreurs, CEO at Ebiquity. “Taking a uniform commoditized approach to communications or media is not a good strategy.”

“If your identity graph and your customer intelligence is your most important intellectual property, then you can’t choose infrastructure whose business model creates the appearance of conflicting incentives,” said Lai.

Schreurs argued, however, that working with LiveRamp alongside rivals won’t be the act that jeopardises a brand’s edge. “I do think it’s a little overplayed,” he said. “You can absolutely manage things in a way that if [your] agency also works for competing brands, that doesn’t necessarily have to be a problem. The same is true with LiveRamp.”

Whether any of this has a material impact on the trajectory of the business under Publicis remains uncertain. The likely answer is that Publicis wants LiveRamp’s data and connections for its own AI ambitions, combining LiveRamp’s clean room and data connectivity tech with the identity capabilities of Epsilon and the tech services of Publicis Sapient.

In any case, many of Vatere’s peers may find themselves boxed into to signing up with a Publicis-backed LiveRamp, or one of the rival holdco solutions, due to the market’s contraction in recent years.

“Clients absolutely care about conflict, how conflict is managed, but they have less and less optionality,” noted Schreurs. “There is now a big global three to choose from, so you’re going to work with a company that also works for competing brands. Then it becomes all about how you retain your competitive edge.”

Competitors and non-Publicis businesses are also a sliver of the business, accounting for roughly 5% of LiveRamp’s $800 million annual revenue.

A few defections at the edges are a price the holdco can afford to pay. Whether marketers like Vatere peel away in big enough numbers to change that sum remains an open question.

Now that Publicis has closed its acquisition of LiveRamp, marketers have a decision to make.

Axiom Math wants to keep AI slop out of mathematics

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

It feels like AI is cracking a new math problem every week. Last month, OpenAI released 719 manuscriptsclaiming solutions to some of the field’s hardest problems. But even the computer checks meant to confirm those results can slip. After years of AI slop, it’s fair to wonder whether math is next.

Axiom Math is betting math can avoid that fate, not by slowing down, but by standing on firmer ground. The $1.6 billion startup wants to build a verified foundation that AI agents can rely on to write software and, eventually, do science.

On Wednesday, the company announced it had formally verified the Stacks Project, one of the largest bodies of mathematics ever written by humans. Mathematicians often hold Stacks in rare regard — Columbia mathematician Peter Woit has claimed there’s “nothing else really comparable,” comparing the project to “the famous Bourbaki books,” a series that rebuilt math from its foundations in the 20th century.

Woit’s Columbia colleague Johan de Jong started the Stacks Project in 2005 as an open source reference. Mathematicians have contributed to it ever since, building out results from college-level algebra all the way up to the field’s most advanced ideas across algebra, geometry, and number theory.

The company’s fleet of AI agents translates the library’s roughly 7,654 pages into about 16 million lines of computer-checked code.

Formalizing math is a way of making sure the structure underneath it is sound. Mathematicians rewrite the proof as code in a programming language called Lean, and a program called a proof assistant checks the code line by line. If the proof passes, mathematicians can trust that it’s logically sound.

Engineers use the same idea, called formal verification, to confirm their chip designs work, while researchers in France have used it to catch bugs in software that calculates family benefits.

For a library like the Stacks Project, checking each theorem is only part of the job. The theorems and definitions in Stacks are interconnected, and each one builds on the ones before it. Like a family tree, many results in Stacks trace back to the ones that came before it.

That interconnectedness made Axiom unsure the project would work at first. The team had to keep every connection intact across about 16 million lines of Lean code.

“Things that are big and things that are delicate in life just don’t go together,” Axiom CEO Carina Hong told TechCrunch. “[Stacks] is a collection of mathematical results that is the foundation of mathematics. You want [theorems] to be consistent and not, like, paradoxical. You want the definitions to not fight with each other.”

The 25-year-old CEO dropped out of a Stanford math PhD program and founded the company in March 2025. In March, Axiom raised a $200 million Series A led by Menlo Ventures.

“We were not sure if vibe coding would be able to preserve the beauty of Stacks,” Hong said.

To pull off the project, the team gave its agents a loose structure and let them run in parallel. “With Stacks, we imposed a hierarchy … [but] it was not, like, a complete hierarchy of dictating what every single agent was doing,” said Ashvin Swaminathan, a member of Axiom’s technical staff. “The agents were finding tasks for themselves and identified things that they could work on in parallel.”

Axiom also trained its own models to be good at math, an approach Hong said most other verification startups haven’t taken. At first, the team relied on one of its in-house Lean experts to teach the agents a human sense of taste. Over time, Hong said, the agents have picked up taste on their own.

The result fills one of the biggest gaps in formalized math. “When you look at what has been formalized in mathematics, there’s this huge black hole. And that black hole covers algebraic geometry,” Swaminathan said.

Until now, researchers who wanted to check new work in algebraic geometry with Lean had little to build on, Swaminathan said. “Well, now they do.”

Axiom argues that each verified result becomes a building block, so future proofs can lean on checked math instead of starting from scratch. Terence Tao, the UCLA mathematician and Fields Medal winner, has made the general case more simply: “The more formalized proofs you have, the more formalized proofs you can make.”

Mathematicians aren’t Axiom’s only intended audience. Developers have long struggled to guarantee that their code is free of bugs and security holes. That problem grows as AI agents write more of the world’s code.

Axiom is betting that the software of the future will need to rest on verified foundations, the way Stacks now does.

“We believe that mathematics is the domain where you start building these things and that it’s a transferable skill,” Swaminathan said. “Agents can read our code, understand it, and build new infrastructure on top of it. Our Lean formalization is now the substrate that agents can build upon.”

The way Axiom’s agents built Stacks, working in parallel and picking their own tasks, is how Hong expects much of AI’s future work to happen. AI agents, she argues, will need a well-built, reusable body of knowledge to keep from producing slop.

“You want [agents] to have a reusable library for them to build on and keep pushing the frontier,” Hong said. “If you slop drop in a way that’s bad engineering choices in the beginning, then it’s very hard for them to grow that boundary.”

Hong is confident enough in that approach to issue a dare. “I openly challenge all the coding companies to work on formalization at this scale.”

‘Different level’ – William Gallas backs Arsenal star for Real Madrid transfer

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

William Gallas believes that William Saliba could be tempted to join Real Madrid if the Spanish giants tried to sign the Arsenal defender.

Saliba joined Arsenal in a £27m move from Saint-Etienne in 2019 and has gone on to establish himself as one of the best centre-backs in the league.

The Frenchman was an integral part of Mikel Arteta’s side that won the league title last season, forming a rock-solid partnership with Gabriel Magalhaes at the heart of Arsenal’s defence.

Saliba is under contract at Arsenal until 2030, signing a long-term deal in 2025 after he was heavily linked with Real Madrid.

But Gallas still believes he is destined to play for Madrid, and said his fellow countryman would find it hard to turn down a move to a club of that stature.

‘Are Arsenal at a high enough level now that William Saliba would turn down Real Madrid if they came in for him?’ Gallas told SeatPick.

‘I know a lot of people will be mad about what I’m going to say right now but if we are talking about the history of both clubs, Real Madrid and Arsenal, then Real Madrid is Real Madrid. They are on a different level.’

‘The pressure is bigger there. The atmosphere around the club is different. Every game, the fans expect you to win. Every game, the fans expect you to have a great performance. Every season, the fans expect you to win La Liga or to win the Champions League, so it’s a different level.

‘For me, even if their performances at the moment are not the best, and even if they haven’t won the biggest trophies recently, Madrid is Madrid, and I’m telling you, any player who has the opportunity to go to Madrid, they will try to go there, and you can’t blame them. You couldn’t blame a player like Saliba if that is his decision.

A high-status burial surprised archaeologists when they discovered leprosy in a man's bones from the fifth century.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Archaeologists investigating the genomes of a dozen people buried in an abandoned Roman-era settlement in northwestern Germany have discovered a surprising organism lurking in one man's remains: a rare strain of Mycobacterium leprae, which causes leprosy.

This strain of leprosy has never been seen in this part of Europe before, which hints that the individual had traveled and interacted with people from across the Roman world. The man was buried in a wooden boat surrounded by unusual objects, indicating he was a high-status person, according to a research study.

"The results were particularly striking in the case of one individual," study co-author Ben Krause-Kyora, an archaeologist at the Institute of Clinical Molecular Biology (IKMB) at Kiel University in Germany, said in a Nov. 3 statement. "He could, for example, have been a mercenary in the Roman army who later settled between the Elbe and the Weser [rivers]."

In the study, published online Oct. 15 in the journal iScience, Krause-Kyora and colleagues analyzed the genomes of people buried at the coastal settlement of Fallward. The settlement originated in the first century B.C. but gradually declined and became a cemetery by the fifth century A.D. Among the 200 cremation burials and 60 inhumation burials originally excavated in the 1990s was a distinctive grave made from a wooden boat.

The oak boat was over 13 feet (4 meters) long and sealed with a roof-like structure, the researchers said. It held the skeleton of a man who was about 50 years old when he died some time between A.D. 425 and 450. His grave goods included a small box shaped like a water bird, an ornate chair or throne carved out of alder wood, a bronze basin and a maple stool with a hunting scene and a runic inscription. The maritime artifacts coupled with the man's well-developed upper arm bones suggest he may have worked as a fisherman or boatman just before he died, the researchers wrote.

"The grave goods placed in the tomb aroused particular scientific interest," study co-author Hauke Jöns, an archaeologist at the Lower Saxony Institute for Historical Coastal Research, said in the statement. "The man possessed a late Roman military belt, which suggested that the deceased had had connections across great distances."

Archaeologists found a chair made from the trunk of an alder tree near the man's burial. The chair was likely a symbol of authority or power.

To learn more about this unusual burial and to determine where the man was from, researchers analyzed his DNA.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"Genetic analyses confirm that a man buried at Fallward originated from the Danube region," study first author Daniel Myburgh, an ancient DNA researcher at IKMB, said in the statement. "He was also infected with a very rare leprosy pathogen."

The researchers found the DNA of the bacterium M. leprae in one of the man's teeth and in a foot bone. Because the leprosy DNA was so well preserved, the researchers were able to narrow down the strain that had infected him. This strain of leprosy may have evolved in Egypt around the fourth to second centuries B.C. and had never before been found in Central or Northern Europe, according to the statement.

But the man showed no signs of leprosy lesions in his skeleton, the researchers said. Leprosy first attacks the nerves and skin, but it can eventually eat away at the bones of the skeleton, particularly affecting the nose, jaw, hands and feet.

"Leprosy is a chronic disease," study co-author Almut Nebel, a biologist at IKMB, said in the statement. "Either he was affected by an asymptomatic form of the disease, or the disease had so far only affected soft tissues. He may have travelled long distances whilst carrying the disease."

While the researchers don't know exactly what happened in this man's life, they suggested he might have been a mercenary who served in the Roman army and later retired to northwest Germany, becoming a high-status member of the community who was honored with an alder-wood "throne" in his burial. But he also may have acquired the Roman belt through trade, inheritance or as a war trophy, the researchers wrote.

Either way, the man's skeleton and his grave goods suggest that northwest Germany had long-distance connections in Europe even after the fall of the Roman Empire, according to the statement.

Myburgh, D. A., da Silva, N. A., Romeyer-Dherbey, J. H., Grefen-Peters, S., Jöns, H., Peek, C., Nebel, A., & Krause-Kyora, B. (2026). Paleogenomics reveals leprosy at the late antique burial site of Fallward in northern Germany. iScience, 29(11), 117877. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.isci.2026.117877

Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

Use Your Year-End Review to Reach for a Promotion

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The November issue of IEEE Spectrum is here!

Maintaining a document of your successes throughout the year gives you a starting point

Brian Jenney is the owner of Parsity, a program that helps engineers through hands-on training.

This article is crossposted from IEEE Spectrum’s careers newsletter. Sign up now to get insider tips, expert advice, and practical strategies, written in partnership with tech career development company Parsity and delivered to your inbox for free!

When I landed my first job as a software engineer, I had a simple plan: Work hard and somebody would notice. Eventually a manager would tap me on the shoulder and hand me a promotion (probably with a nice little speech).

That plan stalled my career for about five years.

I switched jobs twice, kept my head down, read a stack of programming books, and sometimes pulled 12-hour days. I was never promoted. At my third job, out of frustration, I started interviewing at other companies and almost immediately got an offer for a senior engineer role.

So I went back to my job and quit. My team was blindsided. I liked my manager and the people I worked with, and they came back with a counteroffer. Then someone from HR asked me, “Why didn’t you just ask us?”

I took the counteroffer and stayed. Three jobs in, I finally got promoted, and all it took was asking (in the most dramatic way possible).

Today, I would take a different approach.

Year-end review discussions are a great time to push for a promotion. But at most companies, raises and promotions are largely settled before the formal review conversation happens. If the year-end review is the first time your manager hears you want a promotion, you’re usually too late.

Now is a great time to start the conversation.

Engineers tend to believe good work speaks for itself. It rarely does. Your manager is juggling a team, a roadmap, and their own boss. They see a fraction of what you do, and the work they don’t see might as well not exist when raises get decided.

Perception is reality, so you have to shape it. That means learning to brag a little.

A “brag document” is a running list of what you accomplished, when, and why it mattered. Add to it every two weeks or once a month, and write each entry so your manager’s manager would understand it.

Engineers tend to write things like “migrated the billing service.” A better entry reads “migrated the billing service, which cut failed payments by 30 percent and unblocked the Q3 pricing launch.” If there’s no number, name who benefited and how.

Include the work people forget to count, too: handling incidents, mentoring new hires, interviewing candidates, and writing the docs everyone else relies on. If you helped your team adopt AI tools this year, write that down. (It counts as leadership, whether or not anyone called it that.)

The document does two jobs. It gives you ammunition when you make your case, and it gives your manager something to ctrl + c then ctrl + v when they argue for you in meetings you’ll never attend.

Bragging builds the case. Asking forces action.

If your goal is a promotion or a raise, you might ask: “What would it take for me to get to [the next level] in the next year?”

A good manager will give you an outline that can turn into a plan. Push for specifics until you have deliverables you can work toward, then send a short recap email so the plan exists somewhere besides your manager’s memory. (Managers change jobs too.)

Sometimes the answer will still be “not this year,” and the reason may have nothing to do with you.

Budgets get frozen, promotion slots get capped, and decisions get made above your manager’s head where even a manager who wants to help can’t.

Don’t read too much into that. But be honest with yourself, too. Most of the time, a no means there’s something you aren’t doing yet. Find out what it is instead of guessing for five years like I did.

Asking costs you one slightly awkward conversation. Sitting back and waiting to be noticed can cost you years.

Don’t try this at home: To better understand the risk AI slop poses to scientific publishing today, IEEE Spectrum contributor Benjamin Skuse put IEEE’s research integrity checks to the test by submitting two entirely AI-generated papers. “Science has a spam problem. And the spam filter is getting clogged up…”

Policy change doesn’t just come down to paid lobbyists—lawmakers want to hear from working experts, too. One engineer shares what he learned from his first two visits to Capitol Hill.

Could community colleges help fill the coming talent shortage in the U.S. semiconductor industry? Alternatives to four-year university degrees are a promising option for the technicians who will be needed to run new fabs.

Parsity helps career changers transition into software by focusing on practical skills, real-world systems, and the kind of experience companies actually look for.Outside Parsity, Brian works a senior software engineer in the SF Bay Area.Brian is also a father of three, and when he’s not working with students or building programs, you might catch him running around Lake Merritt in Oakland, California.

Enriched Environments Rewire the Brain via Shared Synaptic Gene Networks

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A comprehensive meta-analysis of rodent brain studies reveals consistent molecular mechanisms that enable environmental stimulation to enhance neuroplasticity. Published in Molecular Neurobiology, the study was co-led by Federico Miozzo at the Institute for Neurosciences (IN CSIC-UMH) and Bartek Wilczyński at the University of Warsaw.

By harmonizing bulk and single-cell RNA-sequencing datasets across multiple research facilities, the investigators uncovered a conserved transcriptomic signature focused on synaptic communication and immediate-early activation. The findings confirm that the AP-1 transcription factor complex acts as a central genomic driver connecting enriched life experiences to long-term cognitive adaptation, supported by an interactive open-access platform called mEEtaBrain.

Source: CSIC / Miguel Hernández University of Elche / University of Warsaw

Environmental enrichment, providing laboratory animals with enhanced physical exercise, social interaction, and novel cognitive exploration opportunities, has long been recognized as a powerful non-pharmacological stimulus for brain resilience. Enriched environments enhance adult neurogenesis, strengthen synaptic density, and protect against neurodegenerative models of Alzheimer’s and Huntington’s disease.

Despite decades of experimental validation, pinning down the exact molecular transcriptomic cascade driving these cerebral benefits has proved elusive. Individual laboratories employ varied enrichment enclosures, diverse rodent strains, distinct exposure durations, and disparate gene-expression profiling protocols, leading to fragmented and sometimes conflicting single-study findings.

To overcome this experimental heterogeneity, a collaborative research team led by Federico Miozzo, PhD, at the Institute for Neurosciences (IN), a joint center of the Spanish National Research Council (CSIC) and Miguel Hernández University of Elche (UMH), and Bartek Wilczyński, PhD, at the University of Warsaw, executed a large-scale meta-analysis.

Publishing in the journal Molecular Neurobiology, the team harmonized independent transcriptomic datasets to isolate the core molecular signature through which enriched living conditions remodel the mammalian brain.

The researchers initiated a comprehensive systematic review, screening over 300 publications investigating rodent brain responses to environmental enrichment.

To ensure analytical rigor, the team narrowed the cohort to 20 benchmark studies that utilized standardized high-throughput RNA-sequencing (RNA-seq). By reprocessing raw data through uniform computational pipelines and combining outcomes using three independent statistical meta-analytic models, the investigators eliminated single-lab idiosyncrasies to extract biological patterns that hold true across experimental boundaries.

“One of the strengths of meta-analysis is that, although the studies differ from one another, analysing them together increases the number of samples available,” explains Dr. Federico Miozzo, a researcher in the Transcriptional and Epigenetic Mechanisms of Neuronal Plasticity laboratory at the IN CSIC-UMH. “This makes it possible to detect common patterns that might otherwise go unnoticed in individual studies.”

The unified transcriptomic screen highlighted significant enrichment in genes directly responsible for the architecture and function of chemical synapses:

These proteins dimerize to assemble the Activator Protein 1 (AP-1) transcription factor complex. AP-1 operates as an activity-dependent molecular gateway: when an animal engages with complex sensory or social environments, neural activation triggers rapid AP-1 assembly, which subsequently initiates downstream transcription of structural synaptic proteins.

This independent emergence of AP-1 reinforces previous work led by Dr. Ángel Barco at the Institute for Neurosciences, whose laboratory demonstrated in Nature Communications that AP-1 serves as an epigenetic bridge translating early-life sensory experiences into lifelong cognitive gains.

“The fact that the AP-1 transcription factor emerges again in this broader analysis is important because it reinforces and generalises our findings, pointing to these proteins as key regulators of the brain’s molecular response to environmental enrichment,” said Dr. Barco.

To ensure the meta-analytic data serves as an active discovery tool for the international neuroscience community, the researchers designed and deployed mEEtaBrain, a freely accessible online application.

While the investigators caution that rodent genomic profiles cannot be seamlessly generalized to human clinical contexts without validation, defining this conserved transcriptomic backbone provides a concrete molecular roadmap. Understanding how lifestyle factors engage the AP-1 pathway could lead to novel therapeutic strategies aimed at promoting healthy cognitive aging and mitigating neurodegeneration.

What is the core molecular finding of the new environmental enrichment meta-analysis?

The study found that across varied laboratory conditions, environmental enrichment consistently upregulates genes essential for synaptic communication and synaptic plasticity, orchestrated by immediate-early genes forming the AP-1 transcription factor complex.

Why was the AP-1 transcription factor a significant discovery in this dataset?

AP-1, composed of proteins from the Fos and Jun families, acts as a primary molecular bridge that translates electrical neural activity into long-term gene expression. Its recurrence across 20 independent RNA-seq datasets establishes it as a universal driver of experience-dependent brain remodeling.

mEEtaBrain is an open-access web application created by the researchers that allows the global scientific community to explore, query, and re-analyze the meta-analysis data across specific genes, brain regions, and statistical parameters.

Funding: This study is part of the international EPI-3E project (JPND2022-115), funded by the Instituto de Salud Carlos III (AC22-00030) under the European Joint Programme for Neurodegenerative Disease Research (JPND). The research was also supported by the Spanish State Research Agency, the European Regional Development Fund (ERDF), the ”la Caixa” Foundation and the Generalitat Valenciana. The Institute for Neurosciences CSIC-UMH is a Severo Ochoa Centre of Excellence.

Systematic Review and Transcriptomic Meta-analysis of Environmental Enrichment Reveal Core Molecular Programs of Brain Plasticity

Environmental enrichment (EE) paradigms in rodents have long demonstrated that enhanced sensory, cognitive, social, and motor stimulation positively impacts brain function, improving learning, memory, and neuroplasticity. These effects have significant implications for understanding cognitive development and mitigating cognitive decline and brain aging. While numerous transcriptomic studies have explored EE-induced molecular changes, a unified view of the genes and pathways consistently modulated remains lacking. To address this gap, we performed a systematic review and meta-analysis.

We conducted a comprehensive PubMed search for all studies published up to February 2025 that matched all the following inclusion criteria: (1) employed EE paradigms; (2) were conducted on rodents; (3) utilized genome-wide transcriptomic methods; (4) examined brain regions or neuronal populations. The 323 retrieved articles were manually screened for relevance to the study aims and data availability. Datasets from 20 eligible RNA-seq reports were reprocessed using a unified analysis pipeline and subjected to a meta-analysis with three complementary statistical methods.

Despite considerable heterogeneity across studies, our integrative analysis identified consistent gene expression signatures linked to synaptic function, plasticity and their transcriptional regulation. In particular, our findings highlight the upregulation of the activity-dependent transcriptional program, including Fos and Jun family members.

These molecular insights advance our understanding of how EE impacts on neuronal and behavioral outcomes, and may inform therapeutic strategies aimed at replicating or enhancing EE benefits. To promote open science and foster further research, we developed an accessible web application, mEEtaBrain, that enables the neuroscience community to navigate and interrogate our meta-analysis results.

Substantial methodological heterogeneity across source studies increased variability in the meta-analysis outcomes. The use of stressors or disease models, particularly in rat studies, introduced a major confounding factor and limited reliable interspecies comparison. Overall, the studies exhibited a low to moderate risk of bias.

With “Bimbo University,” Nikita Redkar leans into femininity to explain the news

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, ‘OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.’”

The data is in: News creators and influencers are a major source of news for Americans, especially people under 30. This is the latest edition of Creators of Record, an occasional series of interviews with popular creators about how they do their jobs.

For Nikita Redkar, a big part of filming a video is putting together an outfit.

In a July video about youth-led protests in India, the 33-year-old is walking around New York City explaining how movements grow when people with opposing views come together on the things they do agree on. She’s wearing a white tube top and black jeans with sunglasses holding her hair back, along with a matching gold necklace and earrings that look like soda can tabs. You can’t see her shoes, but they clack enough for you to know she’s wearing a heel.

In another about comedian Shane Gillis’s Saturday Night Live monologue in which he called women “whores” for wearing shorts to the gym, she’s seated on the floor of her living room wearing a black silk velvet top with a pink faux-fur collar and sleeve cuffs. In the style of a Get Ready With Me video, she explains how 2000s comedy and pop culture entrenched misogynistic ideas of gender roles while applying her makeup.

A full-time news creator who mostly focuses on American politics and culture through a leftist and feminist lens, Redkar calls herself a “professor at Bimbo University.” The girliness is the point, she told me.

“I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice these parts of yourself to be taken seriously,” Redkar said. “You should be taken seriously no matter what.”

Redkar and I chatted in October about how she does her job, leaning into her femininity to explain the news, and what her relationship with social media is like. Our conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Tameez: Tell me about why this work is important to you. Redkar: I feel like my work has made a lot of these daunting topics a little bit more accessible. I incorporate a lot of humor, which is important because there’s a lot of doom and gloom on social media. It’s a normal reaction to have toward everything that’s going on, and I feel it myself, too. But when I talk to my audience, I intentionally give them hope. I don’t just tell the news, but tell them, OK, if we really locked in and held these corporations and the government accountable and we were intentional in our activism, this is what the world could look like. It’s important to make information accessible because we are going through so much news-wise and not all of it is easy to understand. I enjoy bringing this information I’m naturally interested in to people in a way where everyone [can] get involved. When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

Tameez: Tell me more about that. You have a little bit of an online persona. Redkar: That was the whole reason I created Bimbo University. I wanted to get more women on board with being interested in politics and history in particular, because history is seen as such a male-dominated [field]. It was really important to me to bring, colloquially speaking, the girls and the gays and the theys into the space, because it is fascinating and it provides context to what we’re going through right now. It makes the present a little less daunting when you study historical cycles and realize that people 100 years ago dealt with [similar things to what we’re going through now]. How did they come out of that?

How did they come out of that?

Tameez: How do you define your niche and how did you get there? Redkar: My niche is educational. Bimbo University is a place where anyone can start no matter where they are [in their political education]. My audience [ranges from] people who are just getting into politics to people who maybe were triggered by a specific event in the news to start paying more attention to it. I feel like leftist content creators all operate on a spectrum. You have people who are getting really deep into Marxist theory, for example, and things like geopolitics. I’m at the beginning. I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, “OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.”

Tameez: You’re the first news creator I’ve talked to who does Get Ready With Me videos. You take all of your interests and apply them [to your videos] to be relatable. Redkar: I got into this huge argument with my best friend’s mom, who was a second-wave feminist in the 70s and 80s. She was upset that I called my page Bimbo University. She was like, “You’re dumbing yourself down. Women shouldn’t dumb themselves down. You are just as smart as a man, if not smarter than a man.” That was my lightbulb moment of, oh, your feminism has always been to measure your worth as equal to a man and by the standards of men…working toward a man’s acceptance. In some industries, you’ve got to do that. I don’t blame [people] for thinking like that. But the reason I dial so deep into this hyper-femininity — leaning into these girly things like getting ready with me or walking with my outfit showing — is because I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice those parts of yourself to be taken seriously. You should be taken seriously no matter what.

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: On Mondays, I have a researcher and we go through the topics that people are talking about on social media. We pull up some news sites and see what the big stories are, and we leave some room for breaking news throughout the week.

My week is [split] between videos [on topics] that the general public is talking about, and videos that are just interesting to me. That’s how I stay sane in this business. I’m lucky to have the flexibility of doing that. My researcher combs through news sites and research papers and compiles all that information for me, then I put together a script.

Redkar: It’s definitely my favorite color, but it’s like the chicken and the egg — I don’t know if I liked it before I was doing this.

I pick whatever outfit I feel cute in. In getting ready with me videos, I start with a completely bare face, so at least the outfit has to be giving a little bit.

Tameez: Tell me about the Get Ready with Me videos. How do you decide what’s a GRWM and what’s a walking video?

Redkar: It’s like: What am I comfortable screaming out in the streets versus what do I need to keep at home? Some topics require more seriousness; if it does, I can do the GRWM for that. But if it’s something [less serious], I can be walking my dog or have on some ridiculous outfit.

Redkar: If I get a fact wrong, I’ll do a follow-up video. It’s rare that I’ve gotten a fact wrong because between me and my researcher, that’s two levels of checks before something hits the internet.

For one video, I didn’t factually get it wrong, but I should have included more of the story. It was about the Ugandan dictator Idi Amin and how Indians were expelled from Uganda, which is kind of how we got both Zohran Mamdani and Charli XCX [living in the U.S.], because both of their parents were part of the group that was expelled. I disregarded the racism that local Ugandans faced because of the Indians. I just focused on the expulsion and I framed Indians as the victims.

A lot of people in the comments were like, “Hey, like, you really should have said this other part too.” I just deleted that video, because I didn’t want this to spread and only give half of the story.

Redkar: My favorite video in recent memory was about girls on the first date, how we’re all so vigilant these days about dating men. I had so much fun doing it because I was talking about how life is affected by politics, instead of just politics. I think that video did really well because a lot of people connected to it.

Last year I did a video that I called “How we went from Brat Summer to Hitler Winter” and talked about the hedonism of the 2024 summer and the fascism that followed. I related it to the Roaring 20s transitioning to the Great Depression to the World War. I incorporated a lot of pop culture [references].

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: Constantly. I posted a video about the Cornell professor who invented the term sexual harassment. I thought it was so interesting and timely, but it did not do well at all. The problem with content creation is sometimes you can’t tell if people didn’t like a video or if you’re being shadow banned.

Redkar: I really love Liz Plank. She’s @feministabulous on Instagram. I love her coverage and how she leans into current conversations in pop culture.

I really like Charles McBryde. He’s not talking about just news — he’s very foreign policy-focused as well.

@MissEatingGood on Instagram is a great explainer of what’s going on and the historical context.

Redkar: Just finding ways to work together. I think there is so much benefit in that. I know countless creators who take a journalist’s article and dissect it down. Maybe [the journalists] should be the ones doing that.

I would happily pair up with journalists and teach them the ins and outs of content creation and how to make a short-form video article that’s also attention-grabbing. That’s just the reality; people are on social media, everyone’s attention span is shot, which sucks. We could try to fix that, but at this moment, we’re not fixing it. It’s really helpful to go on social media and talk [about your reporting] because journalists work hard on their articles.

Creators [should] take more time. We rush way too much. It’s not our fault, because these platforms reward you for making content more frequently and punish you if you take a few days off. [Not rushing] sucks, but what can you do? It’s better than putting incorrect, or rushed, or incomplete information out there.

American Housing Corp names leadership for new Austin division

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

American Housing Corporation launched in Austin with a focus on infill urban rowhomes. The company completed a “Demo 2” prototype using a componentized prefab system and is building a 250,000-square-foot Southeast Austin plant to produce up to 1,200 homes a year.  AI Summary

The American Housing Corporation launched an Austin homebuilding division and named three industry veterans to lead the new operation as it ramps up an infill-focused, prefabricated rowhome platform, the company announced this week.

Austin is the launch market for the company’s infill homebuilding strategy, which centers on designing, manufacturing and constructing urban rowhomes. The move comes as builders look for ways to add “missing middle” housing in supply-constrained, high-cost markets without relying solely on traditional greenfield development.

Parker Scott, a former division president at LGI Homes, was appointed general manager, Daniel Green will serve as director of construction operations and Luke Allen will serve as director of land.

Scott brings more than 20 years of homebuilding sales and operational experience to the role. Green joins from senior construction positions at Homebound, ATCO Structures and Perry Homes, where he focused on building operations and execution systems. Allen, who previously held land roles at Lennar and Ashton Woods, will oversee land acquisition, underwriting and pipeline development for the Austin market.

In October 2026, American Housing Corp completed its “Demo 2” prototype using a componentized, prefabricated building system. The company is building a 250,000-square-foot manufacturing plant in Southeast Austin designed to produce up to 1,200 homes annually, according to the announcement.

The factory-scale approach is intended to standardize production, shorten build times and provide more cost certainty, which could appeal to capital providers and municipalities looking for scalable infill housing solutions. For real estate investors and lenders, the combination of an infill land strategy with a controlled-offsite construction environment could change underwriting assumptions around cycle times and construction risk.

Alongside the Austin division launch, the company restructured its real estate operations and parted ways with co-founder Bobby Fijan. The firm credited Fijan with helping establish its early real estate footprint and championing urban, family-sized housing.

The Tripadvisor-Viator experiences business has long had a "Tripadvisor" brand problem. The consolidation of their teams didn't work, and both experiences revenue and profits fell in the third quarter.

Tripadvisor is cutting jobs in its legacy hotel business as part of a new global restructuring, and CEO Matt Goldberg, whose departure the company announced Thursday, acknowledged that combining its Viator and Tripadvisor experiences teams hasn’t worked as expected.

CFO Michael Noonan is also leaving, the company said.

Tripadvisor is cutting jobs in its legacy hotel metasearch business under a global restructuring, and both CEO Matt Goldberg and CFO Michael Noonan are leaving. Goldberg said the decision to combine the Viator and Tripadvisor experiences teams "hasn't proved out as we expected," a judgment backed by the numbers: third-quarter experiences revenue fell 3% and adjusted EBITDA in the segment fell 27%. Total revenue dropped 11% to $436.4 million, and the company posted a net loss of $316.9 million against net income of $48.5 million a year earlier. Goldberg said Viator and B2B2C experiences bookings grew in the low teens while the Tripadvisor channel declined by high single digits, citing SEO headwinds, weakness in the North America to Europe corridor, and execution problems. The restructuring shifts product engineering and data analytics roles into experiences and is expected to deliver $40 million in gross annualized savings. Tripadvisor said it will keep investigating strategic alternatives and will use the $685 million from the sale of TheFork to American Express to pay down debt. Goldberg leaves February 16, 2027; Noonan departs December 31, with Chief Strategy and Operations Officer Kristen Dalton stepping in as interim CFO.
