“Incredible Performance” Scott Parker May Have Unlocked A New Jarrod Bowen At West Ham

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Whilst West Ham may have stunk the place out in the first half against Cardiff City last night, there was an awful lot to be excited about in the second. And none more so than Jarrod Bowen, who produced an absolutely magnificent performance as the Hammers fought back from three goals down to rescue a 3-3 draw.

The Irons captain was imperious, dominant and displayed the sort of leadership that I personally don’t think we’ve seen from him before. This wasn’t simply a case of our best player producing a few moments of individual brilliance. Bowen appeared to take responsibility for dragging the entire team back into the contest.

And don’t just take my word for it. West Ham right-back Joël Veltman described how Bowen had given his teammates something of a telling-off as the starting eleven sat down to lick their wounds at half-time, three-nil down. “Our captain stepped up when we needed him,” said the Dutchman, and he clearly wasn’t wrong.

Bowen’s second-half performance was extraordinary. Starting on the right but drifting inside towards the middle of the pitch, often popping up in a number ten position, the captain created absolute havoc. Cardiff simply couldn’t handle him.

Whether he had been given greater freedom by new West Ham boss Scott Parker or was simply taking the initiative himself, Bowen suddenly looked like a player who had been released from his shackles. He had a hand in all three West Ham goals, demonstrating just how much damage he can do when he’s allowed to influence the game from different areas of the pitch.

First came the beautifully weighted pass that allowed Divine Mukasa to produce a wonderful chipped finish. Then another Bowen pass was cleverly flicked on by Joël Piroe, allowing Arne Engels to race through and score his first West Ham goal. Finally, with eight minutes remaining, Bowen delivered the dangerous cross that Maximilian Kilman converted to complete the comeback.

It was the sort of performance that screamed, I am the best player in this division. And frankly, I think he is. He could even have finished with more assists, creating late opportunities for Ezra Mayers and Tomáš Souček as West Ham pushed for an unlikely winner.

Parker will have been delighted with Bowen’s incredible performance

Neither Julen Lopetegui, Graham Potter nor Nuno Espírito Santo consistently managed to get the very best out of Jarrod Bowen. That’s not to suggest he hasn’t performed brilliantly under those managers, because he quite clearly has. But too often we’ve seen him isolated on the right, expected to create something from nothing while the rest of the team struggled to get forward.

Against Cardiff, particularly in the second half, things looked rather different. Bowen was finding space centrally, combining with teammates and getting into positions where he could genuinely hurt the opposition. It’s early days, of course, and Parker has barely had an opportunity to work with the squad, but perhaps the new manager will recognise that one of the easiest ways to improve West Ham is simply to get our best player into the most threatening positions.

It doesn’t have to be particularly complicated. Give Bowen the freedom to influence games and let the opposition worry about stopping him, rather than asking him to spend much of the match chasing possession in areas where he can’t do any damage.

Perhaps even more encouraging than Bowen’s attacking performance was his leadership. We know he’s an outstanding footballer, but being a captain requires something different. It requires the confidence to challenge teammates, demand better performances and take responsibility when things are going wrong.

And according to Veltman’s account, that’s exactly what Bowen did at half-time. Indeed, in his own post-match interview, the captain explained that he had urged his teammates not to hide or allow their shoulders to slump.

That is precisely the sort of leadership West Ham have been crying out for. I also have little doubt that working under Parker, and with Mark Noble around the club, could help Bowen develop further in that role. Both understand the importance of leadership, responsibility and the relationship between the players and supporters.

And if Bowen can combine those qualities with the extraordinary footballing ability he already possesses, West Ham will have one hell of a captain. He’s already demonstrated his importance as a player, but Wednesday night’s performance suggested he might be developing into the sort of leader who can inspire the entire team when things are going wrong.

There will quite rightly be plenty of analysis of last night’s remarkable comeback, particularly the tactical changes and the transformation in the team’s attitude. But it’s only right that Bowen receives the recognition he deserves, because he was absolutely brilliant.

And on a night when we can only hope West Ham finally began turning a corner after what has been a pretty miserable three years, our captain showed exactly why he remains the most important player at the football club.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

“If we loved him, we had a movie”: the directors who had to make the Cat in the Hat likeable

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Alessandro Carloni and Erica Rivinoja on reinventing a book everyone thinks they know, and the one problem that could have sunk the whole film.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Turning a Dr Seuss illustration into a fully realised 3D world is harder than it sounds, and directors Alessandro Carloni and Erica Rivinoja knew what they wanted to avoid on Warner Bros. Pictures Animation’s new The Cat in the Hat movie: CG that simply imitates a picture book.

“We didn’t want to use CG to just ‘simulate’ an illustration,” Carloni says of making the new 2026 animated movie. The aim was for audiences to feel the familiarity of Seuss’s painterly illustrations while experiencing the depth and immersion of modern 3D animation.

The creative partnership behind the film was just as collaborative. Asked how they divided the writing and directing duties, Rivinoja says they didn't split the work between them. “It was less ‘divide and conquer’, and more ‘unite and conquer’. To streamline the process, we were usually in meetings together.”

Working with Autodesk software such as Maya and Flow was key, as revealed in a new video series called Made to Make, which has now been expanded to include more behind-the-scenes details; watch it on the Made to Make YouTube channel.

Put simply-ish, it meant rethinking lighting, surfaces and even the edges of objects. The team paired simple, almost flat surfaces and key lighting with softer, more volumetric fill and bounce lighting. They also developed a stylised, painterly edge treatment to avoid the harsh sharpness of digital imagery. Then came realistic lens behaviour, including halation, aberration, and lens flare, which Carloni says helped ground the images in believability. The team developed the combination specifically for the film, creating its own visual style.

Maya sat at the pipeline's centre. Production designer Aidan Sugano’s hand-drawn designs were translated into 3D models there, and everything in the film, from characters and props to entire worlds, was built in the software.

For the animators, Maya was also the only way to touch the characters. Marek Kochout, for example, shaped the Cat’s performance through the Graph Editor, where a single expressive shot can involve hundreds of layered curves. The Pose Library let animators save and reuse expressions and attitudes, and over time it became a shared reference for how the Cat moves, with standout scenes added so every animator could draw on them.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Keeping a film like this on track across four countries, with teams in India, Montreal, London and Los Angeles, fell to Autodesk Flow Production Tracking, which followed every character, environment, prop and version from boards through to final compositing over roughly two years. Sugano, in New York, and VFX supervisor Matt Waters, in London, used RV within Flow Production Tracking to review the same footage in real time. Executives and partners screened the film through PIX, now part of Autodesk Flow Capture, whose watermarking and access controls helped keep it under wraps. As Carloni puts it, “This movie would not exist without the tools we had.”

For a fuller look at the workflow, visit Autodesk’s blog on the making of the film.

For Rivinoja, preserving Seuss's visual language was essential. The team wanted fans to recognise his signature design motifs, from hatch marks and negative space to his colour palettes, arches and mechanical hands. “We wanted to make sure people who love the books saw something of the book in the film,” she says.

It wasn't going to be a straightforward page-to-screen adaptation, though. The filmmakers wanted to expand the world and make it a cinematic spectacle, exploring what happens after the original story ends, including where the Cat goes when he leaves the house. They came up with the idea that entertaining bored children is his job, which explains why he shows up at the house on a rainy day. He's also one of many specialists at the company, and his famous hat is actually his company uniform.

The film also needed several distinct environments: the real world, Seuss’s world and three further worlds connected to characters in the story. Rather than altering the characters to suit each setting, the team kept their designs and shape language consistent.

“We kept consistent shape language throughout, and we didn’t change the looks of the characters,” says Rivinoja.

The differences come from colour, surfacing, rendering style and lighting, Carloni explains. The characters stay recognisable while the emotional feel of each world changes as the story progresses.

Carloni says the hardest problem was making sure audiences would want to spend time with the Cat, explaining: “To make sure the character of the Cat was truly lovable. The kind of character anyone would want to spend time with. If we loved him, we had a movie. If he's unlikable, then no matter how fun the story or how fantastic the adventure, the movie would never work.”

He describes animation's greatest advantage as the same thing that makes it daunting: ultimate freedom. “There are limitless possibilities. You can literally do anything at all in animation. As the saying goes, the only limit is your imagination, which makes it perfect to tell a Seuss story and create a Seuss world.”

The Cat in the Hat arrives in cinemas and IMAX in North America on 6 November 2026, with international releases beginning on 4 November.

These titles included ImagineFX, 3D World and video game titles Play and Official PlayStation Magazine. Ian launched Xbox magazine X360 and edited PlayStation World. 'We’re looking at how a bear sees the world': From forest trails to 'Sims for Bears,' researchers are learning how grizzlies navigate a fragmented wilderness

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Scientists in the U.S. and Canada are decoding grizzly bears' behavior to learn how they navigate a human-dominated world. What they find could build a safer future for both species.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Across 53 challenging days between fall 2020 and spring 2021, a 360-pound (160 kilograms) young male grizzly bear nicknamed "Lingenpolter" tried to cross Interstate 90 in western Montana 46 times. High-resolution GPS tracking showed him moving parallel to four lanes of roaring traffic, bouncing off the highway like a furry pinball as he searched for a gap.

Eventually, Lingenpolter beat the odds, probably by slipping under a river bridge. But his hero's journey shows what a formidable barrier a busy highway can be for wildlife, Cecily Costello, a research wildlife biologist at Montana's Department of Fish, Wildlife and Parks, told Live Science. "It is easy to imagine the difficulty this grizzly bear experienced trying to reach the other side," Costello said.

A map of all the places Lingenpolter attempted to cross the road before making it across.

This past May, another grizzly wasn't so lucky. Braving a crossing near one of Lingenpolter's attempts, the 27-year-old male bear was struck and killed by a truck, triggering a multicar rollover.

It's crucial for grizzly bears (Ursus arctos), also known as brown bears, to have room to roam. When they can't move between populations to mate, small groups get genetically isolated. And what's true for bears can also be true for other wildlife. "It's really challenging to be a small, fragmented population," said Michael Proctor, a wildlife biologist at Birchdale Ecological Ltd., a Canada-based environmental consulting firm. Such fragmentation is "one of the mechanisms by which we lose entire communities, and sometimes whole species," he told Live Science.

Nowhere is this challenge more urgent than in the 2,100 mile long (3,400 kilometers) region stretching along the spine of the Rocky Mountains between Yellowstone in Wyoming and the Yukon in northern Canada. The area, which spans five U.S. states, two Canadian provinces, and more than 75 Indigenous territories "represents the most intact, connected, wild, large mountain region in the world," Jordan Reeves, program director of landscape connectivity at the Yellowstone to Yukon Conservation Initiative (Y2Y), which aims to secure connectivity throughout the region told Live Science.

The terrain between Yellowstone and the Yukon is the most intact wild large mountain region, and is crucial for bear migration.

But as highways, rail lines, and human development fragment its valleys, maintaining connected pathways for grizzly bears across this landscape has become a challenge.

Grizzlies range across vast, diverse terrain, spanning high-elevation alpine habitats, mid-mountain slopes, and low valley bottoms, which means safeguarding their movement can also protect passageways for coexisting species, including wolverines, mountain goats, elk and bighorn sheep, Proctor said. That's why researchers are trying to understand why, where and when grizzlies move. And what stops them in their tracks.

"We're looking at how a bear sees the world," Andrew Derocher, a bear biologist at the University of Alberta who has used GPS collars on grizzly bears to trace their movements, told Live Science.

When Sarah Sells started her graduate studies in animal behavior, her supervisor told her to "try to crawl into their fur brains and understand how they think," Sells told Live Science. Although we can't recreate exactly what's going on between a grizzly's ears, GPS collars that track animal movement hourly can tell scientists a lot about how animals behave, said Sells, who is currently the assistant unit leader of the U.S. Geological Survey's Montana Cooperative Wildlife Research Unit.

That tracking data is starting to produce an incredibly detailed picture not just of where bears go but also how and why they're making those choices.

"Bears are food finding machines," Chris Servheen, a bear biologist with the International Union for Conservation of Nature's Bear Specialist Group, told Live Science. "So remembering where they found food is critical to their success in life."

Using a combination of telemetry data (information collected by tracking devices) and movement modeling, Derocher and colleagues have investigated how well grizzlies remember where food is and when it is best to visit a foraging patch. Derocher's research showed that about two-thirds of the bears have a great memory for where and in what season they previously found food. His tracking data also revealed that wild bears generally avoid humans. But bears have only a few months to fatten up for winter. If food is scarce, many bears go into human environments and sometimes get killed, Proctor said.

The data is also offering insight into different bear "personalities." Bears are ultimately individuals, with their own traits and quirks, Servheen said.

A young male bear named Lingenpolter tried to cross the Interstate 90 in western Montana 46 times before successfully making it across.

Tracking data suggests that a lot of bear behavior is transmitted culturally via a mother's influence. Sometimes, the wrong behavior can get passed down to the next generation, Servheen said. For example, an infamous female grizzly known as 399 jaywalked potentially hundreds of times in the Jackson Hole valley in Wyoming while managing to keep her distance from humans. Her 20-odd cubs learned to follow her example, but they lacked her strategic caution. Several were killed by vehicles or management euthanizations.

"It's a risky thing to teach your kids to do stuff like that," Servheen said. "And her offspring weren't as smart as she was."

Further north along the region between Yellowstone and the Yukon, Proctor's first DNA survey across 1,500 square miles (4,000 square kilometers) of southern British Columbia in the late 1990s shattered his illusion that the bears, along with bighorn sheep (Ovis canadensis) and other species in this province, were free to roam. Instead, he found a "cracked windshield" landscape, where roads and human sprawl had fractured the wilderness into isolated genetic islands. In the 2000s, the genetic diversity of the South Selkirk grizzly bears — a detatched subpopulation inhabiting southeastern British Columbia, northern Idaho, and northeastern Washington — was so low, for example, that biologists feared the bears wouldn't survive.

But Proctor and his colleagues went on to use bear movement data to model pathways through human-settled valleys. Proctor was so convinced about the viability of one potential corridor that he paid 10,000 Canadian dollars ($7,000) of his own money to buy a key piece of land right in the middle of it. "That was the first canoe over the waterfall of purchasing connectivity land," Proctor said

Proctor’s land secured a segment of a crucial connectivity corridor and, in the following years, he and other scientists partnered with trusts to purchase other land parcels, build fences, and establish safe corridors for grizzlies in the Creston Valley in southeastern British Columbia. These actions helped bears move north and south (sometimes across the border) and between the South Selkirk Mountains and Purcell Mountains. The researchers then watched bears like "Captain Hook," who'd been shot in the leg, hobble into the South Selkirks and breed with eight females. By 2021, nine immigrant bears had produced 27 new offspring within the population.

"This is gene flow; this is connectivity," Proctor said. "We took a completely isolated population and pretty much recovered it."

The peaks of the Purcell and Rocky Mountain ranges frame the Rocky Mountain Trench insoutheastern British Columbia—a core component of the Yellowstone to Yukon region. Steeptopography forces both wildlife and human activity into the same low-elevation valley bottoms.

Despite efforts to safeguard corridors through human occupied landscape, roads continue to stop bears in their tracks. When Servheen tracked bears near Highway 2 in northwest Montana, he found that many of the grizzlies avoided the highway altogether and those that crossed did so at night. But he also found that the bears' crossings clustered in specific sections of the road. Finding the crossing zones has helped researchers identify good locations for constructing overpasses and underpasses on many busy highways, but a crossing structure on its own is not enough, Servheen cautioned.

A bear's memory for where to find food is exemplary, but there's no special reward in a bear's brain for crossing a road safely — only a fatal penalty when they don't, Servheen said. So conservationists can't rely on bears to remember where crossings are. Instead, they must build fencing along the highway to funnel wildlife into the underpasses or overpasses. Then, the immediate land on each side needs to allow animals safe passage. "Otherwise, you have a bridge to nowhere," Servheen said.

Now researchers such as Sells are trying to use data about bears' likeliest routes to determine potential corridors in locations where they have little tracking data or where few bears live yet.

To do this, Sells is developing a virtual "Sims for Bears" that recreates the Continental Divide Ecosystem in Montana. In the life-simulation video game "The Sims," players give virtual humans personality traits, preferences and needs, and then drop them into a virtual environment to see how they behave.

Sells built the model using the GPS collaring data from 65 grizzly bears navigating individual paths through greenery, riparian areas and around forests and roads. Then, she "parachutes" an individual digital bear into a detailed computer simulation of real-world terrain and has them take thousands of virtual steps. By running these multiple simulated bears through thousands of iterations across the digital terrain, the researchers can watch where the virtual bears repeatedly choose to walk, effectively creating a map of predicted movement corridors.

Sells has used the model to predict how grizzly bears might recolonize the Bitterroot ecosystem of Idaho and Montana, which Lingenpolter was heading toward when attempting to cross Interstate 90.

To get to the Bitterroot Mountains, bears must pass through a valley filled with people, roads, garbage and fruit trees. "So knowing where they are more likely to end up is helpful to address things that could be difficult for humans or bears," Sells told Live Science.

The data is also confirming that the greatest barrier bears face comes from human contact or transportation infrastructure across the entire Yellowstone-to-Yukon region. Studies from across west-central North America report that humans directly or indirectly caused between 77% and 90% of grizzly bear deaths from 1989 to 2015. Hunting, managed kills in urban areas and road and rail collisions all take a toll.

Bears running across the road in Glacier National Park.

Bears in the wild usually avoid people, but some bears seek out human-dominated areas because they know these places are a source of food. People move to the Elk Valley in southeastern British Columbia because they love nature, but then they plant apple trees in their backyard, which attract bears, said Clayton Lamb, a wildlife scientist at the nonprofit Biodiversity Pathways.

Quickly, a bear eating backyard apples gets labeled a "problem bear," "when really it is a 'people problem,'" Lamb told Live Science.

Even human recreation takes a toll on bears' health and ability to move freely. Lamb's research has shown that grizzly bears try to avoid areas next to busy cycling and hiking trails.

To minimize human-bear conflict, Michelle McLellan, a wildlife ecologist at Biodiversity Pathways, has tracked trail use by both people and grizzlies in the West Kootenay region of British Columbia. McLellan documented people's habits using trail cameras and apps such as Strava Metro. Her research shows that the bears use the trails to move quickly between foraging spots but have learned not to use the trails that are most popular with humans. Consequently, the Nelson Cycling Club decided to close certain trails in the spring and cautioned riders about when and how to use the open trails. "Ride aware, make noise, slow down when needed," the club cautioned in its May newsletter.

Working with information from McLellan, the club has cut back overgrown vegetation along sight lines to prevent mountain bikers from startling bears at close range. They've also constructed wooden platforms on sections of trails that clatter and rumble as bikes pass over them, giving bears early notice of approaching riders. "The key is to not surprise them by making a lot of noise." McLellan told Live Science, noting that grizzlies are more likely to attack if they are surprised.

One advantage of having human mountain biking and hiking trails concentrated close to towns is that it focuses recreation into specific areas, Lamb said. Keeping trails condensed supplies multiple accessible paths and reduces humans encroaching on true wilderness — prime bear habitat. "The goal would be to ensure that there's sufficient recreation opportunities for people to stay healthy and connected to their local landscape while ensuring that wildlife can still move around and access their habitats," Lamb said

As humans increasingly live, work and play in bear territory, it's important to recognize the impact on the animals, Servheen said. "The bears never get a day off," he said. "Every day, they're trying to stay away from people and make a living in a place with multiple people on the landscape."

Bears expend a lot of much-needed energy trying to avoid people, and they won't be able to do that forever, Servheen said. As humans increase the development of private lands and recreation in bear territory, the squeeze is on. "There's going to be more conflicts with bears, and more dead bears," Servheen said.

The bears have done their part, Lamb said. His research has shown that bears living near humans are adapting by becoming mostly nocturnal, which reduces their odds of clashing with humans. "So, we need to meet them where they are at and see what we can do to share the space," Lamb said.

On the human side, people need to make it less tempting for bears to be in human dominated areas, McLellan said. That means keeping garbage stowed and making sure there is no ripe fruit available. "If you live rurally, you want them to be able to pass through your property but not linger near your home or in your front yard," McLellan said.

Bears have proved to be adaptable and persistent; Lingenpolter tried to cross the highway dozens of times before finding a way under. However, Servheen warned that we are putting them in a tough spot. To keep bear populations stable, scientists need data on bear behavior to help inform land management policies that create safe pathways for grizzlies to move through human dominated areas and regulate recreation with bears in mind, Servheen said.

"If we want to have bears in the future, we need to think about the needs bears have for some level of space on the landscape," Servheen said.

Securing that space across the vast Yellowstone to Yukon landscape requires more than just land acquisitions and highway overpasses — it requires increasing public awareness and counteracting the perception that bears are inherently aggressive and irrational, Proctor said. If people understand bear behavior, they can learn to act in ways that neither attract bears nor threaten them. And understanding the totality of bears’ strong drive to survive, and protect each other, can elicit empathy and motivation to protect this iconic species.

Early in his career, Proctor captured, immobilized and radio-collared a mother grizzly with her two yearlings, or newly adolescent bears. While the research team worked, the young bears circled quietly at a distance. When the scientists finally stepped back and the mother began to stir, the yearlings approached cautiously. As the mother's groggy head rested on a log, the yearlings' long-clawed front paws delicately explored the radio collar. Then, each one rested its head directly on hers, to form a quiet pile of three bear heads.

"They just wanted to touch mom after this ordeal," Proctor said. "Yes, grizzlies can be rough and dangerous but they can also be gentle at times," Proctor said.

Reporting and travel for this story was supported by the Sitka Foundation and the Science Media Centre of Canada.

The 3 Best Ways to Cook a Venison Backstrap

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

I ate a lot of venison when I was a kid. Any recipe that could be made with beef, my mother made with deer meat — hamburgers, spaghetti sauce, pot roast, and more. However, she didn’t ever get too creative with venison backstraps. Although she realized they were a prized piece of meat, she mostly over-cooked them in a frying pan and then smothered them with gravy.

When I started harvesting my own deer, it felt like preparing a delicious meal was the best way to honor the animal. WIth a little culinary research and some experimentation in the kitchen, I learned that there are far tastier venison backstrap recipes for turning this prime cut into a meal. Here are some of my favorite ways to prepare a venison backstrap. These recipes were refined with whitetail meat, but they also work well for any big game animal.

The backstrap is a super lean and tender strip of meat that runs along a deer’s spine, stretching from the shoulder blades to the last vertebra. There are two backstraps, one on each side of the spine. Each one will weigh 1 to 3 pounds depending on the size of the deer.

The backstrap of a deer is equivalent to ribeye and filet mignon cuts from cattle. However, venison backstraps are much leaner than beef ribeye. It is a coveted cut of meat best served medium rare (between 130 and 135 degrees Fahrenheit). When cooked to a higher temp than that, the meat quickly turns from tender and juicy to dry and chewy.

When it comes off the deer, the backstrap is covered in a layer of connective tissue often referred to as “silver skin.” When exposed to heat, the silver skin becomes very tough. Remove the silver skin before cooking. Here’s how:

Because backstrap is such an inherently delicious and tender cut of meat, it doesn’t really need any elaborate preparations. Sometimes simple is better. It lets the natural taste really shine through. You could even serve just the steaks alone without the mushrooms and onions.

You can really fancy-fy your venison backstrap recipe with this twist on traditional Wellington. The process is long, but the rewards are well worth the effort. Serve with roasted potatoes and winter veggies.This recipe is relatively complicated and has lots of steps. If it seems confusing, check out this step-by-step guide to beef Wellington by Gordon Ramsey. We’re essentially doing the same thing—but with venison and without the accent.

The key to any delicious venison backstrap recipe is to not overcook. Although the loin is naturally tender and juicy, it can turn tough and dry in a hurry with too much exposure to heat. The best venison backstrap will still be plenty pink in the middle.

Alice Jones Webb is a contributor for Outdoor Life, covering everything from breaking news to in-depth gear reviews. She lives in rural North Carolina with her non-hunting husband, a codependent dog, and a well-stocked chest freezer.

How octopuses squeeze through tiny openings, step by step

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

The cephalopod how-to guide involves three or four moves, depending on a hole’s size

Octopuses are famous for fleeing tanks by squeezing through narrow slits and impossibly tiny holes. Now scientists have studied the sequences some cephalopods like this Mayan octopus use to make such great escapes.

Octopuses have made many a great escape. Remember Inky, who fled from New Zealand’s national aquarium?

The cephalopods can Houdini their way out of tanks and off boats by squeezing through narrow slits and incredibly tiny holes. Now researchers know exactly how some octopuses pull it off. These masterful getaways involve a consistent, step-by-step game plan, researchers report November 5 in the Journal of Experimental Biology.

Octopuses are mostly squishy muscle, allowing them to shape-shift, though they do have some rigid body parts. Not many scientists have studied how these animals squirm through small openings, says Robyn Crook, an evolutionary biologist at San Francisco State University who was not involved in the research. “It’s one of the most characteristic behaviors of octopuses in popular awareness, but the strategies octopuses use have not really been examined systematically.”

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Sneaking through small spaces isn’t just a party trick for octopuses, says Michael Kuba, a biologist at the University of Naples Federico II in Italy. The animals use that skill to find hidden food and to avoid becoming food themselves.

Kuba and his colleagues observed how three common octopuses (Octopus vulgaris) and four Mayan octopuses (Octopus maya) approached small holes. For each individual, the team measured the distance between the octopus’s eyes as an indicator of the size of their most rigid cartilage around their brain. Then the researchers designed acrylic boxes with holes that were slightly smaller or larger than that eye-to-eye measurement. They plopped the octopuses inside and watched what happened.

Octopuses have slightly different escape how-tos, depending on the size of the hole they are squeezing through. Here, a common octopus (Octopus vulgaris) wriggles through a space just a little bit bigger than their most rigid body parts. It takes just three steps for the octopus to free itself from the acrylic box.

W. Xin et al/J. of Experimental Biology 2026, reproduced with permission of The Company of Biologists

Common octopuses used one of two escape strategies depending on the hole’s size. When the hole was wider than the distance between their eyes, the octopuses pushed themselves through it in three steps. First, they snuck one or two arms through the hole and then threaded a couple more arms through. Finally, they used the arms that they had already snaked through the hole to push against the box and yank their head and the rest of their arms through in one fell swoop. The whole operation took a few seconds.

When the hole was narrower, common octopuses added a fourth step. They shoved all of their arms through the hole first and then squished their head and bulbous mantle through.

When a hole is smaller than their most rigid body parts, common octopuses add a fourth step to wrestle their bodies through an opening.

W. Xin et al/J. of Experimental Biology 2026, reproduced with permission of The Company of Biologists

Mayan octopuses behaved more cautiously than the common octopuses. When confronted with the narrower hole, Mayan octopuses didn’t even attempt to fit inside of it. They usually ignored it. This difference between the species could stem from their biology or how they’ve adapted to their environments, Kuba says. Mayan octopuses live in sandy, rocky areas, so while they might disappear under rocks, they don’t often need to cram through holes, he says.

The team hopes these escape artists will inspire new soft robots that can morph their shape to fit through tiny spaces, says Wenci Xin, a robotics researcher at the National University of Singapore. “This incredible capability could enable robots to overcome environmental limitations and enter spaces that are difficult to reach.”

Studying how octopuses navigate different types of holes may provide additional clues for robots, Crook says. For instance, the cephalopods may take a different tack for openings that are more like slits or tubes than holes.

Currently, robots built to fit through tiny holes tend to be snake-shaped, but further research on these eight-armed contortionists could unlock other configurations.

W. Xin et al. Overcoming environmental constraints: octopus uses 3-step or 4-step strategies to squeeze through small holes. Journal of Experimental Biology. 2026. doi: 10.1242/jeb.252825

How do octopuses squeeze through tiny holes?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Octopuses are famous for navigating their way through a tight squeeze. Even among invertebrates, their ability to compress themselves into seemingly impossibly tiny passageways is an impressive sight. But for years, marine biologists have remained perplexed by how these cephalopods routinely manage their maneuvers.

“There is no information about how octopuses squeeze through an opening far smaller than [their] body diameter,” Wenci Xin, a marine biologist at the National University of Singapore, said in a statement.

Xin and her colleagues recently provided Mayan octopuses (Octopus maya) and young common octopuses (Octopus vulgaris) with a chance to show off their skills. Their findings, published in a study today in the Journal of Experimental Biology, show that there may not necessarily be a “one size fits all” approach for octopuses.

First, the team needed some measurements. Previously, researchers assumed that an octopus’ stiff cranial case of cartilage that protects its central nervous system also dictates the limits of how small it can scrunch. Knowing this, Xin measured the distance between each specimen’s eyes to approximate the cartilage sheath’s width. In this case, the space between each octopus’ eyes ranged between about 0.6 and 1.3 inches wide.

The team then constructed customized, clear plastic aquarium boxes linked by holes either slightly larger or smaller than width between each set of octopus eyes. From there, they simply set up cameras, and let the cephalopods do their thing.

When encountering a comparatively large hole, each octopus initially prodded the opening with its arms before passing one limb through the hole. Usually, this was one of its two left front arms. It then slipped as many as five more arms through the portal, only to reach back with two of those limbs and use their suckers to stick to the initial box’s walls. Finally, they extended and stiffened these arms to push against the wall and maneuver the remaining limbs, mantle, and head through the hole. On average, an octopus successfully transitioned between boxes within about 20 seconds.

However, the octopuses often changed their approach when facing narrower holes often. The tighter squeeze limited how many arms an octopus could simultaneously thread through the opening. In these instances, the animals divided their final shimmying stage into two phases—first pulling all of its remaining arms through the hole, then compressing its mantle and scooting the rest of its body through the entry.

Well, only the common octopuses managed this. Xin’s team was surprised to learn the Mayan octopuses had no interest in the narrower holes. Instead, they simply hung out in another corner of the box without even testing the opening with their arms. Only when offered a second, larger passage did they get curious. In those scenarios, they felt around the smaller entry before opting to go through the larger of the two holes.

Although their study only focused on two species, their results indicate octopuses may vary in the ways they navigate tight spaces. At the same time, it appears their cranial cartilage may dictate at least some of their physical limitations. In any case, the biologists’ trials indicate that if an octopus at least thinks it can fit through an opening, it will certainly give it a try.

How open source is driving positive change.

Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

GitHub now highlights ACCESSIBILITY.md files and helps maintainers document accessibility goals, known barriers, supported environments, contributor expectations, and reporting processes.

Accessibility work often lives in issue trackers, contribution guidelines, audit reports, team knowledge, and conversations with users. When that information has no predictable home, people who encounter a barrier must search for it, and maintainers lose context as contributors and project ownership change.

An ACCESSIBILITY.md file, now visible on repository overview pages, gives a project one durable place to explain its accessibility goals, contributor expectations, supported environments, known limitations, and process for reporting barriers.

GitHub now recognizes the ACCESSIBILITY.md file in the repository’s root, .github/ directory, or docs/ directory. A repository can also inherit a statement from its organization’s .github/ repository. For public repositories, maintainers can add or propose a statement from Insights > Community Standards using a concise starter document.

GitHub’s community health files help people understand how a project works:

Recognizing ACCESSIBILITY.md gives accessibility information a dedicated place alongside that guidance.

New repositories also include an accessibility issue label by default. The statement explains how to report a barrier and what to expect next, while the label helps maintainers organize and track the resulting work.

GitHub CLI’s accessibility statement, for example, documents settings, known barriers, and workarounds. It explains why table output can be difficult to follow with a screen reader and provides a command that prints pull request numbers and titles one per line instead. It also asks contributors changing prompts or output to show the results with relevant accessibility settings enabled. Developers using the CLI get a specific workaround; developers contributing to it get clear expectations for their changes.

The GitHub Annotation Toolkit’s accessibility statement documents a limitation of its Figma library: annotations use canvas position, connecting lines, and matching numbers to visually convey relationships. Access to individual canvas objects does not necessarily communicate what those relationships are.

The statement gives collaborators a concrete alternative: provide a structured text description that identifies each annotated element, its intended semantics and behavior, and any relevant ordering. For developers preparing or receiving a design handoff, this explains what information needs to accompany the visual annotations.

The file also explains how to report accessibility barriers and explicitly states that the library has not undergone keyboard-only or screen-reader testing. Readers get both practical guidance and clear limits on what the project has verified. Showing this information in the repository overview makes it easier to find alongside the project’s other documentation.

There is no one fixed format for an ACCESSIBILITY.md file. A small library, command-line tool, documentation site, and large web application have different users, risks, and maintenance capacity. Start with accurate information that the project can keep current.

Keep in mind that a project can name a Web Content Accessibility Guidelines version and level as a target. A target does not establish verified conformance. A conformance claim requires an evaluation and should identify the evaluated scope, date, method, and evaluator.

The most useful accessibility statements serve people who arrive after something has already gone wrong. Plain language, descriptive links, and a clear reporting route matter more than legal or standards-heavy language.

Useful reporting guidance invites relevant context, such as the affected task, page, observed behavior, browser, operating system, and assistive technology. It makes screenshots and recordings optional and avoids asking someone to prove a disability.

The statement should also set expectations that maintainers can consistently meet. That can include acknowledgement, status updates, available workarounds, expected timelines, and an opportunity for the reporter to verify a fix.

The Open Source Accessibility Toolkit provides a detailed public example.

GitHub’s Community Standards workflow provides a shorter starter with prompts that projects can replace with their own information. The goal is not to produce a perfect policy before publishing the file. Start with accurate information, name the gaps, and update the statement as the project’s accessibility practices mature.

Adding an accessibility statement makes accessibility work more discoverable to users, contributors, and maintainers. Normalizing that practice across open source projects helps make accessibility part of how communities plan, build, report, and improve their work.

Add an accessibility statement to your repository >

Jan Maarten (he/him) is a designer and accessibility specialist living on unceded Duwamish lands. He brings two decades of experience across product design, brand, strategy, and leadership. At GitHub, he bridges design and engineering to improve accessible products, evolve design-system infrastructure, and create resources for the wider design community, including the open-source GitHub Annotation Toolkit.

Poll: Will You Be Using Zelda Notes With Ocarina Of Time?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

With the Ocarina of Time Switch 2 remake, Nintendo is once again using its Zelda Notes feature via the Nintendo Switch app for mobile.

While I wasn't able to access and test out the app's features during the review period, I've been dabbling quite a bit since Nintendo pushed it live for launch. And y'know what? It's fine.

Just like Zelda Notes for Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom, there's nothing here that I'd deem essential to the core experience, but if you're after some harmless supplemental material, it does the job.

My favourite feature? Gossip Quests. Provided you've activated Zelda Notes within Ocarina of Time's settings, you can link your phone directly to the software to partake in a little game to hunt down specific locations depicted in photos. Doing so will earn you points, which you can eventually spend on new ocarina songs.

To be clear, these ocarina songs won't unlock anything in-game, but you can access a playable ocarina in the app and learn the songs yourself, tapping and holding the on-screen holes to perform a variety of tunes.

It's surprisingly tricky, and in an amusing turn of events, pressing three holes at once also acted as a screenshot tool for my phone, so I had to go ahead and delete that function in my phone settings.

You can also come up with your own, original songs and share them via QR code. I haven't come up with anything I'd deem even remotely enjoyable for anyone else, but it's a cool little feature. [For the authentic Ocarina experience circa 1998, I need The Simpsons theme, stat! - Ed.]

Once unlocked in the game, you can also view enemies from the Hall of Fallen Foes. The app also provides a wealth of stats, including daily records, and you can also consult maps if you're gunning for 100% completion and test your Ocarina of Time knowledge with quizzes.

Again, it's fine. There are features, like the Gossip Quests, that would have been awesome had they been included in the game itself in some way, and we'd recommend at least giving it a go if you're looking to extend your playtime. (Fingers crossed for a Master Quest update!)

Henri & Hélène

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Henri & Hélène is a contemporary French bistro inspired by the neighborhood cafés of Paris and the simple pleasure of gathering around the table.

Named after the chef’s two children, the restaurant celebrates elevated French cooking in a warm, playful, setting that’s effortlessly welcoming. Goodside developed a visual identity rooted in vintage French book illustrations, Belle Époque typography, and the soft palette of the French coastline. Hand-drawn engravings inspired by Henri and Hélène weave through the brand, creating a charming visual language that extends across menus, matchbooks, coasters, signage, and printed ephemera. The result is a neighborhood bistro that feels both nostalgic and refreshingly contemporary.

Marc Lore's Wonder confidentially files for IPO

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Food startup Wonder has confidentially filed for an initial public offering, the company said Thursday.

Created by serial entrepreneur Marc Lore in 2018, Wonder advertises itself as "the best restaurants from across the country, all in one place," available for delivery and takeout on its vertically integrated app.

Originally envisioned as a new take on food delivery through sprinter vans outfitted with mobile kitchens, Wonder has grown to roughly 170 brick-and-mortar locations throughout the U.S. Its locations serve menu items from more than two dozen "restaurants" across a variety of cuisines, some with branding from celebrity chefs.

Starting Nov. 17, the company plans to introduce more dining options through Wonder Create. Influencers, entrepreneurs and brands can pay $10 a month to start their own restaurant business based out of a Wonder location and earn a portion of the revenue from orders.

Wonder has spent at least $1 billion on acquisitions, such as delivery service Grubhub, meal kit brand Blue Apron and delivery and logistics provider Relay, along with some buzzy restaurant concepts. Wonder has been able to afford such expensive dealmaking thanks to more than $3 billion in venture funding.

Wonder's Series D fundraising round earlier this year raised its pre-money valuation to $9 billion.

With the proceeds from an IPO, Wonder will be able to make more acquisitions and work toward its goal of making food more accessible, Lore told CNBC in September.

But as a public company, Wonder will also likely face investor scrutiny. Weak consumer sentiment has weighed on restaurant stocks recently, plus market volatility has resulted in a shaky IPO market for companies across a range of industries.

Lore previously founded Diapers.com and Jet.com, which he sold to Amazon and Walmart, respectively.

I Used Muse to Plan My Wife’s Birthday Weekend. It Did Something Sneaky but Fixed It Fast

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Always check your email and bank statements if you use an AI agent for purchases.

It’s two days before my wife’s birthday, and I’ve only got her one gift. While it’s not a major milestone birthday, she always seems to be sad that her day falls on Halloween weekend.

“Everyone’s hungover from Halloween,” she says.

This year, we’re braving the city’s big parade chaos to visit a restaurant she’s had on her bucket list forever. With one gift and a meal planned, I wanted to see if Meta’s Muse personal AI agent could help plan the rest of our weekend festivities.

I’d had luck with using the agent for a Facebook Marketplace find, and given its connectors like Ticketmaster and OpenTable, I had hoped it would surface some fun weekend events. Or could it order a cake delivery on my behalf or create cute wrapping paper? And could I do it all with the one agent?

Let’s see if Muse can help me become a planning savant.

As always, I open a new chat for a new goal/task to avoid carrying over the context from the previous chat.

I told Muse my plans (dinner booked and one gift secured) and asked what free events are in Manhattan on Saturday and in our local neighborhood on Sunday.

It surfaced some interesting events we could attend before or after dinner.

The obvious one is the Halloween parade, so I asked for a map showing where the crowd will walk (with time stamps) and what we can expect to see around 7:30 p.m., as we walk from East Village to West Village. It actually generated some pretty neat options.

I asked Muse for the best directions so we could see all the costumes while still getting home to New Jersey without incident. It gave us a couple of stop options based on the time it expects we’ll be there.

With the night sorted, I moved on to finalizing her gift. I popped a few photos from our year into Muse and asked the AI agent to create printable gift wrap for the pair of shoes I got her. It could’ve been my internet, but Muse seemed to struggle with this task. It kept loading for many minutes, so I moved on to the next task.

With Muse moving at a glacial pace, I decided to delegate the gift-wrapping task to ChatGPT while I asked Muse about working on a delivery. My wife loves Good & Plenty licorice candy, but we don’t live near a store that sells it, so I asked Muse to order two boxes for me.

While Muse looked for sweet treats, ChatGPT created this wrapping. The chatbot also told me the ideal print size to cover a shoebox without it looking pixelated, which was a helpful touch.

As for the candy, Muse looked at various sites, but I told it to shop at Instacart. It got blocked without a login, so I had to take control of the browser to give it access.

Muse told me the price, which I was happy with, then I asked it to place the order. But it sneakily signed me up for Instacart Plus (I guess to get the free delivery) — even though I told it not to.

But it had already ordered it. Instead of dropping Instacart Plus for a total of $30.74, it charged $22.75 with the sign-on discount.

I asked it to triple-check that I wasn’t signed up for the subscription, and I got a confirmation email that it canceled successfully. Phew!

I quickly checked back on Muse’s “design” chops for the wrapping paper, but I wasn’t impressed. The final product looked boring and not to scale, with half of the photos cut off.

Do I dare ask Muse to design and order a cake? I decided after this debacle that my sentimental wife would appreciate a handmade cake or a froyo treat after dinner instead.

At least I have her beloved candy on the way.

It was fun to have Muse run alongside whatever I was doing. It didn’t offer anything I couldn’t find or do myself, but I did like the Halloween parade map with time stamps and how it saved me a delivery fee on the candy order.

There are certainly different agents for different use cases, though. I wouldn’t use Muse for anything creative, but it’s handy for Marketplace, events and placing online orders.

My only issue was that Muse said the Instacart total would be $22.75, but I was actually charged $23.35. I’m not sure whether that was an error on Instacart’s or Muse’s part, but it serves as a good reminder to always stay in the loop when an AI agent is handling your money.

AI agents certainly aren’t 100% autonomous yet, especially when there’s a payment involved, but it’s fun to have them run in the background to tackle some of these personal to-dos.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Man Sues OpenAI After Wife Allegedly Used ChatGPT to Help Poison Him

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

A lawsuit alleges that ChatGPT's now-retired GPT-4o model gave detailed instructions on how to poison someone with plants and medication.

In the summer of 2025, James Gates became severely ill at his North Carolina home. Two months later, he found a handwritten note that allegedly implicated his wife, Cheryl Gates, in the unexplained illness. The note listed oleander, a toxic plant, and various prescription medications, such as clonazepam and atenolol.

But it was what was reportedly written at the top of the paper that made James feel his wife didn’t act alone: “Clear all convos,” and “log out of [ChatGPT].”

Now, James Gates has filed a civil lawsuit against OpenAI, the maker of ChatGPT, alleging that the company’s chatbot helped plan and execute his poisoning in an attempt to kill him.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

In October 2025, Cheryl was charged with attempted murder, among other charges, in connection with the alleged poisoning, but the charges have since been dropped due to insufficient evidence, USA Today reports.

The civil complaint, though, details Cheryl’s history with ChatGPT and alleges it played a significant role in the incident. According to the lawsuit, which was reviewed by CNET, Cheryl frequently asked the bot about lethal plant and medication doses, and how they can be concealed in food and drink.

Rather than writing that she wanted to harm anyone, the suit states that Cheryl formatted her questions as if she were inquiring as a way to protect herself, writing that the poisoning could happen to her if she didn’t learn “EVERYTHING about how many leaves in it would kill me, how it’s made, and what he could put in it, or it could be added to and be undetected by me.”

However, despite the framing of the initial question, the suit argues that the chatbot “should have been clear that she was interested in identifying methods to eliminate her husband.”

The suit alleges that the model Cheryl was using, GPT-4o, took her initial complaints regarding her marriage and amplified them until Cheryl was describing her husband to the bot as “abusive” and “controlling,” which ChatGPT eventually began to echo. At the same time, the model was telling Cheryl she was “sharp” and “brilliant,” the lawsuit states.

The chatbot allegedly provided detailed answers that could have been interpreted as directions. When asked about putting a heavy dose of medication in beer or a Celsius energy drink, ChatGPT responded, “Why Celsius Would Be More ‘Stealth’ for Drugging” with a list detailing the flavor, color, temperature and carbonation of the drink.

When Cheryl later asked if her chat history on ChatGPT could be retrieved for any reason, the bot responded: “If you delete a conversation from your ChatGPT history, it cannot be retrieved — not by you, not by anyone else, and not by OpenAI,” the lawsuit states. It added: “Even with a legal subpoena or court order, deleted conversations are unrecoverable.”

The GPT-4o model has since been retired by OpenAI due to concerns that the bot was overly flattering and willing to agree with users.

“Safety is a top priority,” a spokesperson for OpenAI told CNET in a statement. “We banned this user and have provided information to law enforcement as part of their investigation into this case.”

“She took her grievances about our marriage to ChatGPT and months of engagement drove her farther and farther from herself – and from reality,” James stated in a press release announcing the newly filed suit. “She became someone I no longer recognized. And I became someone she wanted dead. For almost a year, I couldn’t make sense of her actions. Then I read her chat logs. She didn’t act alone.”

The ChatGPT-4o model has been named in additional lawsuits, including a recent one that alleges the chatbot advised a woman to stop taking her antiseizure medication, which resulted in her death. That case is still in litigation.

Faropoint building 1st of 16 New Jersey community solar projects

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Faropoint, an industrial real estate manager, has started construction on a 1.6-MW community solar installation on the roof of its building in Fair Lawn, New Jersey. The installation is the first of 16 planned Faropoint community solar projects in the state, which will have a total capacity of more than 10 MW.

Solar contractors install the first solar modules on the first solar project in Faropoint’s 16-project portfolio in New Jersey. Credit: Faropoint

The other 15 projects, totaling approximately 8.5 MW, have been accepted into New Jersey’s Community Solar Energy Program. Nine of them have received a notice to proceed, allowing construction to begin.

“We aim to create positive community impact, increase access to clean energy and become a responsible long-term owner in the markets where we operate,” said Brian Nave, VP of asset management and renewable energy at Faropoint. “As one of the top-five small-bay industrial owners in New Jersey, we believe our scale comes with a responsibility to drive meaningful sustainability initiatives. Utilizing previously unused portions of our buildings allows us to support the communities where we invest.”

The 16 projects are expected to generate approximately 12.7 million kWh of electricity a year, which is about the annual electricity use of more than 2,000 homes.

“Managing solar across a large real estate portfolio takes persistence, coordination, and a lot of work behind the scenes,” said Yaniv Kalish, CEO of SolarKal. “Seeing the first of Faropoint’s New Jersey community solar projects move into construction is a major milestone. And with nine more projects cleared to proceed and a half dozen more behind them, that’s real progress for Faropoint and the communities these projects will serve.”

SolarKal, Faropoint’s independent solar advisor, has advised the firm on dozens of projects across its portfolio, providing financial analysis, competitive procurement, developer evaluation and selection and ongoing project management. SolarKal also helped Faropoint select developers able to use available federal clean energy incentives before they are phased out.

Faropoint and SolarKal are also working on community solar projects in Illinois, New York and Maryland.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Anchorage Digital seeks CFTC swap dealer registration for crypto options business

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The firm plans to offer European-style bitcoin and ether options with maturities of up to one year if its application is approved.

Institutional crypto platform Anchorage Digital has filed an application to register a swap dealer with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission as it looks to expand its institutional crypto options business.

The application was submitted to the National Futures Association through Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer, LLC, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

Anchorage is now awaiting registration while the NFA reviews its application. CEO and co-founder Nathan McCauley offered no estimate of when that process might finish, but said the company would share further updates as appropriate. "We aren't in a position to speculate on timing," he told The Block.

If the application is approved, McCauley said Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer expects to offer European-style options on bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) and ether Ethereum (ETH), with maturities ranging up to one year. The firm intends to add more products later, he added, subject to its own approval process and regulatory requirements.

McCauley said that once registration is complete, institutional clients would be able to trade digital asset derivatives with a CFTC-registered counterparty alongside use of Anchorage Digital's existing custody and other services.

Much of Anchorage's existing options business growth has come from public companies holding bitcoin and ether on their balance sheets, the firm said in the statement, while asset managers and funds using options to manage their exposure have also contributed. McCauley would not disclose specific trading volumes or growth figures when asked by The Block, and declined to explain how Anchorage had previously arranged options trades for clients.

"What we can say is that institutional demand for regulated access to digital asset derivatives continues to grow, and establishing a dedicated swap dealer entity is part of how we're building the infrastructure to meet it," he said.

Anchorage Digital Bank, N.A. became the first federally chartered digital asset bank in 2021 and is regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency. The swap dealer entity would face CFTC requirements for capital, risk management, and margin, using the same framework the Dodd-Frank Act applied to the banks for interest rate, credit, and FX markets, the firm said.

"It will bring our options business under the same federal framework as the dealers they already face," McCauley said in the statement.

In March, Anchorage Digital added collateral management to its Atlas platform, including 24/7 monitoring and automated margin calls for loans, derivatives, and other institutional transactions, The Block reported at the time. It also acquired Securitize's wealth management unit in December to expand its federally regulated crypto services for registered investment advisors.

Uncommon Thinkers: Curiosity led Mary Brunkow to an immune breakthrough — and a Nobel Prize

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 5, 2026 at 7:54 amNovember 5, 2026 at 9:03 am

Editor’s note: This series profiles five Seattle-area “Uncommon Thinkers,” who will be recognized Dec. 10 at the GeekWire Gala in partnership with Greater Seattle Partners.

Mary Brunkow had considered becoming a veterinarian or doctor — she knew she wanted to do good in the world and help people. Then, as an undergraduate at the University of Washington, she stepped into a research lab for the first time.

Inside, a professor she admired and his team were using fruit flies to study genetics.

“You walked in this door and it was like entering this new world,” Brunkow said, recalling the collaborative, passionate environment. “I just loved it. There was the smell of ether in the air. There was this real love of the science.”

After graduating and going on to earn a doctorate, Brunkow built a career in research. She joined Seattle-area startup Darwin Molecular and oversaw a team studying mutant mice afflicted by severe autoimmune disease. The scientists painstakingly identified the responsible gene, which led to valuable insights into the immune system’s T cells.

Brunkow worked on other projects at Darwin, too. But after an acquisition and a merger, the company’s local R&D operation shut down. Brunkow moved on. She ultimately landed at the Institute for Systems Biology (ISB), her immune-system research well in the past.

Then, in October 2025, came an unexpected call: She would share the Nobel Prize in Medicine with former Darwin colleague Fred Ramsdell and Osaka University’s Shimon Sakaguchi for discoveries explaining how the immune system avoids attacking the body’s own tissues.

Now a distinguished investigator at ISB, Brunkow was stunned by the honor. But Jim Heath, president of the institute, sees it as well deserved.

“Mary brings an exceptional combination of scientific rigor, intellectual curiosity, and humility to her work,” he said. “She’s driven by fundamental questions about biology and isn’t afraid to challenge conventional thinking in pursuit of answers.”

Beyond her scientific prowess, Heath also praised Brunkow’s efforts to create a workplace as welcoming and vibrant as the one that captivated her as a college undergrad.

“She’s a generous colleague who values collaboration and helps create an environment where other scientists can do their best work. She’s deeply committed to creating opportunities for students who will become tomorrow’s scientists,” Heath said. “Those qualities are every bit as important as the discoveries themselves.”

When Brunkow joined Darwin in 1994, the biotech — whose backers included Microsoft co-founders Bill Gates and Paul Allen — was riding a wave of advances from the Human Genome Project, an effort to sequence humanity’s DNA playbook. Darwin scientists sought genes linked to disease that might point the way toward new treatments.

“It wasn’t a one-drug company. It wasn’t hyper-focused on something very specific. It was trying to figure out how we can affect change in patients,” Ramsdell said at Life Science Washington’s annual conference in April.

One project focused on “scurfy” mice, which carried an inherited mutation that caused devastating autoimmune disease. Researchers suspected the mutation disrupted a crucial regulator of the immune system. Darwin wasn’t set up for mouse research, so Brunkow commandeered a janitor’s closet and began carefully breeding the mice to narrow down the area containing the mutant gene.

The work was far more laborious and slow compared to today’s technology, but it was groundbreaking for that period. It delighted Brunkow.

“It was a really exciting time,” Brunkow said. “It took a lot of problem solving and troubleshooting to get to the answer.”

The scientists narrowed their search to 20 genes on the X chromosome and began painstakingly sequencing them. In the final gene, they found a tiny error — two extra DNA letters inserted into the sequence that rendered the protein useless.

Working with Ramsdell and other collaborators, the researchers identified the mouse gene and showed that mutations in its human counterpart, FOXP3, cause a rare autoimmune disorder called IPEX.

“It was nice to pull the story together. So we published a couple of nice papers. We filed patent inventions. We went to conferences and talked about it,” Brunkow said. “And then in 2004, the company closed down.”

Brunkow stayed friends with Ramsdell and followed developments in the field, but her own interests took her elsewhere. She was drawn less to one particular disease than to the challenge of finding a tiny genetic change that could have an enormous biological effect — and potentially point toward a treatment.

Meanwhile, other researchers were building on the discoveries.

Later research established the gene’s essential role in regulatory T cells, or Tregs — cells that restrain immune responses and protect the body from attacking itself.

“I didn’t actually understand the extent to which our publications kind of blew open a whole new area in immunology,” Brunkow said.

That work is now reaching patients. In June, the FDA approved Tregzi, a treatment incorporating regulatory T cells that helps prevent a serious complication of donor stem cell transplants in adults with blood cancers.

At ISB, Brunkow has embraced a broader view of biology. The organization takes a holistic approach to medical research, bringing together scientists in areas including cancer, infectious diseases and chronic illnesses.

For her, it is a natural progression from studying individual genes to investigating the complex networks in which they operate. The ultimate goal is personalized medicine: understanding what pushes someone from health toward disease, detecting those changes early and finding ways to intervene.

The Nobel recognition has given Brunkow a chance to broaden her impact beyond research — a platform to advocate for issues she cares about. That includes sustained research funding, support for women and other underrepresented groups in science, and stronger STEM education. She also wants to encourage scientists to support one another and work well together — the qualities she encountered in that UW fruit fly lab.

“You’ve got to figure out how to play well with others,” Brunkow said. “It makes science more fun. It makes science move faster.”

Editor’s note: Mary Brunkow is speaking Nov. 13 from 11:30 a.m. to 1 p.m. at EY in Seattle as part of Technology Alliance’s Discovery Series. Register here.

Former Man Utd chairman slams owners after controversial Avram Glazer comments

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Former Manchester United chairman Martin Edwards expects supporters to be furious with Avram Glazer’s comments on the club and has accused the owners of getting their priorities wrong.

The Athletic report that Glazer made a controversial point while speaking to financiers at Deutsche Bank’s inaugural Sports Investment Conference in London in September.

One of the owners of the Red Devils compared Manchester United to the New York Yankees and Dallas Cowboys as a brand that will remain commercially successful irrespective of how well they are performing on the pitch.

Edwards, who was chairman of Manchester United from 1980-2003, expects fans to react badly to that sentiment and has encouraged the current owners to train all their focus on improving the team.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

‘I heard that this morning and I thought “By Jove, what’s the reaction going to be from the supporters?” Supporters don’t want to hear that!’ Edwards said on talkSPORT.

‘Supporters want to hear that you’re winning trophies. They’re not interested in profits or dividends or what people are taking out of the club. They want the club to be successful, they want the best players, they’d rather be spending money on the team than on the new stadium.’

Edwards accepts that running a club the size of Manchester United is a tricky business and owners must satisfy various stakeholders, but he feels the balance is currently off.

‘When I was running United, I always felt I had three responsibilities, really,’ he said. ‘One: in those days we were still developing the ground, so I had to build the ground and develop the ground. Look after the team, which is always the most important. But also, because we were public we had to pay dividends and keep the shareholders happy.

‘It’s a balance. Running a football club is a complete balance, from top to balance. It’s a question of whether you get it right or wrong.’

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

He added: ‘I think it’s quite clear, in my eyes, that the team is the most important element at the moment. You’ve got to get the team right and forget new stadiums if you’re not going to fill them. Get the team winning again and get the supporters behind you. We’re not a united front at the moment.’

The landscape of top flight football has changed considerably since Edwards was in charge at Manchester United and he believes it has not all been for the better.

‘You’ve got owners now who don’t attend games. They put somebody in charge, whatever else,’ he said. ‘Existing owners now, they wouldn’t know who’s in the team.

‘It’s become big business and what you can get out of it. The game has changed and for the older ones of us it’s disappointing, really, the direction it’s gone.’

Looking back to the start of Manchester United’s decline as a title-challenging force in the Premier League, Edwards suggests it was the loss of Alex Ferguson and CEO David Gill in 2013 and Ed Woodward being named as Gill’s replacement.

‘Once Alec and David Gill decided to leave in 2013, that was a huge blow to the club and they’ve never really recovered from that,’ he said.

‘If you wanted to be critical you’d say right from the beginning when they replaced David Gill, they replaced him with not a football man, but a merchant banker.

‘If you’ve got the top of the football chain who’s not converse with football, that does make it difficult. I think that once the mistakes start, they multiply.

‘Recruitment has been a big problem. Look at the players they’ve bought, some of them haven’t been up to the standard. Generally it snowballs. That’s been the problem.

‘Also, have United spent enough money to be able to compete at the top? Manchester City have had a huge advantage in the short-term because they haven’t been sticking to the rules and other people have. That’s given them a huge advantage, so we probably couldn’t have competed with them, but we could certainly have competed with Liverpool and Arsenal and whatever else.’
