Who Is Elena Kagan? Ideology, strategy, and the art of disagreement

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

On May 21, 2026, Justice Elena Kagan did something she had never done in nearly 16 years on the Supreme Court: she dissented alone in a signed decision. The case, Havana Docks v. Royal Caribbean Cruises, concerned whether cruise lines could face liability for using Cuban docks confiscated decades earlier. Eight justices, including Kagan’s liberal colleagues Sonia Sotomayor and Ketanji Brown Jackson, sided against the cruise lines. Kagan thought the majority had misunderstood a basic principle of property law: the interest originally confiscated had expired long before the cruise ships arrived.

The solitary dissent was unusual for a justice who has spent much of her tenure avoiding separate opinions. At a judicial conference in July 2025, Kagan observed that the court “speaks best when it speaks as a court,” and acknowledged that she sometimes drafts dissents only to decide against publishing them.

Kagan’s professional background is also unusual. She is the only current justice who arrived at the Supreme Court without previously serving as a judge. Before her appointment in 2010, she had been a law professor, dean of Harvard Law School, a White House adviser, and solicitor general of the United States. Her career was therefore shaped substantially by institutions in which persuasion, negotiation, and managing competing perspectives are routine.

But how much does this explain her jurisprudence? Ideological measures generally place Kagan to the right of her liberal colleagues, and her voting record contains meaningful differences from Sotomayor’s and Jackson’s. Yet those differences are far from uniform, and Kagan’s reluctance to write separately persists even when she reaches precisely the same result as her colleagues.

Understanding Kagan therefore requires examining not only how she votes, but how she chooses to agree and disagree.

Kagan has generally occupied a position to the right of the other liberal justices in measures of Supreme Court ideology. But the distance between them depends considerably on how ideology is measured, which cases are examined, and when the comparison is made.

The best-known measure is the Martin–Quinn score, which estimates the justices’ ideological positions from their voting patterns over time. In the 2024-25 term, Kagan’s score was −1.85, compared with −2.80 for Jackson and −4.19 for Sotomayor. Kagan was thus the rightmost of the three liberals, although she remained substantially to the left of the court’s median justice, Amy Coney Barrett, whose score was +0.53. The estimates for Jackson and Kagan had been closer in earlier terms (and, in fact, Jackson was initially positioned slightly to Kagan’s right).

The scores capture something revealing about how the justices vote, but they do not directly measure the substance of their legal reasoning or their willingness to compromise. Nor does a justice’s location relative to the other liberals necessarily reveal whether her own jurisprudence has changed. Martin–Quinn estimates are relative positions derived from voting coalitions, and those coalitions are themselves influenced by the court’s membership and docket.

The voting record offers a more concrete comparison. During the 2010–2021 terms, there were 56 contested decisions in which Kagan joined the majority while Sotomayor dissented, compared with just 19 in which their positions were reversed. Kagan was nearly three times as likely to be on the prevailing side when the two disagreed. That is a substantial difference, although it does not establish that every disagreement reflected an ideological division.

Justice Stephen Breyer, long considered a moderate liberal on the court, provides a useful point of reference. During their overlapping time on the court, Kagan joined the majority without Breyer 41 times, while Breyer joined the majority without Kagan 31 times. The more balanced relationship suggests that Kagan’s historical voting behavior often resembled Breyer’s more closely than Sotomayor’s.

The picture has changed since Jackson joined the court in 2022. Across 121 contested decisions in which Kagan and Sotomayor both participated through the 2025 term, they voted on the same side 109 times. Kagan prevailed without Sotomayor seven times, while Sotomayor prevailed without Kagan five times. Their historical difference in majority participation has thus narrowed dramatically.

The convergence is particularly visible in closely divided cases. Of 82 decisions decided 5–4 or 6–3 in which Kagan, Sotomayor, and Jackson all participated, the three liberals voted together in 71. When the court divided most sharply, Kagan usually found herself alongside her liberal colleagues rather than occupying a position between the liberal and conservative wings.

There are then two different stories in the numbers. Over much of her tenure, Kagan was more likely than Sotomayor to support outcomes favored by conservative justices. More recently, that distinction has diminished, particularly in closely divided cases. Whether this reflects a change in Kagan’s jurisprudence or the changing opportunities for cross-ideological agreement remains uncertain.

The cases in which she breaks with her liberal colleagues offer a way to examine that distinction more closely.

One way to identify a comparatively moderate justice is to examine how frequently she votes with colleagues across the ideological divide. By that measure, Kagan has demonstrated considerable independence from her liberal colleagues.

Across 26 terms, Kagan was the only liberal voting alongside conservative justices in 27 contested decisions, approximately 5.3% of her non-unanimous votes. Sotomayor occupied that position in 18 decisions, or 3.4%. Yet Breyer did so in 29 cases, representing 7.1% of his contested votes during the years he served alongside Kagan. When he was on the court, Breyer, rather than Kagan, was more likely to separate from the other liberals in that way.

The pattern becomes even more interesting with Jackson’s arrival. During the 2022–2025 terms, Kagan was the sole liberal voting with conservatives in nine contested cases, compared with ten for Jackson (though this frequency has decreased) and just two for Sotomayor. Jackson’s cross-ideological votes were evenly divided between joining majorities and dissents, while Kagan’s predominantly placed her in the majority. Thus, even when two justices cross ideological lines with similar frequency, they may not be doing the same thing.

These voting coalitions also raise a question about Kagan’s reputation as a consensus-builder. Does she actually attract more conservative support when writing for the court?

The analysis provides surprisingly little evidence that she does. Of Kagan’s 47 majority opinions in contested decisions, 27 attracted at least three conservative votes. Sotomayor achieved the same result in 30 of her 49 contested majority opinions. Their records are then similar, suggesting that Kagan’s reputation for building agreement is not necessarily reflected in a distinctive ability to assemble ideologically diverse majorities.

The individual cases also complicate the idea that Kagan’s departures from the liberal wing are simply strategic compromises. In 2020’s Kahler v. Kansas, for example, Kagan wrote for six justices, including five conservatives, holding that the Constitution does not require states to recognize an insanity defense based on a defendant’s inability to distinguish right from wrong. Breyer, joined by Ginsburg and Sotomayor, dissented. Kagan emphasized the historical diversity of insanity rules and the latitude afforded to state legislatures; Breyer saw Kansas’s approach as eliminating a fundamental protection deeply rooted in criminal law.

Four years later, Pulsifer v. United States presented a dispute over the meaning of a single conjunction in a federal sentencing statute. Kagan wrote for a six-justice majority, including five conservatives, adopting a reading that restricted defendants’ eligibility for relief from mandatory minimum sentences. Gorsuch dissented, joined by Sotomayor and Jackson, favoring an interpretation that would have made relief more widely available.

Neither case is easily explained as Kagan merely accommodating conservative colleagues and both show substantive disagreements over the meaning of legal rules, with significant consequences for criminal defendants.

These decisions also show why ideological distance and judicial method cannot be treated as interchangeable. Kagan’s cross-ideological votes sometimes reflect legal commitments that produce outcomes different from those favored by her liberal colleagues. They also suggest that her willingness to build agreement does not automatically lead her toward the narrowest available resolution.

If Kagan is not consistently more successful than Sotomayor at attracting conservative votes, the more distinctive feature of her jurisprudence may lie elsewhere: what she does after reaching agreement, and particularly when she decides that a disagreement warrants an opinion of her own.

The art of agreement: choosing when to write

The most distinctive feature of Kagan’s judicial behavior may have less to do with how she votes than with how she expresses agreement. During her 16 terms on the court, Kagan authored just 18 concurrences in signed decisions, compared with 81 for Sotomayor. Breyer, often described as an institutionally minded pragmatist, also wrote concurrences at more than twice Kagan’s rate during their overlapping service. Jackson has been considerably more prolific in her shorter tenure. Kagan’s comparative restraint is therefore striking even among justices who share many of her substantive positions.

The difference becomes even more revealing when the comparison is limited to cases in which the justices reached exactly the same outcome. Between 2010 and 2021, Kagan and Sotomayor both supported a conservative-authored majority opinion in 93 contested decisions. Sotomayor wrote separately in 13 of those cases; Kagan did so in just one. The difference is difficult to explain through ideology or case selection alone: the two justices were participating in the same decisions, agreeing with the same majorities, yet approaching the question of whether to write separately very differently.

The distinction has narrowed somewhat in recent years, but it has not vanished. During the 2022–2025 terms, Kagan wrote separately in three of 38 conservative-authored majorities she shared with Sotomayor, compared with five for Sotomayor. In 29 comparable cases involving Kagan and Jackson, Kagan wrote separately twice and Jackson eight times. These samples are smaller, but they reinforce the larger finding that Kagan is particularly reluctant to add her individual voice to an outcome she already supports.

Kagan herself has offered a remarkably candid explanation for this. Speaking at the 9th Circuit Judicial Conference in July 2025, she described the Supreme Court as an institution whose opinions should communicate a collective judgment. She acknowledged that producing a single institutional voice requires “a little bit of flexibility and compromise.” Separate opinions, she suggested, can complicate the law by presenting competing explanations of what the court has supposedly decided.

Yet Kagan’s restraint should not be mistaken for unqualified agreement. In 2024’s United States v. Rahimi, she joined Roberts’ majority upholding a federal restriction on firearm possession by individuals subject to certain domestic violence restraining orders. She also joined Sotomayor’s concurrence, which defended the majority’s application of New York State Rifle & Pistol Association Inc. v. Bruen while reiterating their disagreement with that earlier Second Amendment decision holding there to be a constitutional right to carry a firearm. Jackson wrote her own concurrence addressing the difficulties lower courts had encountered applying Bruen. All three liberals agreed with the outcome, but their choices about authorship differed.

A similar pattern appeared in 2023’s National Pork Producers Council v. Ross, in which the court rejected an argument by pork producers that a law violated the Constitution by regulating the pork industry outside of California. Kagan joined Sotomayor’s separate opinion explaining why they agreed with the judgment without embracing portions of Gorsuch’s reasoning that threatened to alter the established constitutional framework. Kagan was willing to register a qualification, but she did so by joining a colleague’s opinion rather than writing another.

These distinctions again complicate the portrayal of Kagan as a consensus-builder. The data do not show that she consistently attracts more conservative support to her own majority opinions than Sotomayor does. They show something different: Kagan is considerably more selective about articulating separate disagreements when she already supports the court’s disposition. That behavior may preserve a clearer institutional message, but it also leaves fewer public indication of her reservations about particular decisions.

The difference matters when interpreting Kagan’s ideological position. A justice who joins an opinion without writing separately may appear closer to its author than one who publishes a concurrence, even when both share the same substantive objection. Kagan’s record suggests the absence of a separate opinion can reflect a decision about judicial expression rather than any lack of disagreement.

The limits of agreement: textualism, precedent, and dissent

Kagan’s willingness to work across ideological lines has never meant avoiding deep disagreements with the court’s conservatives. Some of her sharpest opinions have challenged not only their conclusions but also their fidelity to the interpretive principles they profess to follow.

In a 2015 lecture at Harvard Law School honoring Antonin Scalia, Kagan famously remarked, “We’re all textualists now.” The observation acknowledged Scalia’s influence on statutory interpretation, including among justices who did not share his ideological commitments. Seven years later, in her dissent in West Virginia v. EPA, Kagan explicitly revisited that statement: “It seems I was wrong.”

Her objection concerned the majority’s use of the major questions doctrine to restrict the Environmental Protection Agency’s authority under the Clean Air Act. Kagan argued that the court had departed from ordinary statutory interpretation by demanding an unusually clear congressional authorization for agency action of substantial economic or political significance. In her account, the conservative majority’s claimed commitment to textualism yielded when the statutory text pointed toward an outcome it disfavored.

The exchange is revealing because Kagan was challenging the majority from within a methodology often associated with conservative jurisprudence. Her embrace of textualism did not entail agreement with the conservatives about what particular statutes meant, much less about how much authority courts should exercise over Congress and administrative agencies.

That distinction became even clearer in 2024’s Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo. Writing for the three liberals, subject to Jackson’s recusal in one of the consolidated cases, Kagan defended Chevron deference (deferring to agency interpretations of ambiguous statutes) against the majority’s decision to overrule it. Her argument rested on statutory interpretation, congressional allocation of authority, administrative expertise, and the importance of precedent (a common theme for her). Textualism, in Kagan’s jurisprudence, does not necessarily require courts to resolve every statutory ambiguity without deference to an agency authorized to administer the law.

The same institutional concerns appear in her criticism of the court’s emergency docket. In Trump v. Wilcox, Kagan dissented from an order permitting the administration, pending further litigation, to remove officials from independent agencies. Joined by Sotomayor and Jackson, she objected both to the treatment of longstanding precedent and to the use of an expedited proceeding to alter the practical operation of existing law without ordinary briefing and argument.

These opinions provide an important counterweight to Kagan’s reputation for accommodation. Her restraint in writing separately does not preclude forceful disagreement when she believes the court has departed from established interpretive or institutional principles. Nor does that pattern necessarily distinguish her substantive positions from those of Sotomayor and Jackson, who joined these dissents.

The recurring question is therefore not simply whether Kagan is willing to compromise but where she draws the line between disagreements she can accommodate and those that she considers important enough to articulate publicly.

Sixteen years of decisions reveal a justice whose reputation for moderation captures only part of her judicial behavior. Kagan has historically been more willing than Sotomayor to join conservative-supported outcomes, and some of those differences reflect genuine disagreements about the law. Yet the gap has narrowed considerably in recent terms, particularly in cases that divide the court along ideological lines.

What distinguishes Kagan more consistently is her approach to disagreement. She is not uniquely successful at attracting conservative votes to her majority opinions. Rather, she is unusually reluctant to write separately when she can support an existing opinion, even in cases where her liberal colleagues reach the same result but feel compelled to explain their reservations. Her willingness to join another justice’s concurrence further complicates any assumption that silence necessarily signifies agreement.

The distinction has become more consequential as the court has moved rightward. Kagan’s opportunities to assemble majorities across ideological lines have diminished, while her dissents in disputes over administrative authority, precedent, and institutional power have become an increasingly public part of her jurisprudence.

Whether those patterns reflect a conscious strategy, enduring professional instincts, or some combination of the two cannot be determined from voting records alone. But the comparisons suggest that understanding Kagan requires looking beyond where she falls on an ideological scale. Her place on the court is defined both by the legal positions she takes and by the choices she makes about when – and how – to express them.

Dr. Adam Feldman is the creator and author of the blog Empirical SCOTUS and the Substack Legalytics.

We Tested the Viral ‘Slither Rig’ So You Don’t Have To

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 3:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

When it comes to bass fishing baits, the Slither Rig is the latest craze on social media. Half flipping bait, half saltwater spoon, this setup certainly raises an eyebrow at first glance. But when an urchin is the most effective lure for catching a bass, who’s to say a saltwater spoon couldn’t work, too? So I set out to try this new contraption, and here’s what I found.

The Slither Rig consists of three parts: a saltwater jigging spoon, a flipping hook, and a flipping bait. Any saltwater jigging spoon will do; I picked up a pack of five for about 12 bucks on Amazon. You’ll want the spoon to weigh at least an ounce, because you’ll essentially be using it as a flipping weight. Remove the hooks from the spoon and replace the back one with a 4/0 flipping hook. Then slide your soft-plastic bait onto the hook, rig it weedless, and you’re ready to go.

The idea behind the Slither Rig is that you replace the traditional bullet-style weight with a spoon and use the weight of that spoon to plunge your bait through thick vegetation. For this style of fishing, you’ll need an extra-heavy/fast flipping/punching rod that’s at least 7 feet, 8 inches long. Pair that rod with a reel with a 7:1 or higher gear ratio, and spool the combo with 65-pound or stronger braid.

I fished the Slither Rig intermittently over the course of three trips, totaling six or seven hours with it. Toward the end of my testing, I finally got a couple of bites flipping thick gator grass and hyacinth mats. But up until that point, I found the Slither Rig to be more trouble than it was worth.

My biggest frustration with the Slither Rig was its inability to punch cleanly through the mat. Granted, I was only using a 1-ounce spoon in areas where I would typically use a 1 1/2-ounce tungsten weight. But this chunk of metal was already as long as my bait, so I didn’t want to go any bigger for fear of increasing the risk of a fish hitting the spoon and missing the bait carrying the hook. And I’m not sure it would have mattered anyway. The issue had less to do with the weight of the setup and more to do with the way it hinged in the middle.

Nine times out of ten, when I flipped the Slither Rig out, it would land flat on the cover and then bend in the middle, causing both the bait and the spoon to catch on the cover. It was like trying to push an already-sprung drywall anchor into a hole in the wall. The physics just didn’t work.

I found that I could flip the bait higher and get it to go through a little better, but the kerplunk of it smacking the mat was off-putting to me and no doubt disturbing to any bass sitting in the foot or two of water beneath the mats. So I was left with no option but to flip the bait onto the mat and then gently jiggle it until it fell through, which worked about a third of the time.

Personally, I don’t see a situation in which the Slither Rig would ever be a better choice than a traditional punching setup. Yes, the spoon adds flash that could have some appeal in stained water, perhaps. But silver tungsten weights can do the same thing, and they punch through cover far more cleanly.

While I did manage to get a couple of bites on the Slither Rig, I have zero doubt that I could have caught more fish had I flipped a traditional pegged punching rig or Texas rig through all of the same areas, simply because I would have been able to present my bait to the bass better.

Is the Slither Rig legit? Practically speaking, no. You may see someone on facespace talking about how it’s the next big thing, but don’t let them fool you. While I did prove to myself that I could get bit on it, the mechanics of the setup just aren’t conducive to heavy cover. Consider this one debunked.

Nintendo says it isn't planning any more Switch 2 price hikes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo, which unveiled the results of the first half of its fiscal year 2027 earlier today, has now said it has access to enough quantities of memory to manufacture more Switch 2 consoles.

As a result, the platform holder does not plan to increase the price of the Switch 2. That's according to Reuters, citing Nintendo president Shuntaro Furukawa.

Nintendo, which has so far sold 26m Switch 2 consoles, has already raised the price of the console earlier this year. The price hikes, which took effect on 1st September in the US, Canada, Japan and Europe, saw the console's price increase by $50 / €30 / ¥10,000. Although that was technically the first price hike for the console itself, prior to the Switch 2's launch last year, Nintendo raised the price of Switch 2 accessories in the US as a result of the Trump Administration's tariffs.

The reason that would cause Nintendo to raise prices again is the same that already caused rival PlayStation and Xbox to do so: AI-driven, global memory and storage shortages.

PlayStation already raised PS5 and PS5 Pro prices, on several occasions. Xbox has likewise gone through multiple rounds of price hikes for its Series X and Series S consoles, with the most recent having taken place in August.

That same crisis pushed one of Nintendo's longtime ROM suppliers to exit the business, opting instead to focus on the more lucrative business segments. ROM memory is used in Switch cartridges, though Nintendo hasn't commented on the news.

Incidentally, Switch 2 hardware sales have been in decline for several quarters now compared to its banner launch in 2025. Still, Switch 2 sales are tracking ahead of the Switch 1.

Tool Trials: Putting Ridgid’s Cordless Crown Stapler To the Test

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Is a cordless stapler a real upgrade, or just a nice-to-have? I put one to the test to find out.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

Last spring, I was sitting at home with my family watching football on a Sunday when my wife caught me doing what I often do: scrolling Zillow. “What don’t you like about the house we’re in?” she asked. I told her that ever since I finished the basement, I’d missed having a shop space to work on projects.

Her answer: “Then build that garage you always wanted.”

Fast forward eight months, and my new garage is up. I’m insulating the main level and sheathing it with OSB before adding cabinets and shelving and moving on to the upstairs. It’s a big garage; we’re talking 30-plus rolls of insulation on the main level, so on a run to pick up the next half-dozen rolls, I wandered down the tool aisle and saw the Ridgid 18V Cordless 3/8-in. Crown Stapler staring back at me.

Beyond getting the garage insulation up faster, it looked like a tool I’d keep using on future wiring and upholstery projects, and the same goes for anyone re-screening a porch, recovering a chair seat, or tacking down low-voltage wire.

A cordless 3/8-in. crown stapler kit with a 2.0 Ah battery and charger, depth adjustment, an onboard wire and screen attachment, and a built-in LED light.

The Ridgid 18V Cordless 3/8-in. Crown Stapler is a 3-lb. battery-powered stapler that runs on Ridgid’s 18V battery platform. It features an integrated LED light, an onboard wire and screen attachment, a depth adjustment knob, and a belt hook.

It drives 3/8-in. crown (T50) staples from 1/4 to 9/16 in. long. Ridgid claims it can drive 105 staples per minute and up to 6,800 staples on a single charge of a 4.0 Ah battery.

One last note, and in full transparency, I’m a regular user of Ridgid tools myself: Ridgid offers a lifetime service agreement on its tools as long as you register them within 90 days of purchase.

My goal was to install a half-dozen rolls of insulation, then run wire to an outlet to try out the wire attachment. I wanted to see how easy the tool was to use, whether it jammed, and what I liked and didn’t like about it.

The short version: this tool is awesome. No jams, nothing wonky. It’s as simple as load, point, and pull the trigger. It took a little playing with the depth knob to get the staples seating at the right depth, but I had it figured out within a handful of staples.

I really like the wire attachment. I try to keep my wiring clean and neat, usually with zip ties and tie mounts. With this attachment, running the wire was simple, and the stapler did it much more neatly than I expected.

I was also pleasantly surprised by the onboard LED light. It has its own trigger, separate from the firing trigger, so I could turn it on only when I needed it.

It’s also easy on the battery. I put up six rolls of insulation during the test and a couple more off camera, and the indicator on the 2.0 Ah battery didn’t budge.

There were a couple of things I didn’t like, though they’re minor. First, Ridgid advertises dry-fire lockout on this stapler, which I took to mean it wouldn’t fire once the staples ran out. On mine, it still did. To be fair, it never marred the work surface, which Ridgid lists as one of the lockout’s benefits, so it may be doing part of its job. But it was annoying to pull the trigger and have nothing come out, and it made it easy to miss that I was out of staples.

Second, the depth drifted. After every 20 or 30 staples, I’d notice they were no longer seating at the depth I’d set and were starting to sit proud. I don’t know if that was due to denser 2x4s or inconsistencies between strips of staples, but it was something to keep an eye on. When I noticed it, I’d add a few clicks on the depth knob.

I really like this tool. If you’re only insulating a garage, it’s really nice to have, but not essential. You could get by with a standard stapler. That said, the LED light, the wire attachment, and the price, especially with the battery and charger included, make this a tool I know I’ll use more than I expected on future projects.

A cordless 3/8-in. crown stapler kit with a 2.0 Ah battery and charger, depth adjustment, an onboard wire and screen attachment, and a built-in LED light.

The tool alone retails at The Home Depot for $119, but for the last few months, there’s been a special buy that includes the tool, a charger, and a 2.0 Ah Ridgid battery for $104.

Ridgid is owned by Emerson Electric Co., which makes the red Ridgid plumbing and pipe tools directly. The orange Ridgid cordless power tools, including this stapler, are made by Techtronic Industries (TTI), the company behind Milwaukee and Ryobi, under a licensing agreement with Emerson.

No. Staple guns need staples that match the tool’s crown width, leg length, and wire gauge. The Ridgid 18V crown stapler takes 3/8-in. crown staples from 1/4 to 9/16 in. long, including standard T50 staples. Other staplers may use different types, like narrow-crown or other heavy-duty series, so check the tool or the manual before you buy.

Young Climbers Bag New Routes, Including First Ascent, in Rolwaling

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

An energetic foursome of young French climbers had a busy time recently in Nepal’s Rolwaling Valley, east of the Khumbu. Eric Bazon, Pierre Girot, Sophie Jacob, and Kilian Moni did two new routes, including a first ascent of a 5,205m rocky spire and one very difficult line. They then climbed another peak on a “rest” day.

As soon as the climbers reached Rolwaling, they had to change their primary goal, which was in poor condition. Their acclimatization was also progressing more slowly than expected. Instead, they focused their attention on a ridge they saw from their base camp every morning. On October 20, Bazon, Jacob, and Girot set off toward the summit, while Moni waited for them at the base.

“Before long, the climbing turned vertical, and the ridge became razor-sharp and exposed,” Bazon wrote. The climb took three days on the ridge, bivvies included, and led Bazon, Sophie Jacob, and Pierre Girot to the west summit of ChekiGo (6,128m). It took another full day and one more bivouac to reach base camp.

Cheki Go’s main summit was first climbed in 2005 by Bruce Normand of the UK and Oliver Von Rotz of Switzerland via the west face. A Spanish team led by Mikel Zabalza first reached the west summit via a direct route up the west face in 2017. The team had just returned from bagging the first ascent of Jugal II.

“It was quite an intense experience for a ‘secondary’ project: one that combined rock climbing on mediocre granite, improvised protection, and steep snow ridges, all while hauling our ropes on our backs,” said Bazon.

The ridge was technical enough to be a good start: 5c on rock and M4 on mixed terrain. In Alpine grades, the team assessed it as TD (very difficult).

Since the weather forecast looked good for another four days, the team decided to continue climbing. They split into two teams: Bazon and Girot headed for a peak previously climbed by elite American alpinists Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey in 2014: Chukyima Go (6,259m). Like their predecessors, the French team approached the west face from the Naa Valley in Rolwaling.

“There was this direct, narrow, and obvious couloir that simply begged to be climbed,” Bazon reported.

The climbers at an open bivy. Photo: Eric Bazon

The next morning, they found the west face in great condition, which allowed them to climb a direct route to the summit along rock cracks, steep ice, and varied mixed terrain.

It took them two days (October 27-28), with one cold bivy on the face with their feet in their backpacks, to complete the route’s 23 pitches. Their West Face Direct route is 900m long, and the team assessed it as ED (extremely difficult), with M6 on mixed sections, and an Alpine ice grade of AI5.

The photo below shows that the new French route, TchikitaMa Go, runs to the right of the previous line opened by the two Americans.

Left: the new route opened by the French team, compared, right, to the 2014 line by Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey. Photo: Eric Bazon/PlanetMountain

Meanwhile, Sophie Jacob and Kilian Moni went for an unclimbed peak that also rose right in front of their base camp: 5,205m Janjmu. The pair completed the first ascent on an aesthetic 600m line up the north face. They called the route The Eternal Stars (“Les etoiles eternelles”) after some friends who had recently perished.

“It felt just like being at home in the Alps, with the added challenge of high altitude,” the climbers reported. “We pushed ourselves to the limit, both physically and mentally, across demanding yet incredibly logical pitches.”

But this was still not enough for the pair. They squeezed in a final climb of Yalung Ri in what they called “a contemplative, active recovery” day. They then finished the trip by paragliding from the summit.

Kilian Moni flies from the top of Yalung Ri. Photo: Kilian Moni

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

If youâve seen The Pitt, youâve got an idea of what happens behind the scenes in an emergency vet hospital. Thereâs always another case to see, another owner to talk to, another patient to check up on. The biggest difference is that we donât often refer things. The initial emergency clinician is also the one assessing the radiographs, interpreting bloodwork, performing surgeries, and managing hospitalized cases. Itâs not uncommon for me to have a critical ICU patient Iâm managing at the same time as Iâm working up an ear infection, or about to go into surgery for a C-section.

Anyone who walks through the ER doors gets triaged, and I see what I can. This can be ear infections, severe dog bite wounds, or behavioral issues.

The most rewarding part of working in emergency veterinary medicine? When the patient is feeling good enough to try to bite you!

Initially, I wasnât sure if I wanted to go to veterinary school. I worked in a boarding clinic attached to a general practice that occasionally let me help on the medicine side, but I wasnât sold on vet med. I didnât get into veterinary school my first application round, so I found a job at a local emergency clinic. My working interview was so wild, I realized Iâd found my passion!

Emergencies come in waves! Some days are âDiarrhea Day,â where everything that comes in has diarrhea. I had âUrine Dayâ last week! By far, Iâd say GI upsets (vomiting, diarrhea, not eating) are the most common ER cases.

Seizures, protracted vomiting, severe weakness. I think anything that comes on suddenly should be investigated quickly!

If youâre worried, Iâm worried. You know your pet best, and if you feel something is âoffâ â donât wait! Even if itâs just for an exam or to get vitals, itâs well worth the peace of mind.

Itâs totally okay to be open about your budget and what you can and cannot do. The ER tends to be more expensive than a daytime practice, and we acknowledge that.  Our job is not to judge; itâs to help! If that means stabilizing until your vet can see you in a few hours, letâs do that.

I saw a lot of disinformation online. I figured if I was louder, I could drown out the bad advice.

I try to time my posts with real-world presentations. Heat stroke cases in the summer, toxicities around the holidays. Sometimes I just have a really interesting case, and I share it right then and there.

That veterinarians are humans too! I post a lot of information for other veterinary professionals, and I hope pet owners see the comments and realize how engaged the community is. Here is a group of people talking about vet med on their day off, learning about cases and new studies, and trying to keep up to date on the latest medicine.

Veterinary medicine is very science-focused. While we are always learning, thereâs a sort of structure to it that doesnât allow for much movement. My books are a whole different part of my brain, one where I can be creative and silly and make up worlds!

âAnother Damn Rescueâ is a spicy shifter romcom where a veterinarian finds an injured âdogâ on the side of the road, only to realize heâs a wolf shifter. Itâs ultimately a love letter to vet med, where I talk about the struggles we face, while also showing the humor and humanity. Oh, and how amazing veterinary technicians, assistants, and receptionists are!

Itâs more of a good time, kick your feet up and giggle sort of story! Pet owners might gain some insight into what itâs like to work in vet med in 2026. Veterinary professionals will enjoy seeing themselves accurately represented in media with a happy ever after!

The podcast is an extension of my social media â itâs a resource for other vets when theyâre not sure where to go. Itâs mostly focused on education for other veterinarians. I bring on specialists and let them pick a topic they feel passionate about. Think of it as continuing education.

Bridging my author identity with my veterinary one is very scary, but also very cool! Iâm especially excited about the podcast because I hope to get companies to talk about new products before they launch as well.

Fatigue! Thereâs compassion and ethical fatigue, where we feel helpless in the face of obstacles outside our reach. Some days I think, âI didnât help anyone today.â And thatâs not true; itâs the story we tell ourselves when weâre in that headspace.

Sometimes we need to take a step back and remember that this is that person's worst day. Ultimately, the team is there to help you. While we donât tolerate abuse, we TOTALLY UNDERSTAND being upset, overwhelmed, having decision paralysis, and everything else that goes along with these scary situations!

I am a pet owner as well as a veterinarian, and Iâve had cases that hit me as a pet owner as well. âIâve been in your shoes before,â goes a long way for owners in these situations. Weâre often told not to interject our opinions, but I am quick to tell owners that Iâve had a pet in the same situation.

Keep ALL of your records! Ask your vet to email you bloodworkand keep it in a folder. Take pictures of your pet's medications. Walking into the ER and saying âHereâs Fluffyâs last bloodworkâ and âhereâs the medication Fluffy is onâ is SO valuable!

XYLITOL!! It can cause fatal low blood sugar and liver damage. Itâs in sugar-free gum, and lately theyâve been calling it âbirch sugar,â so not a lot of people realize itâs the same thing.

Good mentorship is worth its weight in gold! Even the best veterinary school on the planet will not prepare you to step right into an ER without assistance. Ask for help, and find people who are willing to help you learn.

At Waymo, we believe women should get that energy back.

In Loving Memory of Tiger

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

This article is part two of From Misfortune to Blessing: How a Cat Lost a Leg, But Got a Forever Home

Tiger came to my family on 2 June 2025 after an accident at a car park that resulted in his right hind leg being amputated. Before surgery, he was diagnosed with Stage B2 Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) â subclinical heart disease with a high risk of progression. We were advised to follow up on Tigerâs heart condition.

Having incurred a high bill for his condition in 2025, we brought Tiger to a general practice vet in Singapore, not a cardiologist. It was our first time rescuing a cat, and we didnât know the difference. The vet was more concerned about Tigerâs kidneys, and during several visits in 2025, she focused on his renal function. At each visit, she would listen to Tigerâs heart with a stethoscope and tell us the condition âshould still be the sameâ. Unknown to us, Tigerâs heart condition was quietly deteriorating.

In late December 2025, we suggested to the general vet that we book an echocardiogram to scan Tigerâs heart. We had to wait for the appointment, and it finally took place on 14 February 2026.

When Tiger came home from the echocardiogram, he was extremely weak. He had not eaten since midnight the previous day; the gabapentin used to relax him for the scan made him so weak that he could barely stand up, and he pooped on the floor.

The echocardiogram results took more than three weeks, and by then we were informed that Tigerâs heart condition had progressed from HCM stage B2 to congestive heart failure. Our vet asked for a one-minute video of Tigerâs breathing and told us his respiration rate was between 42 and 56, significantly higher than the normal upper limit of 30.

Two days later, Tiger had a thoracentesis procedure at our regular vet to remove excess fluid from around his lungs, and was discharged a few hours later. He was also placed on furosemide (a diuretic) and clopidogrel (a blood thinner).

It was our first time administering medicine to a cat. Despite our inexperience, we managed to administer furosemide as it was a liquid. Clopidogrel proved much harder to administer; we tried hiding the tablets inside a pill pocket, but Tiger hardly ate it.

In the initial few days after the thoracentesis procedure, Tigerâs breathing was less laboured. But I noticed that despite giving Tiger furosemide, he did not drink more water or urinate more often, and it didnât take long for fluid to accumulate in his lungs again.

On 25 March 2026, Tiger had his first major episode of congestive heart failure.

He groaned and lay on the floor; his breathing was laboured, and he was experiencing nystagmus (involuntary rhythmic movement of his eyeballs). We immediately took him to the emergency room at Beecroft Hospital.

At Beecroft Hospital, we were told that Tiger was about to âfall off the cliffâ and almost had a stroke. He had another thoracentesis procedure to remove fluid from his lungs and was placed in an oxygen chamber for observation for the next few days. We were told that the dosage prescribed by our regular vet was insufficient to control Tigerâs heart condition.

After a few days, Tiger was discharged on the maximum dose of furosemide, together with a combination of other medications (spironolactone, pimobendan, rivaroxaban, and a potassium supplement).

The hospital nurse demonstrated the proper ways to administer Tigerâs medicine, which our regular vet had not done. Initially, we dissolved the pills in a small amount of water and syringe-fed him, but this method was messy and wasted some of the medicine. Later, we placed the different medications in a single capsule and used a pill popper to administer them. This proved much more effective.

We had to feed the medicine at the same time each day, as delaying the diuretic by a few hours could cause the fluids to build up again. We had to adjust our life schedule around Tigerâs medication. We were also introduced to the Cardalis app to monitor Tigerâs resting respiratory rate.

After being discharged from the hospital, Tigerâs breathing was normal at first, but within a week, we were back in the emergency room. This time his diuretic was changed to torsemide, a stronger diuretic.

All in all, Tiger was in and out of the emergency room every week for a total of four visits, and the bills were mounting fast.

The cardiologist told us Tigerâs prognosis was poor and suggested euthanasia. We consulted an animal communicator who told us Tiger wanted to fight on. We were prepared for palliative care. February to April was a sad and emotionally draining period for us all.

With the stronger diuretic, his breathing rate was normal from May to July. We also gradually reintroduced his renal diet. It was a happy three months for us with many fond memories. He even bonded with my other cat during this time. For a while, we were able to forget about his palliative condition.

In early August, his breathing became laboured again, and he refused to eat his renal food, and I knew his kidneys would deteriorate fast on a normal diet. In the next two weeks, his appetite dropped, he hid more frequently around the house, and became increasingly lethargic.

From 20 August 2026 (Thursday) onwards, he would barely manage more than a few licks a day, and his weight dropped a lot. We could feel his spine and rib cage! He had not urinated for more than 24 hours, and we were so worried that his kidneys had totally shut down.

On Friday, miraculously, he urinated a few times and had some licks of food. His last meal was on Saturday night.

Tiger passed away naturally and peacefully at home on Sunday, 23 August 2026. He was so weak and lethargic, with barely any energy left, but still managed to get inside his little box and slept there. In this final moment, my sister calmed him to his final breath, and we were fortunate to witness his final, peaceful passing.

Throughout this ordeal, Tiger was very, very resilient and wanted to live, but he did not get the chance.

We held a private cremation for him on 24 August 2026, complete with Taoist chanting to guide him on his way. His ashes are stored at Mandai Pet Sanctuary in Singapore.

Despite the financial, emotional, and time sacrifices, it was an honour to take care of Tiger in the final stage of his life.

Now the room feels so empty in his absence.

Sheryl Sin is an editor, proofreader, indexer and writer based in Singapore. Outside her work, she is a committed community cat feeder who takes care of the street cats in her neighbourhood. She is also mum to Cow Girl (an FIV+ black-and-white domestic shorthair adopted off the street) and Tiger (the tripod cat with kidney and heart issues). Follow their journey on Instagram @sinmp24

Aina’s smart ring has a screen, a joystick, and a mic so you can talk to AI agents

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Hardware startup Aina released a smart ring today that features a screen, a joystick, and a mic so that you can talk to your agents and take notes. The $399 ring has a speaker so you can take calls using it as well.

The startup, which raised $5.5 million in July, is positioning the ring as a controller of sorts for both AI and non-AI apps.

The company previously made Mirage, a three-key controller that let you invoke buttons based on the app on your Mac’s screen. For instance, if you were on a Google Meet call, you could map keys to camera on/off, mic mute/unmute, or to leave the meeting.

Aina has baked in lessons from that device to create the new ring, which it’s simply calling “AI ring.” Using its mic, you can take notes, send a prompt to agents, set reminders, and dictate notes on your Mac using Wispr Flow or the startup’s own dictation engine.

Now, there are already smart rings in the market that do similar things, like the Pebble Index 01, Sandbar Stream, Oasis, and Natura, which is probably why Aina has added more controls and functionality to stand out. Its ring comes with a speaker so that you can take calls, and there is a screen, which lets you see notifications from your phone or which apps you are controlling.

For navigation, there is a joystick, and it also supports gestures, so that it can trigger different actions based on what’s on your Mac or phone’s screen. The ring also lets you control music or even smart home devices like Apple TV.

I have used the ring for a few days, and it feels like a smartwatch in a ring format, with more controls. I can get notifications and reply to them. It surfaces my meetings; I can call Uber if I want to get a ride home.

It’s easier to use Apple TV’s remote itself, but the Aina ring proved useful the couple times I couldn’t find the remote. Granted, a lot of the software and features are still being fleshed out, so it’s hard to figure out where such a device ring might sit in my daily usage, but it is squarely aimed at folks who are surrounded by digital devices and AI applications.

“Phones and laptop screens were designed for an age of browsing where you think about opening apps. For interacting with proactive agents, we need a better interface, and that is what we had in mind while designing Aina,” Apoorv Shankar, Aina’s founder, told TechCrunch over a call.

Companies are trying to cater to programmers and AI enthusiasts with specialized hardware that could help them harness different agents. This includes OpenAI’s experimental keyboard and Rabbit trying to position itself as a controller. The hope with such hardware is that enough people will have multiple agents running to invest in specific gadgets to keep tabs on them.

Aina’s ring goes on preorder starting Thursday at early-bird pricing of $399, and the company plans to start shipping the device in January 2027. Once the early-bird period ends, the ring will cost $499.

Se pensant hors antenne, Bertrand Latour révèle avoir gagné de l'argent en pariant sur les 10 buts inscrits du match Lens - Côme !

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Pensant être hors antenne après la rencontre rocambolesque entre le RC Lens et Côme, le journaliste de Canal+ Bertrand Latour s'est retrouvé piégé par un micro resté ouvert. Une confidence inattendue sur ses gains aux paris sportifs qui n'a pas manqué de faire réagir sur les réseaux sociaux.

Ce mercredi 4 novembre 2026, à l'occasion de la 4e journée de la phase de ligue de la Ligue des champions, le RC Lens a sombré sur sa pelouse du stade Bollaert-Delelis face aux Italiens de Côme (1-9). Une déroute sans précédent dans la prestigieuse compétition européenne, marquée par un scénario fou qui a manifestement profité à certains observateurs.

La séquence s'est produite lors de la reprise d'antenne sur le plateau de l'émission « Canal Champions Club », présentée par Hervé Mathoux sur Canal+. Alors que le programme reprenait son cours, les micros ouverts ont capté les échanges privés de Bertrand Latour avec les consultants présents à ses côtés, à savoir Olivier Dacourt, Laure Boulleau et Walid Regragui.

Toutefois, cette scène n'a pas été diffusée sur les antennes françaises de la chaîne cryptée. C'est en effet sur les flux internationaux retransmis hors de France que les téléspectateurs ont pu entendre les propos du journaliste en direct, avant que l'extrait ne soit largement relayé sur les réseaux sociaux. Un couac technique.

Se croyant à l'abri des antennes, Bertrand Latour a révélé avoir tenté un coup particulièrement audacieux au cours de cette soirée européenne. Le journaliste a ainsi précisé à ses collègues avoir misé la somme de 10 euros sur « 9 et 10 buts » dans la rencontre opposant les Lensois aux Transalpins.

Une inspiration payante au terme d'un scénario surréaliste. Grâce à cette avalanche de buts, Bertrand Latour a confié avoir remporté un gain de plus de 500 euros. De quoi provoquer l'étonnement de ses interlocuteurs sur le plateau, surpris par cette confidence.

En revanche, la soirée a été bien plus compliquée pour le RC Lens. En s'inclinant sur ce score fleuve de 9 à 1 face à Côme, le club artésien a en effet concédé la plus lourde défaite à domicile de l'histoire de la Ligue des champions. Ce revers historique relègue désormais la formation nordiste à la 33e place du classement de la phase de ligue.

Il faudra rapidement réagir pour chasser les doutes. Le prochain rendez-vous du RC Lens est fixé au dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 20h45 au stade Bollaert-Delelis face à l'Olympique de Marseille, pour le compte de la 10e journée de Ligue 1.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/oscar-health-boasts-strongest-year-its-history-after-outperforming-q3" hreflang="en">Oscar Health boasts ‘strongest year’ in its history after outperforming in Q3</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →
Oscar Health boasts ‘strongest year’ in its history after outperforming in Q3" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Oscar Health is seeing the “strongest year” in its history in 2026, CEO Mark Bertolini told investors during the company’s third quarter earnings call Thursday morning.

“We delivered record profitability for the first nine months of the year, reporting earnings from operations of $1.3 billion and net income of $1.2 billion,” he said. “We also expect to achieve several of our 2024 Investor Day commitments one year ahead of plan.”

Oscar Health brought in $5.1 billion in Q3 revenue, up “roughly 70%” year-over-year, Bertolini said. It beat analysts’ estimates of $4.72 billion.

Its medical loss ratio was 84%, down from 88.5% year-over-year, according to financial results published Thursday morning.

The company ended Q3 with 2.7 million members, up 27% year-over-year, Bertolini said, adding it is entering open enrollment with a “24% larger footprint” with plans to expand into South Carolina. “Our expansion increases our total addressable market to approximately 10 million consumers across 21 states,” he said.

Moreover, Oscar Health is launching a “new wave” of consumer lifestyle products and “addressing affordability through bundled benefits,” Bertolini said.

He also pointed to new artificial intelligence capabilities across the Oscar Health platform, including Lucie. Unveiled in April, the platform is a one-stop shop for individuals seeking health plans and supplemental benefit options.

“No other platform brings these capabilities together in one place, he said. “Lucie is on track to sell approximately 800,000 new policies in 2027, driven by growing broker and consumer demand.”

Based on the company’s performance, CFO Scott Blackley said Oscar Health is raising its 2026 full year outlook. Earnings are expected to be in a range of $850 million to $950 million, Blackley said.

Total revenue is expected to be in the range of $19 billion to $19.3 billion, which Blackley notes is “an increase of $300 million” from its prior outlook.

Blackley continued that the company expects to end the year with 2.5 million members, a figure that reflects “additional disenrollments from the second CMS review.” A full-year MLR of 80.5% to 81.5% is also expected.

De Roon: “I never had any doubts about joining Roma.”

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Marten De Roon gave an interview to Idealista.it in which he discussed his arrival in Rome.

“When the coach called me, I already knew it would be a difficult move, partly because of the history of Roma and Atalanta, partly because of my history with Roma on social media; but at a certain point we had made up our minds, I had no doubts. The pitch speaks for itself. I had no doubts; I wanted to prove on the pitch my desire to be here. Gasperini is also demonstrating this; facts count, there’s time.”

The Dutch midfielder explained how he experiences football: “In two different ways. On the pitch, I want to win at all costs. My main quality, what I like to do, is to see the game. I’m tactically prepared, it’s my strength: I see where we can attack well, where we can defend better. From midfield, I like to manage the team, call the markers. Off the pitch, I like to joke, talk, and, above all, observe. I like trying to help my teammates; I’m a professional, and even off the pitch, I try to be helpful, given my age and experience.”

The former Atalanta player spoke about the Giallorossi dressing room: “There are a lot of nice people. I’m still finding my role, so I don’t joke around too much, but the atmosphere is healthy; it seems like an ‘easy’ dressing room. Years ago, I thought it was a very difficult dressing room, even playing against them; now I’ve come in and I’ve seen that it’s healthy, warm, and welcoming. I already knew some of them, obviously Malen, Rensch, Mancini, Gollini, and Cristante. This obviously helps; it’s made things easier.”

De Roon gave a behind-the-scenes look at how he looked for a place to live in the capital: “I spoke to the guys here in the locker room, Pellegrini helped me get to know the city, he gave me a hand with so many things, together with the club.”

Marten De Roon spoke about his relationship with Gasperini: “We spent eight years together, we had the best time with Atalanta, we fought so many battles together, and I’ve always thanked him. Our relationship is above all professional; if he’s your coach, you don’t see him off the pitch during the week. Last year, he called me every now and then, asking about Malen, but more than anything he wanted confirmation; he was already convinced. I had no doubts. He’s doing pretty well, right?”

Did the backlash make Nvidia nerf the AI tech?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As if Potterheads didn't have enough to worry about amid the controversy around the design changes in HBO's Harry Potter series, Hogwarts Legacy now has Nvidia DLSS 5 enhancements. The next generation of Nvidia's AI-driven neural rendering and upscaling came to the game today, and those following the backlash over the tech were prepared for the worst.

So does DLSS 5 ride roughshod over Hogwarts Legacy's art direction and create entirely new characters like people expected when the leaked unfinished DLSS 5 iteration surfaced online back in August? Erm...

As demonstrated by Nvidia in the launch trailer above, the impact of DLSS 5 in Hogwarts Legacy looks surprisingly subtle. The chip giant maintains that the aim is to enhance lighting and material quality to "boost environmental uplift while preserving character identity".

"With the addition of DLSS 3D-Guided Neural Rendering to Hogwarts Legacy, our complete suite of DLSS technologies will now be supported, enabling you to accelerate and enhance your experiences in the wizarding world," Nvidia says.

It seems it just can't please people. After the ridicule Nvidia faced for the initial tests of DLSS 5, some people are now saying that the actual implementation in Hogwarts Legacy is laughably subtle. Some are struggling to see a worthwhile difference even in side-by-side comparisons "Before vs Before", one person jokes in the comments on the trailer above.

What do you think of the comparisons below?

"People complained too much that they just completely nerfed it. Now we just have fps loss with basically little difference in image quality," one person complains in the comments on YouTube.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

"I find it hilarious how the first presentation was so completely over the top strength wise, and now it's reduced to a point where you really can't even tell if it's even on anymore without a direct comparison," someone else writes.

"Asking for another 100-200 watts of power for basically no difference is crazy work," a third person suggests.

Alas, the relatively subtle implementation is unlikely to end the DLSS 5 controversy. While some scenes arguably benefit from a subtle boost in contrast, the extra detail in some of the environments actually makes them feel less atmospheric in my opinion.

Official DLSS 5 support is currently limited to GeForce RTX 50 Series GPUs and RTX Spark systems. Activating DLSS 3D-Guided Neural Rendering, 6X Multi Frame Generation, Ray Reconstruction, NVIDIA Reflex, and ray tracing, allows Hogwarts Legacy to run at a max of 4K at up to 300 frames per second on RTX 5090 (see our guide the best GPUs).

"For an optimal experience, we also recommend enabling DLSS Super Resolution under 'Upscale Type', DLSS Multi Frame Generation under 'Frame Generation', NVIDIA Reflex under 'Low Latency Mode', and 'DLSS Ray Reconstruction' if you're also activating Hogwarts Legacy's immersive ray-traced effects," Nvidia adds.

DLSS 5 is also coming to the free-to-play military combat game War Thunder from mid-November and to the wuxia-inspired Where Winds Meet from 23 November. You can read the full blog post on the Nvidia website

Nvidia said back in March that its next-generation DLSS would bring "photorealism" to gaming through the use of a new generative AI model designed to enhance frames and add detail to lighting, textures and materials in real time. But the GPU maker's early demo immediately sparked controversy by giving Grace from Capcom’s Resident Evil Requiem a glamour makeover.

Leaked through the nvngx_dlssnr.dll library via NBA 2K27, modders soon began testing DLSS 5 for themselves and sharing the videos and screenshots on social media. Faces were the main area of contention, with some modders complaining that Nvidia's new tech makes characters look uncanny and AI-generated or older than they're supposed to be by sharpening skin texture.

That sparked a big debate around artistic intent, with some people concerned that the technology would effectively change game artists' original vision.

Hyperliquid (HYPE) Whales Turn Stan Druckenmiller's Hype Into a $5 Million Exit

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

According to a fresh investigation by Santiment, retail investors, giving in to "fear of missing out" on Hyperliquid (HYPE) price rally on October 30, gave four large wallets a perfect opportunity to fully close their positions, bagging $5 million in profit.

Wall Street legend Stan Druckenmiller's first ever appearance on X (former Twitter) with a post about Hyperliquid and the tokenization of trading was supposed to be a declaration of a new era for the popular DeFi exchange. It turned out to be a "sell the news" signal for HYPE tokens' whales.

The best example of 'cashing out' math was educated by the address "0xa2ce...3126". What was a constant accumulation since the end of May, at the peak of the pump, in the $94–$95 range for HYPE token, became a methodical dump worth exactly $225,000 on the market every five minutes. All this was happening while the crowd bought up on the news. The result is a completely empty wallet and its owner locking in $2,395,168 gains.

This whale was not alone in his approach and as Santiment stresses, citing its own on-chain data, four top players opened short positions simultaneously and are now in profit. The only big buyer who believed in HYPE price growth is losing more than 11%.

The token's price chart against USD only confirms a total exodus of big buyers at the time of writing. The Oct. 30 pump candle left a huge upper wick with a trace of those very five-minute sales. The HYPE price has already slid more than 12% and is squeezed in the $81.79–$85 range. The local level of $84.150 has been broken from top to bottom, and now the token is slowly drifting to the next strong support at $78.020.

Traditional finance's 'big guns' like Druckenmiller undoubtedly remain the best marketing tool for crypto projects. The only difference is that for retail investors a billionaire’s tweet became a call to action, and for big capital an exit order.

The buyers’ rush created a perfect liquidity window for the whales — a chance to sell off huge volumes of coins without the price slipping down. What this makes of the story is that the technology and business of Hyperliquid turned out not to equal the price of decentralized exchange's native token.

Court to consider the reach of Article III courts in immigration contract dispute

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The argument on Nov. 10 in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards brings the justices a complex constitutional problem sure to get their focused attention: does the Constitution allow Congress to delegate to the executive branch – as opposed to the courts – the authority to adjudicate the obligation of an employer to comply with employment agreements it makes with temporary immigrant workers.

Here, the Department of Labor, employing administrative law judges, for several decades has adjudicated disputes under the H-2A visa program, which allows employers to hire temporary foreign agricultural workers. The Department of Labor argues that Sun Valley Orchards failed in several ways to compensate such workers as it promised the government it would to qualify for the H-2A program. As is typical in such cases, administrative employees of the DOL (in its wage and hour division) charged Sun Valley with failing to live up to its obligations, and the DOL for the most part persuaded an ALJ that it was correct. On appeal, a different group of the DOL’s employees (its administrative review board) accepted the ALJ’s findings. At that point, for the first time, Sun Valley had a chance to take the dispute before Article III judges, but under the applicable statute the federal district court owes great deference to the DOL’s adjudication.

The case comes to the justices because the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 3rd Circuit reasoned that the administrative adjudication of the department’s charges cannot be reconciled with Article III of the Constitution.

At a high level of generality, the doctrinal framework here is quite clear. All agree that Congress constitutionally can authorize adjudication by executive branch officials rather than judges, and all agree that its power to do so is limited to cases that involve “public rights,” a notoriously vague category designed to distinguish traditionally executive actions (like granting a visa) from disputes traditionally resolved in court (like tort and contract litigation). When those adjudications go beyond public rights, though, they infringe on the judiciary’s exclusive authority under Article III. The difficulty is that when you get down to details – defining the boundaries of “public rights” – the rulings have neither been stable nor persuasive. The point was made best by Chief Justice William Rehnquist (channeling Matthew Arnold), who famously characterized the decisions as “landmarks on a judicial ‘darkling plain’ where ignorant armies have clashed by night.”

For this particular dispute, the government contends that the case plainly involves public rights because it involves immigration or because it involves the assessment of remedies for transgression of privileges granted by the executive. By contrast, the employer characterizes the subject of the adjudication as a contract dispute. Granting the government’s free ability to decide (and adjudicate if it wishes to do so) the entitlement of any particular immigrants or group of immigrants to a visa or other immigration status, the dispute here involves whether the employer has complied with its employment contract. Because that is a prototypically private dispute – the kind of thing that could have been brought before a court at Westminster in Old England in the days of the drafting of Article III – the employer contends that it is not the kind of public right that Congress can have adjudicated by the executive.

It would be naïve to expect the justices will feel themselves constrained by the existing precedents on this topic. The case directly implicates the prerogatives of the judicial branch and the justices will see it that way. Moreover, decisions like Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo and Trump v. Slaughter show a bench with a great appetite for reining in the administrative state even when precedents are settled and directly on point. And there is another problem for the government here – many of the cases they rely on predate the days of FDR and the rise of the administrative state. Furthermore, none of them crisply validates an administrative adjudication that takes money from one private party and gives it to another.

I would expect Justice Neil Gorsuch to be a major figure at the argument, as he has been pressing this particular point for several years, albeit in the context of the securities laws. For him, the government’s contention that the lower court decision would disrupt a large swath of long-accepted administrative arrangements will be a feature, not a bug. Chief Justice John Roberts also can be expected to be an important player, as he has written several of the modern opinions in the area. So this seemingly narrow dispute may herald something larger: yet another blow against the administrative state.

Ronald Mann is a professor of law at Columbia, where he teaches courses in commercial finance, payment systems and deals.

West Ham Owners’ ‘Marriage Of Convenience’ Could End With Staveley Takeover

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

West Ham’s ownership saga has taken another interesting turn following comments from former football executive Christian Purslow, who believes Daniel Křetínský and David Sullivan could eventually sell the club to Amanda Staveley once the Hammers return to the Premier League.

Speaking to football journalist Henry Winter on The Football Boardroom podcast, Purslow described the relationship between West Ham’s two principal shareholders as a “marriage of convenience” and questioned why the previously announced agreement to purchase Vanessa Gold’s shares has still not been formally completed.

Regular readers of Claret & Hugh, and indeed viewers of Hammers Chat, will not be particularly surprised by any of this. I’ve been describing Sullivan and Křetínský as business partners for some time, and the possibility of Staveley eventually returning to the negotiating table is something we’ve discussed at length.

Purslow’s comments came during a fascinating discussion with Henry Winter about West Ham’s ownership situation, prompted by the dismissal of Nuno Espírito Santo and the appointment of Scott Parker.

The former Liverpool and Aston Villa chief executive suggested that Sullivan and Křetínský may have very little in common, but they share one overriding ambition: getting West Ham back into the Premier League as quickly as possible.

That, according to Purslow, is enough to keep their working relationship together, even if their longer-term ambitions for the football club are different.

And frankly, I think he’s absolutely right.

Ever since the agreement was reached for Křetínský to purchase the bulk of Vanessa Gold’s shares, with Sullivan increasing his own holding from approximately 38.8% to 40%, I’ve referred to the pair as business partners. Indeed, Claret & Hugh reported last month that a club insider had specifically described their relationship as a “joint partnership”.

I appreciate that this might be uncomfortable for some West Ham supporters to hear, particularly those who hoped Sullivan’s departure from his executive position would signal the beginning of the end of his involvement.

But let’s be fair, a man who wants less financial involvement in a football club doesn’t ordinarily increase his shareholding. Whatever anybody thinks of Sullivan, the proposed arrangement would leave him owning approximately 40% of West Ham, and that’s hardly the behaviour of somebody desperate to walk away immediately.

Staveley has been locked out of West Ham for now

What I found particularly interesting was Purslow questioning something we’ve also discussed repeatedly on this website. Why has the agreement to increase Křetínský’s shareholding to 46% still not been formally completed?

The arrangement was announced back in September, with the existing shareholders exercising their rights to purchase Vanessa Gold’s stake and subsequently agreeing to redistribute shares. As our original investigation into the transaction explained, the process was designed to leave Křetínský as West Ham’s largest shareholder, Sullivan on approximately 40% and Tripp Smith on around 11%.

Yet here we are in November, and the anticipated confirmation of the completed transaction has still not arrived. Purslow admitted he found that puzzling and wondered whether the agreement might have encountered difficulties, although he was careful to acknowledge that he did not know whether that was the case.

I completely agree with him that the delay is odd, although I wouldn’t go so far as to suggest the agreement has collapsed. In fact, when Claret & Hugh last enquired, we were assured that there were no problems with the transaction and that completion was expected soon.

Nevertheless, there is another important point here. Křetínský indicated that he would engage with supporters once his increased shareholding had been finalised, and we’re still waiting for that conversation.

Given the importance of the proposed ownership changes, I don’t think it’s unreasonable for supporters to expect some clarity about when the transaction will be completed and what the future structure of the club will look like.

Kretinsky successfully blocked Vanessa Gold’s attempts to sell her shareholding to Staveley but has yet to purchase her shares according to Purslow

Perhaps the most fascinating part of Purslow’s analysis concerned Amanda Staveley, who previously attempted to purchase the Gold family’s stake before the existing shareholders moved to prevent her from acquiring it.

Purslow suggested that once West Ham have secured promotion to the Premier League, both Sullivan and Křetínský could decide that the time is right to sell the entire football club. He also identified Staveley as a possible buyer, pointing to her previous interest, the work she has already undertaken examining the club and her ambitions for the London Stadium.

His assessment was particularly interesting:

“I have a feeling Amanda Staveley might well reappear because she would be the natural buyer for the whole club.”

Now, this is obviously Purslow’s prediction rather than confirmation of any agreement between the shareholders and Staveley. But I have to say, it’s a scenario I’ve considered entirely plausible for some time.

I’ve never fully believed that Staveley had gone away completely. For somebody who appeared so determined to purchase a football club, there has been remarkably little public indication of her pursuing another opportunity since her attempt to buy into West Ham was blocked.

Of course, that doesn’t mean she hasn’t been exploring other possibilities privately. But we already know she has shown a serious interest in West Ham, and Claret & Hugh previously revealed that discussions had taken place over the possibility of her acquiring Sullivan’s shares if the Gold family route proved unsuccessful.

Purslow also raised the question of valuations. He suggested Staveley’s previous proposal valued West Ham at around £600million, while Sullivan has historically placed a considerably higher value on the club. Promotion would clearly change the financial picture, making a potential sale more attractive than it would be while West Ham remain in the Championship.

The suggestion that Sullivan and Křetínský are effectively working together until West Ham secure promotion makes a certain amount of sense to me. Both have an enormous financial interest in the club returning to the Premier League, and that shared objective could be enough to maintain their partnership regardless of any differences between them.

It also puts the appointment of Scott Parker into an interesting context. Having backed Nuno to lead the promotion campaign during the summer, the shareholders have now agreed to change direction and appoint a manager with three Championship promotions on his CV.

Purslow clearly believes promotion could become the catalyst for something much bigger than another managerial change. His suggestion is that the two principal shareholders might eventually decide to cash in, potentially creating the opportunity for Staveley to return with a proposal to purchase the whole club rather than simply a minority stake.

Whether that happens remains to be seen, and there is certainly no evidence of an agreed sale. But I find it difficult to dismiss the possibility, particularly given everything we’ve already been told about the relationship between Sullivan and Křetínský and Staveley’s previous determination to get involved.

For me, the most revealing aspect of Purslow’s comments is that he has independently arrived at a number of the same questions we’ve been asking for months. Why is the share transaction taking so long? What exactly is the relationship between Sullivan and Křetínský? And has Amanda Staveley really disappeared from the picture?

I suspect we haven’t heard the last of any of those three questions.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live.
