Blackburn Rovers sweat over injuries to two key defenders as West Ham challenge awaits

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Blackburn Rovers face West Ham in their final game before the November international break this Saturday. They come into the tie off the back of a 2-1 defeat against Stoke City, and Tony Mowbray’s side were without centre-back Tom Atcheson for the tie, while full-back Ryan Alebiosu came off in the first half.

Now, speaking to the press, Mowbray has said the Rovers duo will require further assessment to see if they can face the Hammers as they struggle with minor issues. As quoted by the club’s official media, the boss said: “Big Tom has still got a little niggle in his groin, so it’s about whether we take a chance with him or not. Ryan is going to go for a scan, and I hope it isn’t too bad.”

Alebiosu has been a mainstay for Blackburn Rovers this season, starting every Championship match and playing the full 90 minutes in all but two games. Atcheson meanwhile has made 11 appearances in all competitions, including 10 league starts.

Blackburn Rovers aren’t blessed for depth, so it would be a blow to go into Saturday’s game against West Ham without Alebiosu and Atcheson. Mowbray will have to work out a solution if either are ruled out.

Alebiosu has been the mainstay at right-back this season, but his absence would likely offer an opportunity for summer signing Gady Beyuku to impress. The 20-year-old has only made four substitute appearances to date, but he came on for 49 minutes against the Potters on Tuesday.

At centre-back, Hayden Carter partnered Sean McLoughlin with Atcheson out. The former has had injury problems of his own in recent times, so Mowbray may be hesitant to put him in for another full 90 minutes just a few days later.

This is an area where Mowbray has very little depth though. Scott Wharton was the only other central defender in the squad on Tuesday, and he has been on a long road back of his own.

It means Carter may start again if Atcheson is out, with Wharton called upon off the bench late on.

Of course, the hope will be that both Alebiosu and Atcheson are fit and ready to go against West Ham this weekend. However, if they aren’t deemed fit, the Blackburn Rovers defenders will have the international break to recover and rest up before the next club fixture.

After Saturday, the next league fixture sees Rovers head to Turf Moor to face rivals Burnley. Mowbray will be determined to have key men Alebiosu and Atcheson available for the fixture.

One potential worry is Atcheson’s call-up to the Northern Ireland squad for the upcoming international fixtures. If he isn’t deemed fit for Saturday, there will be questions over whether he should link up with Michael O’Neill‘s national side as planned.

Nigeria have not announced their squad for their AFCON qualifiers, but Alebiosu would be in a similar predicament if he is included in their ranks.

Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

Tether CEO Reacts to Massive USDT Demand in Paraguay

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino has responded to claims that the company's stablecoin, USDT, is being increasingly sought after in Paraguay following a significant banking crisis that cut off millions of people from their savings. Ardinoino has described this as "a flight to safety."

As noted in a recent X post by Bitcoin proponent Samson Mow, Tether's USDT has seen a significant rise in value relative to the Paraguayan guaraní amid the collapse of Ueno Bank, one of Paraguay's biggest digital lenders.

Ueno Bank, with an estimated 3 million customers (a significant percentage of adult Paraguayans), has been hit by a major financial crisis.

According to Mow, USDT has risen from 5,692 guaraníes to over 6,000 guaraníes, an increase of over 5%.

The surge in interest in the U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin USDT, Mow argues, is due to people trying to safeguard their savings outside of the traditional banking system.

USDT's exchange rate rose from about 5,692 guaraníes on Oct. 15 to over 6,000 on Nov. 4.

It is worth noting that even the traditional U.S. dollar rose against the guaraní over the same period.

The rising interest in USDT comes as Paraguay faces a major financial crisis over the collapse of Ueno Bank.

On Nov. 3, Paraguay's central bank announced the start of resolution proceedings for the financial institution after discovering that Ueno Bank's solvency ratio had dropped below half the minimum limit.

The move essentially shut down Ueno Bank, cutting off millions of customers' access to their savings and standard banking services for the time being.

The crisis has also cast doubt on public funds parked at Ueno Bank, including hundreds of millions of dollars from Paraguay's social security system.

The scandal has reportedly already led to the arrest of eight people connected to the bank as well as the resignation of central bank president Carlos Carvallo, as reported by Reuters.

Paraguay's central bank has been trying to reassure depositors, stressing that the situation pertains specifically to Ueno Bank and is not indicative of the collapse of the country's banking system.

Customers with frozen funds at Ueno Bank cannot necessarily convert their deposits to stablecoins until they are able to access their money again.

How to Address Common Causes of Knee Pain While Cycling

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Identify common reasons your knees may hurt while riding, and what to do about it.

As a cyclist, you probably know that you should always listen to your knees and the messages they send when they’re not so happy. These messages show up as aches and pains, and they’re common among those who ride. While research suggests cycling can actually help you avoid knee pain later in life, other studies indicate it still affects about 15 to 30 percent of cyclists.

Fortunately, the culprit is often an easy fix, but the first step is to identify what may be causing your knee pain. For example, you may be overtraining and pushing your body harder than it’s used to, straining your connective tissues and causing inflammation and pain.

Any spontaneous twinges of crank-stopping knee pain may feel like they come out of no where, when actually they’re just the first noticeable symptoms of a long-brewing problem. This generally stems from equipment and/or bike position issues, says bike-fit specialist Michael Veal of BikeDynamics in Warwickshire, England.

“Many cyclists are forever tinkering with their position, looking for that elusive perfect position,” he says. But what feels just right for a few pedal strokes around the block can end up feeling pretty wrong after 70 miles of roads. A good bike fit is essential for preventing most causes of knee pain.

For more guidance on what your knee pain means and strategies to avoid it, read on.

WANT MORE INFO ON BIKE FIT? CHECK OUT OUR EXCLUSIVE PROGRAM!

To help you figure out where to start, here are the most common types of cycling knee pain and the ways in which your bike fit may contribute to each symptom.

If your knee hurts right on the cap (or patella), it’s typically because of your quads. The four quadricep muscles attach to the shin via the patella, and when you’re putting force into your pedal stroke, these muscles deliver that force across the knee joint.

Your bike set-up can help with this problem. Determine whether adjusting your saddle height, saddle fore and aft, and/or crank length could alleviate the issue. Working with a professional bike fitter can be extremely helpful in addressing this type of knee pain.

“A saddle that is too low will keep your knee angle too tight at the top of the stroke, which increases the shear forces pulling the patella against the femur, which in turn increases the likelihood of tendonitis and harmful stresses in the cartilage behind the kneecap,” Veal says. “Likewise when the cranks are too long for your leg length, the knee joint is also too tight at the top of the stroke.”

How low is too low for a saddle? “For a quick reference to check if your saddle is the right height, have a seat and rest your heel on the pedal with the pedal in the 6 o’clock position,” says fit specialist and physical therapist Sara Bresnick, founder of Pedal Power Training Solutions in Medford, Massachusetts. “The knee of that leg should be straight. That equates to a 20- to 25-degree knee bend when clipped in.”

Riding with your saddle too far forward also can cause knee pain in the front, says Veal. “Your saddle should be positioned so the tibial tuberosity—or the bony bit below the kneecap—is directly above the ball of the foot, which in turn should be above the pedal spindle,” she says.

Also, be mindful of your form. Big-gear mashing, climbing in monster gears, and hard sprinting (especially if you’re not conditioned for it) can put undue stress on the knee and cause this type of pain.

If you do achieve a solid bike fit and avoid these common mistakes, and pain still persists, focus on stretching the quads to find relief.

Pain behind the knee is less common than pain in the front, and is generally easier to trace back to a culprit: over-extending the knee, says Bresnick. Your saddle is too high or too far back. “Try lowering the saddle a bit or moving it forward a bit in relation to the handlebars,” she says.

This pain is also more common among cyclists who spend a lot of time on fixed-gear bikes; When you ride fixed, you use your hamstrings to decelerate your pedal stroke, which can put the biceps femoris tendon (a hamstring muscle that runs down the back of your leg toward the outside of your knee) under too much load and irritate it. Giving your legs a break with some freewheeling now and then can also help.

If pain sticks around after making bike adjustments, try stretching the hamstrings regularly and avoid frequent out-of-saddle riding.

When you feel pain on the inner (or medial sides) of your knees, look down at your feet: Cleat placement is often a culprit, says Veal. Your cleat position affects your Q-factor, which dictates how far apart your feet are laterally when pedaling.

Ideally, the spacing should be that the loads from your knees to your pedals are traveling vertically without pushing the knee inward or outward, which stresses the ligaments on either side of your knee and can lead to pain.

Cleats positioned too close to the insides of your cycling shoes increase the distance between your feet, which can stress the inside collateral ligaments and cause pain in the inner (or medial) sides of your knees.

Too much float in your pedals also can cause medial knee pain. A little float—about 4.5 degrees—is all you need to be comfortable and keep your knees from getting stressed.

While a professional can help you adjust cleat positioning, you can also have someone take a slow-motion video you riding on a trainer, recording movement from the front of the bike. Look for asymmetry between your left and right side.

If you still feel pain on the inside of your knee after aligning your cleats, especially if it is a burning pain at rest or sharp pain when you start to walk or step up or down, you may have a type of bursitis. This pain typically improves with a warmup and stretching of the hip flexors and adductors (inner thighs).

Pain on the outer (or lateral) sides of the knees is often caused by iliotibial (IT) band syndrome—a stabbing pain that happens when the IT band (a fibrous connective tissue band that runs from the hip along the thigh to the tibia) becomes stressed and inflamed.

IT band issues can appear to come out of nowhere, and hurt most when the knee is at 20- to 30-degrees of flexion, like when you approach the 6 o’clock position and start to come back up.

“Misaligned cleats that cause the foot to be excessively toed in can cause this,” says Veal. So can cleats that are placed too far to the outsides of the shoes, causing too narrow of a stance on the bike.

If you think the cleats on your bike are misaligned, take your bike in for an adjustment. (A slow-mo video of you riding from the front can also help you see if your cleat positioning is to blame!)

Of course, what you do on your bike will also go a long way to promoting or alleviating knee pain. Here are some tips to assure it’s the latter.

Just as with most sports, a little warmup to get blood flowing and muscles primed is a good idea—so don’t skip it! As a general rule, aim for at least 15 minutes of moderate spinning before you attack the local climb or go hard on the flats.

Look at video of the pros in action, and you’ll notice their rapid and fluid pedal strokes. Try this drill to improve your pedal stroke: The next time you are going up your favorite climb, use a gear at least two teeth lower (thus easier) than usual. This higher cadence is easier on your joints. Think about making round circles as you pedal, not ovals.

Your first ride back after any extended amount of time off shouldn’t be five hours long. Instead, start with a lower mileage and build slowly.

Your body likes consistency, so when you alter equipment, your knees often protest. Whether you get a new bike, new shoes, new pedals, or change stem length, go easy at first to give yourself time to adapt.

Riding in 40-degree weather with red, chapped knees is a recipe for trouble. Pros routinely cover their legs in training when the temperature is below 60 degrees—that’s why knee warmers exist!

While each individual’s tolerance will differ, there’s certainly no harm in keeping muscles and joints warm. By using warmers, you can easily slip them off mid-ride if you start to warm up.

Take note of what gear works best in certain weather conditions, so you can repeat on future rides.

If knee pain persists, consider getting a professional bike fit. And no, we’re not talking about when a salesperson sets your seat height at purchase.

A professional bike fit should be an in-depth experience with a professionally trained fitter that includes an interview process, off-bike flexibility testing, and on-bike measurements and adjustments. Yes, it’s an investment (usually at least $200), but it’s worth it to ride pain-free. It could end up being the best money you ever spent on your cycling habit.

If you’re still experiencing cycling knee pain after doing everything mentioned above, including a professional bike fit, it may be caused by muscle imbalances instead—like weak glutes, hips, or core. It’s best to consult with a sports medicine physician and/or physical therapist to find the right course of treatment for you.

John Vasudevan, M.D. is an associate professor at the University of Pennsylvania. He is board-certified in Physical Medicine & Rehabilitation and Sports Medicine. He is a Team Physician for UPenn Athletics and  medical director of the Broad Street Run and Philadelphia Distance Run, and previously for the Rock 'n' Roll Half-Marathon and Tri-Rock Triathlon in Philadelphia. He is a director of the running and endurance Sports Medicine Program at Penn Medicine.  Dr. Vasudevan provides non-operative management of musculoskeletal conditions affecting athletes and active individuals of all levels, and combines injury rehabilitation with injury prevention. He utilizes a variety of ultrasound-guided procedures and regenerative approaches such as platelet-rich plasma and percutaneous ultrasonic tenotomy. He sees patients at the Penn Medicine and the Philadelphia Veterans Administration hospital. Dr. Vasudevan attended medical school at the University of Wisconsin School of Medicine and Public Health in Madison. After his Transitional Year in Tucson, Arizona, he went to residency in PM&R at Thomas Jefferson University in Philadelphia and onwards to Stanford University for his fellowship in Sports Medicine. He has been in practice at the University of Pennsylvania since 2012.

Cristiano Ronaldo’s 143 international goals: Could any of these five players surpass him?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Cristiano Ronaldo has announced his international retirement and set a very high bar for the top goalscorer of all time.

After 234 caps, Ronaldo confirmed that he was retiring from the Portuguese national team and does so having scored 146 goals for the team in a 23-year stint.

That tally puts him top of the all-time scorers for their national team with the also-retired Lionel Messi next best with 20 fewer goals.

Ali Daei of Iran makes up the top three but are there any players who could beat Ronaldo’s record?

The current highest-ranked active player is Lukaku who has scored 94 goals for Belgium.

His age, however, makes it very difficult for him to reach Ronaldo’s tally. Lukaku is 33 now and scores at a rate of 0.7 per match, that means he needs another 76 matches to score 147 goals.

He plays between 5-10 international games per year and so would need to play for another seven years or so which would take him to the age Ronaldo is now.

It’s not impossible but is unlikely you would figure.

If you’re looking for a 33-year-old who could break the record then ignore Lukaku and instead focus on Kane.

The reason why Kane is a more tempting bet is he is in ridiculous goal scoring form, the kind of form that wins you the Ballon d’Or.

Kane’s got 91 in 125 England games, meaning he’s at 0.73 per match, his numbers though have really ramped up in the last five seasons.

He got 16 in 2021, including four in the delayed Euro 2020, and 13 in 2026 with more games yet to come.

If he continues at the rate of his career so far, he would need 77 more matches but he has a one-in-one record for 2026. Continue at that pace and he would reach the tally a lot quicker.

If you were a betting man, you would probably put your money on Mbappe to be the one to reach it.

He’s got a lot of good things going for him. First of all, he’s the main goalscoring output for a nation that regularly reaches major tournament finals. Secondly, he’s incredibly good in those tournaments and has won the Golden Boot in the last two World Cups. Thirdly, he’s only 27.

Mbappe’s goal-scoring rate overall is a little lower than Kane or Lukaku’s but he is making up for any lost time. The Real Madrid forward has got 12 in 13 2026 games. In 2022 and 2023, he broke double digits and since moving centrally, he has only scored more for his club side.

Mbappe has got 67 goals from his 107 games, putting him 78 behind Ronaldo but you would fancy him to get close at the least by the time his career is over.

READ: Will Cristiano Ronaldo get a Portugal farewell match? What we know after retirement announcement

Haaland’s goalscoring ratio makes him a strong contender.

The Norwegian machine has a better-than-a-goal-a-game ratio for his country and, while meaning no disrespect to Norway, he is doing it in not one of the elite nations.

Haaland has got 65 in 59 including five in seven at the most recent World Cup and has age on his side as well with him being 26.

Where Haaland may fall down is that he does not play for one of the top nations and so reaching tournaments and then going deep in them is not a given.

The 2026 World Cup was the first time Norway have appeared in it in 28 years and while you would say they are one of the 48 best teams in the world right now, that could fluctuate more than other nations like France, Germany and Spain.

He’s got a long way to go, but he certainly has the time to get there.

Yamal has 11 goals for Spain so far, putting him a whopping 135 goals behind Ronaldo but Yamal is only 19, meaning he has another 22 years in the game if he were to play to the age of Ronaldo.

Although one of the most impressive attributes about both Ronaldo and Messi is their longevity. Yamal would not be the first young superstar to be off the top perch by the time he was 30.

One other negative for Yamal is he is not an out-and-out goalscorer in the same way Ronaldo in particular was. Yamal got just one goal and four assists in Euro 2024 while he failed to really shine in the 2026 World Cup despite Spain winning it.

If he begins to score more goals, especially for the national team, and if he stays healthy, he could do it but those are two big ifs.

Yellowing leaves this month are usually caused by the #1 seasonal mistake houseplant parents make. Here’s how to save your plant's life before it’s too late.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If your houseplant leaves are turning yellow this November, your plant is sending urgent distress signals that you really need to heed. As days shorten and sunlight becomes less intense in fall, most houseplants use far less moisture as their growth dramatically slows. Continuing your summer watering routine during this shift creates waterlogged soil, quickly leading to root rot – and an avoidable trip to the compost bin. Catch that first yellow leaf early, however, and you can quickly reverse the damage to keep your houseplant happy and healthy.

Failing to adjust a watering routine in line with houseplants’ seasonal needs is the most common mistake we plant parents make, and unfortunately the root rot that results can be fatal. It takes a few weeks of waterlogged soil and rotting roots before the damage manifests with yellowing leaves, which is why this symptom usually shows up in November – and why once you spot a yellowing leaf you need to take swift action.

So, great work getting here to Garden ER where you'll find straightforward solutions to treat this season’s biggest planty problems. Come on in and let’s diagnose and treat your houseplant with a yellowing leaf, and save it from an untimely death with a simple fix.

Take a closer look at the yellow houseplant leaf to see what tone, shape, position and extent of the discoloration is. Different plants show this early symptom of root rot in various ways: a Monstera will likely get a yellow halo on one of its monster-sized leaves while a ZZ plant leaf will turn bright yellow from top to bottom overnight. Peperomia foliage typically fades to a pale yellow while a snake plant leaf takes on a translucent yellow tone at the base. Matching up the symptoms to the plant species is a surefire way to tell if overwatering is the cause.

Match your houseplant to the symptoms and you have your diagnosis.

Large, thin-leafed tropical plants like monstera, calathea, anthurium and clumping philodendron typically show a bright yellow halo around a dark spot from overwatering. This happens because as roots begin to rot in soggy soil, fungal or bacterial pathogens in the potting mix travel up the plant and into the leaf tissue. Dead leaf cells show up as a black or brown area, and the bright ring of yellow around it is where the plant is attempting to stop the infection spreading.

ZZ plants (Zamioculcas zamiifolia), caladiums, and elephant ears (Alocasia and Colocasia) grow from potato-like rhizomes or tubers that store lots of water, so it can take these plants a little longer to show signs of overwatering. When they do, it's dramatic though! If these houseplants receive more water than they can process and rot sets in, nutrients are suddenly cut to a whole stem. The entire stalk and its foliage will quickly drain of color to turn a solid strong yellow, top to bottom.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Prayer plants (Maranta), peperomia, Chinese money plants (Pilea peperomioides), African violets (Saintpaulia), nerve plants (Fittonia) and Boston and maidenhair ferns all have a shallow network of very fine, fragile roots. Suffocating in overwatered soil, these thin roots quickly disintegrate and the leaves lose their lustre then become limp and fade through pale green to a matte yellow. Don’t expect these leaves to be mushy from all that water: because the root damage cuts the water supply off, leaves will become dry and brittle, snapping off immediately when touched or simply dropping from the plant.

Jade plants (Crassula ovata), snake plants, Christmas or Thanksgiving cacti (Schlumbergera), echeverias and aloe vera all store water in their stems or leaves, and excess moisture causes them to turn a translucent, sickly yellow at the base. The area will also feel mushy and this may happen where stems or leaves meet the soil, in which case stems may topple over, but also where leaves attach to stems or leaf segments join together.

Vining plants like pothos (Epipremnum aureum), English ivy (Hedera helix), wax plants (Hoya) and heartleaf philodendron (Philodendron hederaceum) typically show overwatering symptoms on their oldest leaves near the base first. Unable to draw as many nutrients through suffocating roots, the plant strips nutrients from its older, less productive leaves to feed the fresh growth at its tips in the hope of finding a more suitable environment. Stripped of chlorophyll, the base foliage turns an even, uniform yellow while the newer growth at the stem tip remains green.

Overwatered houseplants with long, narrow leaves like spider plants (Chlorophytum comosum) and ponytail palms (Beaucarnea recurvata) typically develop brown foliage tips with a yellow area separating them from the healthy green tissue. This happens because the leaf tips are at the very end of the plant's plumbing system so, when roots are damaged and can't take up water effectively, are the first to run out of moisture. The yellow color below the dead brown leaf tissue is the plant trying to salvage what nutrients it can. The leaves may lose their upright arch and start to droop, too.

If you spot yellowing houseplant leaves, immediately stop watering and let the soil dry out. Slide the pot off and if you see white roots, you’ve caught the problem in time. Pop the plant back into its pot and only water when the top inch of potting mix feels dry. A moisture meter like this from Amazon takes out the guesswork and helps you adjust your routine to the plant's fluctuating needs going forwards, and water houseplants properly.

If there’s a foul, stagnant smell, however faint, and roots look dark, root rot has set in and you’ll need to take action to save your plant. Remove as much soil as possible and use sharp scissors or pruners or to snip off all roots that look dark or feel mushy or slimy. Rinse the roots and wash the pot, then repot your houseplant.

It's vital the pot has a drainage hole so excess moisture can escape (and a saucer to save your surfaces). If you adore a decorative hole-less pot, use it as a cachepot with a holed plastic pot inside. For succulent plants that store water in their leaves or stems, fill your pot with a fast-draining potting mix like Back to the Roots Organic Succulents & Cacti Mix, available from The Home Depot. For everything else, use an indoor potting mix with coconut coir and perlite that evens out moisture levels while allowing good drainage like Miracle-Gro Indoor Potting Mix For All Plants, available from Walmart.

Upgrade your irrigation with a watering can that has a long, gooseneck spout like this indoor can from Walmart for more precise, controlled water delivery.

Every week, the Garden ER series brings you simple solutions to this season's most pressing problems, for happy and healthy plants without any hassle.

Work out what's behind your ailing spider-plant developing brown tips in fall and nurse your houseplant back to health.

Take these proven-to-work steps to make your yard inhospitable to snakes in fall and reduce numbers next year, too.

End rot is a common challenge for winter squash in fall but identify the cause and it's simple to rescue the remaining crop.

A lost leaf or two doesn’t spell disaster for a Christmas cactus in fall but you can't afford to ignore this distress signal.

Chafer grubs have devastated drought-stressed lawns this year but are easily treated if catch this crucial fall window.

Work out what your peace lily needs to recover if spathes develop brown patches or look dry and crispy around the edges.

« Un vide impossible à combler » : La famille de Jesús de Miguel sort du silence après le drame

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ce jeudi 5 novembre 2026, la famille de Jesús de Miguel est sortie du silence après la disparition tragique de l'attaquant du CD Tenerife. Devant les médias, son représentant Manuel Hernández a relayé un message poignant de ses proches.

L'attaquant espagnol, âgé de 30 ans, est décédé le mardi 3 novembre 2026 des suites d'un violent choc au thorax survenu en plein match de deuxième division face au FC Andorra. Selon le rapport d'autopsie préliminaire transmis aux autorités judiciaires, le décès est consécutif à un traumatisme thoracique accidentel intervenu lors d'une sortie du gardien adverse Jesús Owono, exclu sur l'action. Une onde de choc pour le football espagnol.

Rassemblés dans la province de Tolède pour affronter cette perte soudaine, les proches du joueur ont tenu à saluer les nombreuses marques d'affection reçues depuis quarante-huit heures. Devant les médias, Manuel Hernández a souligné l'immense gratitude de la famille De Miguel Alameda envers les coéquipiers de l'attaquant, ses amis, les supporters ainsi que les clubs du monde entier qui ont multiplié les hommages. Une solidarité précieuse dans l'épreuve.

Au-delà de ces remerciements, les proches de l'attaquant ont formulé un appel clair au respect de leur douleur. La famille a en effet insisté sur la nécessité de préserver son intimité afin de pouvoir traverser cette terrible épreuve à l'écart de l'agitation médiatique.

Le recueillement s'organise désormais au sein du village natal de Navahermosa. La messe de funérailles de Jesús de Miguel a ainsi été fixée ce jeudi 5 novembre 2026 à 15h00 en l'église paroissiale San Miguel Arcángel. Un dernier hommage solennel pour accompagner le joueur entouré des siens.

La disparition tragique de l'attaquant a suscité une vive émotion jusqu'au sommet du football espagnol. Avant le transfert du corps vers la péninsule, plusieurs personnalités sont venues se recueillir à la chambre funéraire Santa Lastenia à Tenerife. Le président du Real Madrid Florentino Pérez et le directeur des relations institutionnelles Emilio Butragueño ont notamment tenu à faire le déplacement pour manifester leur soutien aux proches et au club insulaire.

Sur le plan sportif, les compétitions passent logiquement au second plan. D'après la presse espagnole, le match de la 13e journée de Liga Hypermotion entre le Granada CF et le CD Tenerife, initialement programmé le samedi 7 novembre 2026 au stade Nuevo Los Cármenes, a été reporté à une date ultérieure. À en croire ces mêmes sources, le CD Tenerife aurait également décrété six jours d'arrêt total de ses activités et prévoirait d'ouvrir les portes du stade Heliodoro Rodríguez López ce samedi pour un hommage populaire.

The 7 Best Men’s Base Layers of 2027

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

What to know: Our team of expert testers wore 46 bottoms and tops made of natural and synthetic materials for skiing, running, and other winter activities. Their months of evaluation resulted in naming the Stellar Merino Base Top and Tights as the best overall base layer for men for its comfort and moisture management. Most versatile is the Flylow Hanmer Wool Baselayer Top for under $100.

The base layer (aka long underwear or thermals) is both the literal and metaphorical foundation of a proper layering system. A wrong choice makes the whole system fall apart. If you pick a layer that chafes or gets sweaty, you’re going to be miserable no matter how perfect your insulation or shell performs.

Even after testing them for over a decade, however, I’m still hesitant to crown a few as the best because base layers are highly personal. Someone skinning uphill in the damp Pacific Northwest needs something very different from a resort skier sitting on frozen chairlifts in Colorado. Your ideal fabric feel, insulating weight, shoulder fit, and myriad other variables may vary greatly from mine.

This year’s rough snow season in the western U.S. only reinforced how important versatility is in our gear. Due to inconsistent winter conditions, we found ourselves wearing these base layers not only while skiing and touring, but also during mountain bike rides, cold morning runs, gym workouts, and shoulder-season adventures where moisture management and mobility mattered just as much as warmth. After months of testing 46 bottoms and tops—because it is our job, after all—these are the best base layers that we believe will appeal to a variety of preferences.

Pros and Cons + Most comfortable wool layer in test + Best-in-class moisture management + Remarkable odor mitigation – No hood

Out of the 46 base layers we tested, the strongest pool we have ever drawn from, the Stellar Merino Base Top and Tights were the best base layering set in the widest variety of conditions. From 15-degree full-day ski tours in Colorado’s San Juan backcountry to 45-degree high-output mountain bike rides in Durango, the layers scored best in comfort, moisture management, fit, and odor mitigation. One tester put it simply: “Clear best in test.”

Sourced from Uruguay and produced in a family-owned factory in Portugal, the Stellar kit uses premium fine 19.5-micron merino wool fibers, which deliver both softness and stretch. The fit earned praise from our tester, who is 5’10” and 170 pounds, and extremely picky about how his base layers hug his body while he moves in the snow. “Both the top and bottoms were snug but never bunchy, with an ideal sleeve and leg length for unhindered movement,” he said.

Each layer features flatlock seams to reduce bulk, gusseted underarms for full range of motion, and an elongated back panel to block cold drafts. It moved moisture efficiently and transitioned from sweaty to completely dry in under 30 minutes. After a season’s worth of wear, odor buildup was minimal. “Still haven’t washed it!” said our tester, who cohabitates with his fiancée and hasn’t heard her complain. The only knock? No hood, which would’ve been nice to help the top transition to a standalone spring skiing piece.

Sizes: S-XXL Key Material: 100 percent recycled polyester

Pros and Cons + Silky smooth + Friction-free seams + Excellent mobility across multiple sports – Too lightweight for chronically cold skiers

Built with The North Face’s lightweight double-knit DOTKNIT polyester fabric, this shirt combines engineered holes in the face fabric and targeted yarn construction with a wider knit in high-heat areas like armpits. The marriage accelerates moisture transfer during movement, which testers appreciated during sweaty mountain bike rides, icy bump skiing in 45-degree weather, and early-morning runs in Ashland and Bend, Oregon. Despite weighing just 6.67 ounces, the double knit still delivered enough warmth for winter weather while drying quickly and remaining exceptionally breathable. No clamminess here.

Its standout feature was the construction. The low-profile merrow stitching (an overlock stitch that wraps the edges of fabric panels as it joins them) and slim athletic fit made the base layer almost completely disappear during repetitive movements like pedaling, skinning, and gym workouts. Thumb loops also prevented sleeves from bunching underneath outer layers during cold ski days and made transitions between activities seamless. While it kept us warm while we were moving, this is definitely not the thickest or toastiest layer in the test. Skiers who routinely run cold on frozen chairlifts will likely want a heavier option (read on for our recommendation).

Sizes: XS-3XL Key Material: 100 percent recycled polyester

Pros and Cons + Extremely breathable + Casual enough to wear anywhere – Minimal insulation in truly cold weather – Must be coupled with outer layer in wind

Patagonia has been making Capilene layers for over 40 years, and the new Capilene Lightweight Crew is proof that this signature fabric just keeps getting better. Made of 100 percent double-knit, recycled polyester, with HeiQ Mint odor control and a wicking finish, it weighs a scant 3.3 ounces. Those technical details translated directly into real-world performance during mountain bike rides, gym workouts, and early-morning runs where the layer consistently dumped heat and dried almost immediately. The odor mitigation worked so well that this top once went three full weeks of heavy use between trips to the washing machine.

Unlike heavier synthetic layers that can start to feel swampy once soaked, this one retained a dry, barely-there sensation even after sweaty intervals and long climbs. The fitted cut also helped the top transition comfortably between sports and life, and the stripes made it look more like a casual long-sleeve tee than a dedicated technical layer. We ended up wearing it so often that it became part mountain-bike layer, part workout shirt, and part accidental Zoom call uniform. Disclaimer: this is not a deep winter insulation monster. On truly cold days, it worked best underneath a shell and insulating layers, or alone only during high-output activities.

Sizes: S-XXL Key Material: 100 percent merino wool

Pros and Cons + Soft and comfortable + Fantastic warmth-to-breathability balance + Excellent mobility despite heavyweight feel – Too warm for spring touring

The Woolx Backcountry Leggings delivered some of the best next-to-skin comfort and thermoregulation this season. Built with heavyweight merino wool, they struck an impressive balance between warmth, stretch, and breathability that made them equally enjoyable during cold ski mornings, frigid dawn-patrol jogs, and recovery hangs at home.

Despite their thickness, the leggings moved exceptionally well during mountain bike rides and gym workouts. The fabric stretched naturally through deep squats, pedaling, and mobility work without ever feeling restrictive or bunchy under outer layers. While they didn’t dry as quickly as lightweight synthetics, they retained warmth even when damp. The fabric also resisted odor buildup during repeated workouts and back-to-back days of use—another advantage of merino wool in multisport layering systems. We’d recommend reserving these for the most frigid temps: during sunny spring ski days and warmer trail rides, testers overheated in them.

KUIU’s most advanced fleece to date, the Vital S3 Zip-T Hoodie is the base layer that can do it all. The soft-brushed fleece interior traps heat, wicks moisture, dries quickly, resists odors, and still gives you plenty of mobility on high-output cold weather hunts. Boosted by XLANCE®️ technology, the fleece is reinforced with an advanced polyolefin-based stretch yarn with built-in resistance to sweat, chemicals, heat, ozone, and UVA/UVB radiation. This means your hoodie retains elasticity and durability even across the most grueling treks—you’ll get the same fit and performance when you reach for it season after season.

Sizes: S-XXL Key Material: 100 percent merino wool

Pros and Cons + Remarkably breathable for its weight + Soft next-to-skin feel + Immediate warmth during cold starts and transitions – Too warm for sustained high-output efforts – Slightly bulkier under trim shells

The Ridge Merino Inversion Crew managed one of the hardest balancing acts: delivering substantial warmth without feeling stifling the second testers started moving. Made of 100 percent merino wool, it became a favorite during freezing dawn starts, snowy driveway shoveling, and post-ride recovery hangs. Superfine 18.5-micron fibers were impressively soft against the skin for such a warm piece—one tester wore it to sleep even after sweating in it during the day. Low-profile flatlock seams, raglan sleeves, thumb loops, and an extra-long cut minimized friction points underneath packs and outerwear, making it all the more cozy.

What surprised us most was how flexible and breathable the top remained despite its warmth. Heavyweight base layers often feel amazing when you’re standing still and miserable once your heart rate spikes, but the Inversion regulated temperature better than expected during moderate-output movement. This is still, however, fundamentally a cold-weather specialist. During hard uphill efforts and warmer spring conditions, it quickly became too much of a layer. It also felt a tad bulky when coupled with a very trim Rab shell—worth noting if you like tighter outer layers.

Sizes: S-XXL Key Material: Polyester/wool blend

Pros and Cons + Packs down to the size of a tangerine + Fast drying + Silky next to skin – Doesn’t work as a standalone layer in cold

Over ten days of skiing in the Japanese Alps, spinning lifts at Mount Ashland, and ski touring at Crater Lake, the Rab Syncrino Base LS Tee became one tester’s default when he knew he was going to get hot and sweat hard. Built with Rab’s lightweight Syncrino 125 fabric, it combines merino wool for insulation and moisture-wicking with polyester for strength, durability, and fast drying. We found this triad of attributes ideal for high-output touring, hut trips, and traveling in temps 40 degrees and below.

It packed down to the size of a tangerine, which one tester squeezed into his pack as a safety layer on hut trips and a two-week trip to Nozawa, Japan. He praised the next-to-skin comfort, saying it “felt like you didn’t even have it on,” and rated its mobility and sweat management highly. The single-jersey construction, low-bulk seams, drop tail, and set-in sleeves made it especially comfortable under other layers.

Even after sweaty skimo sessions and back-to-back cold days, it stayed fresh. “No matter how much I sweat, it [shone] in many environments,” our tester said. However, it was too lightweight to be a standalone layer during the winter months.

Sizes: S-XL Key Material: Merino wool/nylon blend

Pros and Cons + Wide temperature range + Highly breathable + Minimal friction during exercise – Fit is long for shorter users

The Flylow Hanmer Wool Baselayer Top was winter MVP for one tester after five big days of testing on his local ski hill, Mount Ashland. He not only appreciated the Hanmer’s incredible versatility in the mountains but also felt like he was wearing a normal, non-technical top around town thanks to its two-toned sleeves and casual style. “You could literally wear this every day in winter,” he said. Tested from zero to 40 degrees while skiing, running, and during three full-on nighttime skimo training sessions, the Hanmer consistently stood out for its ability to keep him warm and efficiently move his sweat at the same time—two attributes that often conflict with each other. “I would finish a skimo workout and rip off all of my other sweaty layers and keep the Hanmer top on and throw on my down jacket over it. It was amazing that it wasn’t soggy,” he said.

The Hanmer is woven from a blend of merino wool and nylon that Flylow claims is 50 percent stronger than comparable merino fabrics. While we weren’t able to confirm that number, we can confirm that it did not experience pilling after a full winter’s worth of testing. It proved warm, breathable, sweat-wicking, and also impressively odor-resistant even after days of heavy use. “It was noticeable how odorless it was; most of my other layers would carry a little stink after the same amount of use,” he said. Flatlock seams eliminate chafing, while a long cut makes layering easy. Thumb loops and a backpack-friendly raglan construction enhance usability during high-output movement. “The thumb holes are ingenious,” our tester said. With cozy skin feel and all-conditions reliability, the Hanmer is a layer that disappears in a good way.

Everybody is different, so dialing in the materials that feel the best against your skin is the most important part of the base-layer buying journey. In general, the best base layers are constructed of either polyester or merino wool, with spandex, elastane, or nylon spun into the fabric to increase stretch or durability. Polyester layers are usually cheaper and quicker to dry, while merino is more expensive, dries slower (but retains heat while wet), and is more odor-resistant.

Which is better comes down to personal preference: some love the soft hand of natural fibers, but even the most advanced, small-micron merino wool material can make someone with a wool intolerance miserable. If you can, try on any layer you want to buy. How it feels against your skin is paramount and differs per person. If something feels itchy in a dressing room, run from it, as it is only going to get more uncomfortable with prolonged use.

Once you land on a material that works for your skin, start looking into moisture management. If you sweat a ton, as many of the testers for this category do, look into lightweight synthetic base layers, as they have an excellent track record for moving moisture efficiently. If you don’t sweat much, then you can get away with thicker next-to-skin layers that will also be warmer when you’re not in motion.

In terms of design, base layers are pretty straightforward. To maximize comfort, look for upgrades like flatlock seams and raglan sleeves. These can prevent chafing, especially when wearing a pack.

When deciding what to avoid, we would suggest starting by looking at the label. Staying away from certain materials is a great way to minimize your chances of getting a bum base layer. Cotton is an obvious material to avoid for winter-specific base layers because it holds onto moisture and can become dangerously cold. Synthetics without some type of odor-mitigating technology might also not be your best bet for hut trips, winter backpacking, or high-output skinning because they can become unsavably stinky in one trip. If something is wool, check the micron number. A lower micron count (finer fibers) will be softer and more comfortable next to skin than a higher one.

We also suggest looking a little deeper at the wording of wool/synthetic blends. Purpose-built blends (which intentionally place wool and synthetics in different parts of the blend) can utilize the hydrophilic and hydrophobic natures of the fabrics to its advantage. An example of this is a material that has a hydrophilic synthetic next to your skin to grab moisture with a layer of a hydrophobic wool that will move that moisture away from you. If the blend is described as “intimate,” that means it weaves them together in a way that will deliver the worst of both worlds. An intimate blend creates a tug-of-war over that moisture that can leave you super clammy, so we recommend steering clear.

Due to the abysmal snowpack in the Pacific Northwest, category manager Joe Jackson had the most varied testing conditions for base layers he has experienced in more than a decade of testing the category. In addition to the ski days he eked out at Mount Bachelor and in the backcountry, he used these layers during more than 60 mountain bike rides, early-morning runs, and high-output workouts in his home gym. That broad mix of activities allowed him to evaluate not only warmth and next-to-skin comfort, but also mobility, moisture management, odor mitigation, and versatility transitioning between sports.

Skiing, however, remained a critical part of testing, as always. Testers used these layers during resort skiing, uphill touring, cold transitions in parking lots, and long days spent sweating under packs. We paid close attention to how quickly layers dried after high-output efforts, whether fabrics became clammy during sustained sweating, and how well the materials retained warmth after becoming damp. We also stress-tested mobility across all sports because the best modern base layers should disappear while you move instead of restricting you.

But this year’s low snow totals also created a more realistic picture for how today’s base layers are actually used by many outdoor athletes, as multisport layering pieces that need to perform just as well on trail runs and shoulder-season bike rides as they do under a ski shell.

Category manager Joe Jackson has been testing gear professionally as his main gig for over a decade. While he has tested everything from whitewater kayaks to sporks, base layers remain one of his all-time favorite categories. They can be absorbed into everyday life to get the type of testing reps needed to help tease out the nuance that makes a piece of gear truly special. They also don’t take up much space in his overburdened garage.

If you suffer from FOMO, do not follow Southern Oregon-based tester Yu Kuwabara on Strava—his output is absolutely bananas. He’s the kind of athlete who will climb and ski Mount Shasta (he has 37 summits) in the morning, then teaches a mountain biking clinic that afternoon on a casual day. The local race director and father of rippers logged hundreds upon hundreds of multisport miles and tens of thousands of vertical feet around Southern Oregon, as well as on a trip to Japan, to test the dickens out of these layers.

Durango, Colorado–based tester Ryan Pyles has enough energy to power a small city. He positively channeled said energy this winter with remote packrafting trips, dozens of days skiing in the backcountry around Durango and Red Mountain Pass, mountain biking, trail running, and skiing in his local resort of Purgatory. He worked as a science teacher while testing last winter and has also worked as an engineer. The two jobs arguably make him the perfect candidate to deliver solid feedback because of his ability to really get in the weeds with materials as well as distill that into understandable information.
